      minhash: PQy37YpMedJ+aqhWW5bk2g2PVqKs2kSbqTbYzt/TwWeqFhGXzwLnP/n6gKZsIhDjiST8utS0fTXxbqgJEzi/4rNHBFsj55HMwNf5G4+7FhL8/ayBHl8s1KfGPDpbUzr5+XsfqAHzpc4nFPMMH/axTVv645Kt2HJEyh75wDhOSxmhYi27i80qlYbX3gr4lvNMt+dso3kwRQAbozZZEuidZJoL57bJ3kuTjSG0S6lC7JDLh+dLTJdFR34F1wT5u4nPMEGZmoUyUjCE6BkRT2ocR+gyFXUwoOUWmTT5Z8yCizZGW+ragzSV9rrU9BkZwMWJDM4t95aCXj2hCtexLVc24A==

<!-- theorem-1: body_md -->
There is a universal constant $\overline{\mathrm{opt}} \in (0,1)$ such that the following holds. Suppose $\rho$ is an arbitrary $n$-mode bosonic state, and assume that $\mathrm{opt}\leq {\overline{\mathrm{opt}}}$, where $\mathrm{opt}$ is defined in Eq.~\eqref{eq:optdef}. Let $E$ be the energy of some closest state in $\mathcal G$. Then, there exists an algorithm which takes an $\epsilon > 0$ along with $\mathrm{poly}(n,\epsilon^{-1},\log \log E)$ copies and, using only Gaussian unitaries, heterodyne measurements, and photon counting, outputs a $\ket*{\hat \psi}\in \mathcal G$ satisfying
     $$d_{\rm IF}(\rho,\ket*{\hat \psi})\leq \mathrm{opt} +\epsilon $$
     with probability at least $0.99$.
<!-- end theorem-1 -->
<!-- theorem-2: body_md -->
\label{thm:lowfidelity_informal}
    Suppose $\rho$ is an arbitrary $n$-mode bosonic state. Let $E$ be the energy of some closest state in $\mathcal G$. Then, there exists an algorithm which takes an $\epsilon > 0$ along with $\mathrm{poly}(n^{1/\epsilon},\epsilon^{-1},\log \log E)$ copies and, using only Gaussian unitaries, heterodyne measurements, and photon counting, outputs a $\ket*{\hat \psi}\in \mathcal G$ satisfying
     $$d_{\rm IF}(\rho,\ket*{\hat \psi})\leq \mathrm{opt} +\epsilon$$
     with probability at least $0.99$.
<!-- end theorem-2 -->
<!-- theorem-3: body_md -->
Agnostic tomography of pure Gaussian states is at least as hard as finding maximal cliques in graphs. Thus, unless $\mathrm{NP}\subseteq\mathrm{BQP}$, there is no algorithm for agnostic tomography of pure Gaussian states which achieves runtime $\mathrm{poly}(n,1/\epsilon)$.
<!-- end theorem-3 -->
<!-- theorem-4: body_md -->
In the high-fidelity regime, there is a time-efficient protocol for agnostic tomography of pure Gaussian states which only uses Gaussian measurements and achieves infidelity $O(\mathrm{opt}) + \epsilon$ using $\tilde{O}(n^2/\epsilon)$ copies. However, there exist settings of $\mathrm{opt}$ such that any protocol in this regime that only uses Gaussian measurements cannot achieve infidelity $\mathrm{opt} + \epsilon$ without using $\exp(\Omega(n^{1/3}))$ copies.
<!-- end theorem-4 -->
<!-- fact-1: body_md -->
\label{fact:trd-to-param}
    Let $\pi_1({\bf m}_1,V_1)$ and $\pi_2({\bf m}_2,V_2)$ be two bosonic Gaussian states. Then, 
    \begin{equation}
        d_{\rm tr}(\pi_1,\pi_2)\lesssim \|V_1^{-1/2}({\bf m}_1-{\bf m}_2)\|_2+\tr((V_1^{-1}+V_2^{-1})|V_1-V_2|) \; .
    \end{equation}
<!-- end fact-1 -->
<!-- fact-2: body_md -->
\label{fact:energy-covariance}
Let $\rho$ be an $n$-mode bosonic state with covariance matrix $V$ and finite mean photon number $\nu=\nu(\rho)$. Then
\[
    \|V\|_{\rm op}\leq 1+2\nu+2\sqrt{\nu(\nu+1)}\leq4\nu+2.
\]
In particular, for a pure Gaussian state $\pi$ with covariance $V$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lfpr-energy-condition}
    \|V^{-1}\|_{\rm op}=\|V\|_{\rm op}\leq4\nu(\pi)+2.
\end{equation}
<!-- end fact-2 -->
<!-- definition-1: body_md -->
\label{def:bf-space}
    Define the following inner product for entire complex functions in $n$ variables:
    \begin{align}
       \langle f,g\rangle _{\mathcal B_n}  =\pi^{-n}\int_{\mathbb{C}^n }\bar f (z)g(z)e^{-\|z\|^2}dz
    \end{align}
    Then, $\mathcal{B}_n \equiv \{f:\mathbb{C}^n \to \mathbb{C}: \|f\|_{\mathcal B_n}<\infty\}$ is a Hilbert space known as the Bargmann-Fock space. In particular, $\mathcal{B}_n $ can be identified with the $n$-mode bosonic Fock space $\mathcal F_n $ via the mappings:
    \begin{align}
        \ket{k_1k_2\dots k_n}\leftrightarrow\frac{z_1^{k_1}z_2^{k_2}\cdots z_n^{k_n}}{\sqrt{k_1!k_2!\cdots k_n!}},\quad \hat{a}_i^\dagger \leftrightarrow z_i,\quad \hat{a}_i\leftrightarrow \partial_{z_i} 
    \end{align}
<!-- end definition-1 -->
<!-- fact-3: body_md -->
Every pure Gaussian state $\ket{\psi}$ can be uniquely written as
\begin{equation}
f_\psi(z)=\frac 1 {\sqrt{Z(b,A)}}\exp(b^\top z +\frac 1 2 z^\top Az)
\end{equation}
with $\theta=(b,A)\in \mathcal{P}_n=\{(b,A):b\in \mathbb{C}^n,A\in \mathbb{C}^{n\times n},A=A^\top\}$ and $\|A\|_{\rm op}<1$. The global phase is typically fixed so that the vacuum coefficient is positive. 
In Fock space, these coefficients parameterize the state as follows:
\begin{align}\label{eq:barg-state}
        \ket{\psi(b,A)}&=\frac{1}{\sqrt{Z(b,A)}}\underbrace{\exp\bigg(\overbrace{\sum_{i=1}^n b_i \hat{a}_i^\dagger}^{L_b} +\overbrace{\frac 1 2 \sum_{i,j=1}^n A_{ij}\hat{a}_i^\dagger \hat{a}_j^\dagger}^{Q_A}\bigg)\ket{0}}_{\ket{\Phi(b,A)}}\\
        \label{eq:barg-state-expand}
        &=\frac{1}{\sqrt{Z(b,A)}}\bigg(1+\sum_{i=1}^n b_i \hat{a}_i^\dagger+\frac 1 2 \sum_{i,j=1}^n (A_{ij}+b_ib_j)\hat{a}_i^\dagger \hat{a}_j^\dagger +\cdots \bigg)\ket{0}
    \end{align}
    where $Z(b,A)$ is the normalization factor that gives $\ket{\psi(b,A)}$ unit norm. We also define $K_{\theta}\equiv L_b+Q_A$ for $\theta=(b,A)$.
<!-- end fact-3 -->
<!-- lemma-1: body_md -->
\label{lemma:lf-bargmann-moments}
Let $A=A^\top$ with $\|A\|_{\rm op}<1$, let $\ket\psi=\ket{\psi(b,A)}$ and $\pi_{b,A}=\ketbra\psi$, and set $\beta_j=\bra\psi \hat{a}_j\ket\psi$. If $s_1,\ldots,s_n$ are the singular values of $A$, then
\begin{align}
    b&=\beta-A\bar\beta,
    &\beta&=(I-A\bar A)^{-1}(b+A\bar b),\label{eq:lf-displacement-relation}\\
    (1-\|A\|_{\rm op})\|\beta\|_2
    &\leq\|b\|_2
    \leq(1+\|A\|_{\rm op})\|\beta\|_2,\label{eq:lf-displacement-bounds}\\
    \nu(\pi_{b,A})&=\|\beta\|_2^2+
        \sum_{j=1}^n\frac{s_j^2}{1-s_j^2}.\label{eq:lf-bargmann-energy}
\end{align}
<!-- end lemma-1 -->
<!-- lemma-2: body_md -->
\label{lemma:lf-normalization-formula}
For $A=A^\top$ with $\|A\|_{\rm op}<1$, define
\[
    M(A)=\begin{pmatrix}I-\Re A&\Im A\\ \Im A&I+\Re A\end{pmatrix},
    \qquad u(b)=\begin{pmatrix}\Re b\\-\Im b\end{pmatrix}.
\]
If $s_1,\ldots,s_n$ are the singular values of $A$, then $M(A)$ has eigenvalues $1-s_j$ and $1+s_j$, and in particular is positive definite. Moreover,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lf-normalization-formula}
    \log Z(b,A)=-\frac12\log\det M(A)+u(b)^\top M(A)^{-1}u(b).
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-2 -->
<!-- lemma-3: body_md -->
\label{lemma:barg-param-L2-small}
   Let $\ket{\psi(b,A)}$ denote a pure Gaussian state which is near-vacuum, in the sense that $d_{\rm tr}(\ketbra{\psi},\ketbra*{0})\leq \xi_0$ for some universal constant $\xi_0$. Then, 
   \begin{align}
       \|(b,A)\|_{\mathcal P} \lesssim d_{\rm tr}(\ketbra{\psi},\ketbra*{0}) \; .
   \end{align}
<!-- end lemma-3 -->
<!-- proof-1: body_md -->
Define $\xi\equiv d_{\rm tr}(\ketbra{\psi},\ketbra{0})$ such that $\xi=\sqrt{1-\lvert\braket{0}{\psi(b,A)}\rvert^2}=\sqrt{1-1/Z}$ from \eqref{eq:barg-state-expand}. Taking $\xi_0\leq 1/\sqrt 2$, expanding with \eqref{eq:barg-state-expand} gives:
\begin{align}
    \|(b,A+bb^\top)\|_{\mathcal P }^2 =\|b\|_2^2+\frac 12  \|A+bb^\top \|_{F}^2\leq Z-1=\frac{\xi^2}{1-\xi^2}\leq 2\xi^2  
\end{align}
where the left hand side is the $\ket{1},\ket{2}$ contribution to the norm in $Z$ and where the right hand side removes the $\ket{0}$ contribution. Then, 
\begin{align}
    \|(b,A)\|_{\mathcal P}\leq \|(b,A+bb^\top)\|_{\mathcal P}+\|(0,bb^\top )\|_{\mathcal P}\leq \sqrt 2\; \xi  +\frac{\|b\|_2^2}{\sqrt 2 }\leq \sqrt 2 \; \xi +\sqrt 2 \; \xi^2\lesssim \xi 
\end{align}
which proves the claim.
<!-- end proof-1 -->
<!-- lemma-4: body_md -->
\label{lemma:williamson-unsqueezing}
Let $V$ be the covariance matrix of an $n$-mode Gaussian state, and let $\hat V$ be a real symmetric matrix satisfying
\begin{equation}\label{eq:williamson-sandwich}
    0\preceq\hat V-V\preceq\frac{2\zeta}{1-\zeta}(V+I)
\end{equation}
for some $\zeta\in(0,1)$. Take a Williamson decomposition
$\hat V=S\widehat D S^\top$, where $S$ is symplectic and
$\widehat D=\operatorname{diag}(d_1,\ldots,d_n,\\ d_1,\ldots,d_n)$ with $d_j>0$.
Then the Gaussian unitary associated with $S^{-1}$ transforms $V$ into
$V'=S^{-1}VS^{-\top}$ satisfying
\begin{equation}\label{eq:williamson-unsqueezing}
    \|(V')^{-1}\|_{\rm op}
       \leq\sqrt{1+\frac{2\zeta}{1-\zeta}
          \bigl(\|V^{-1}\|_{\rm op}+1\bigr)}.
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-4 -->
<!-- proposition-1: body_md -->
\label{prop:robust-gaussian-learner}
    Let $P$ be a distribution over $\mathbb{R}^d$ and $Q=\mathcal{N}(\mu,\Sigma)$ be a Gaussian  such that $\mathrm{opt}_{\rm TV}\equiv d_{\rm tv}(P,Q)\leq \overline{\mathrm{opt}}_{\rm TV}$ for a sufficiently small universal constant $\overline{\mathrm{opt}}_{\rm TV}$. Then, there exists an algorithm which given $N\geq O(d^2 \log(1/\delta)/{\mathrm{opt}_{\rm TV}}^2)$ samples and $\mathrm{poly}(N)$ time outputs estimates $\hat \mu$ and $\hat \Sigma$, such that with probability at least $1-\delta$:
   \begin{equation}\label{eq:robust-gaussian-learner-relative-guarantee-F}
   \|\Sigma^{-1/2}(\hat \mu-\mu)\|_2\leq \zeta \quad {\rm and}\quad  \|\Sigma^{-1/2}\hat \Sigma \Sigma^{-1/2}-I\|_F\leq \zeta
   \end{equation}
   for $\zeta=\zeta({\mathrm{opt}_{\rm TV}})= O (\mathrm{opt}_{\rm TV} \log (1/\mathrm{opt}_{\rm TV}))$. This implies that $d_{\rm tv}(\mathcal{N}(\mu,\Sigma),\mathcal{N}(\hat \mu,\hat \Sigma))\leq O (\mathrm{opt}_{\rm TV} \log (1/\mathrm{opt}_{\rm TV}))$.
<!-- end proposition-1 -->
<!-- lemma-5: body_md -->
\label{lemma:list-decodable-gaussian}
There are universal constants $C_{\rm dec},r_{\rm dec}>1$ such that the following holds. For $\beta\in(0,1]$ and $d\geq1$, set
\begin{equation}\label{eq:list-decodable-parameters}
    T_\beta=C_{\rm dec}(2/\beta)^{r_{\rm dec}},\qquad
    m_\beta(d)=\left\lceil e^{T_\beta}(d+1)^{T_\beta}\right\rceil,
    \qquad \ell_\beta=\lceil e^{T_\beta}\rceil.
\end{equation}
Given $\beta$ and $m_\beta(d)$ independent samples from any distribution $P$ on $\mathbb{R}^d$, a randomized algorithm with $\mathrm{poly}(m_\beta(d))$ time returns a list of at most $\ell_\beta$ pairs $(\hat\mu,\hat\Sigma)$ with $\hat\Sigma\succ0$. For every fixed Gaussian $Q=\mathcal N(\mu,\Sigma)$ with $\Sigma\succ0$ and
\[
    d_{\rm tv}(P,Q)\leq1-\beta,
\]
with probability at least $9/10$ the list contains a pair satisfying all three bounds
\begin{align}
    T_\beta^{-1}\Sigma&\preceq\hat\Sigma\preceq T_\beta\Sigma,
        \label{eq:list-decodable-spectral}\\
    \bigl\|\Sigma^{-1/2}(\hat\Sigma-\Sigma)\Sigma^{-1/2}\bigr\|_F
        &\leq T_\beta^2,\label{eq:list-decodable-frobenius}\\
    \bigl\|\Sigma^{-1/2}(\hat\mu-\mu)\bigr\|_2
        &\leq\sqrt{T_\beta(1+T_\beta)}.\label{eq:list-decodable-mean}
\end{align}
<!-- end lemma-5 -->
<!-- proposition-2: body_md -->
Let $\{y_i\}_{i\in [N]}$ be the $N$ samples obtained from a heterodyne measurement on $\pi$, and let $\hat \mu_h,\hat \Sigma_h$ be the empirical mean and covariance respectively such that the latter has relative error $(1-\zeta)\Sigma_h\preceq \hat \Sigma_h\preceq (1+\zeta)\Sigma_h$. Construct the following estimators:
   \begin{equation}\label{eq:h-tomo-estimator}
       \hat{\bf m}\equiv \hat \mu_h ,\quad \hat{V}\equiv \frac{2\hat \Sigma_h}{1-\zeta }-I,\quad \hat\pi\equiv \pi(\hat {\bf m}, \hat V)
   \end{equation}
   For universal constant $C$, take $\zeta=C\sqrt{\frac{n+\log 1/\delta}{N}}$ such that $\zeta \leq 1/4$ for suitably large $N$. Then, with probability at least $1-\delta$, the relative error is at most
   \begin{equation}\label{eq:ht-complexity}
       d_{\rm tr}(\pi,\hat\pi)\lesssim (2n+\tr V^{-1})\sqrt{\frac{n+\log 1/\delta }{N}}\leq \epsilon \text{ for }N=O(n(n+\tr V^{-1})^2\epsilon^{-2}\log 1/\delta)
   \end{equation}
   or alternatively $N=O(nE(\pi)^2\epsilon^{-2}\log 1/\delta)$.
<!-- end proposition-2 -->
<!-- theorem-5: body_md -->
\label{thm:robust-heterodyne}
   Let $\rho$  be an $n$-mode state where $d_{\rm tr}(\rho,\pi)\leq {\mathrm{opt}_{\rm tr}}\leq {\overline{\mathrm{opt}}_{\rm tr}}$ for some Gaussian state $\pi$ with mean ${\bf m}$ and covariance $V$. Then, there exists an algorithm which given $\tilde O(n^2 \log^5(1/\delta)/{\mathrm{opt}_{\rm tr}}^2)$ copies of $\rho$ constructs a Gaussian state $\hat \pi $ with the semi-agnostic guarantee:
   \begin{equation}\label{eq:rht-bound}
           d_{\rm tr}(\rho, \hat \pi)\lesssim (n+\tr V^{-1})\cdot \Tilde{O}( {\mathrm{opt}_{\rm tr}})\lesssim E(\pi)\cdot \Tilde{O} ({\mathrm{opt}_{\rm tr}}).
   \end{equation}
<!-- end theorem-5 -->
<!-- theorem-6: body_md -->
\label{thm:eit}
   Let $\pi$ be a Gaussian state with unknown covariance $V$. Then, there exists an algorithm which takes 
    $$N=O(((n+\log\log\log \|V^{-1}\|_{\rm op})\log \log \|V^{-1}\|_{\rm op}+n^3\epsilon^{-2})\log 1/\delta)$$
    copies of $\pi$ and uses exclusively Gaussian measurements to ouptut a Gaussian state $\hat \pi$ such that $d_{\rm tr}(\pi,\hat \pi)\leq \epsilon$ with probability at least $1-\delta$.
<!-- end theorem-6 -->
<!-- theorem-7: body_md -->
\label{thm:reit}
   Let $\rho$ be a $n$-mode state where $d_{\rm tr}(\rho,\pi)\leq {\mathrm{opt}_{\rm tr}}\leq {\overline{\mathrm{opt}}_{\rm tr}}$ for Gaussian state $\pi$ with covariance $V$. Then, there exists an algorithm which takes $O(n^3{\mathrm{opt}_{\rm tr}}^{-2}\log\log\|V^{-1}\|_{\rm op}\log^5(1/\delta))$ copies of $\rho$ and uses exclusively Gaussian measurements to output a Gaussian state $\hat \pi$ satisfying
   \begin{equation}\label{eq:reit-bound}
       d_{\rm tr}(\rho,\hat \pi)\leq n\cdot \Tilde{O}({\mathrm{opt}_{\rm tr}}).
   \end{equation}
<!-- end theorem-7 -->
<!-- theorem-8: body_md -->
\label{thm:near-optimal-pgs-tomo}
     Let $\ket{\psi}$ be a pure Gaussian state with covariance $V$. Then, there exists an algorithm which takes $O(n^2/\epsilon^2 +(n+\log\log\log\|V\|_{\rm op})\log \log \|V\|_{\rm op})$ copies and uses exclusively Gaussian measurements to output a pure Gaussian state $\ket*{\hat \psi}$ such that, with probability $2/3$, $d_{\rm tr}(\ketbra{\psi},\ketbra*{\hat \psi})\leq \epsilon$.
<!-- end theorem-8 -->
<!-- theorem-9: body_md -->
\label{thm:riuproj}
 Let $\rho$ be an $n$-mode state where $d_{\rm tr}(\rho,\ketbra{\psi})\leq {\mathrm{opt}_{\rm tr}}\leq {\overline{\mathrm{opt}}_{\rm tr}}$ for pure Gaussian state $\ket{\psi}$ with covariance $V$. Then, given ${\epsilon>0}$ and $\delta\in(0,1/2)$, there exists an algorithm which takes $\Tilde{O}(n^2 \epsilon^{-2}\log \log \|V^{-1}\|_{\rm op}\allowbreak\log^5(1/\delta))$ copies of $\rho$ and uses exclusively Gaussian measurements to output a pure Gaussian state $\ket*{\hat \psi}$ satisfying
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:riuproj-bound}
     d_{\rm tr}(\rho,\ketbra*{\hat \psi})\leq \Tilde{O}({\mathrm{opt}_{\rm tr}}) +\epsilon.
 \end{equation}
 with probability at least $1-\delta$.
<!-- end theorem-9 -->
<!-- lemma-6: body_md -->
\label{lemma:frob-h-to-trd}
    Suppose $\|V^{-1}\|_\mathrm{op} \leq 2$ and that there exist estimators $(\hat \mu_h,\hat \Sigma_h)$ which satisfy 
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:frob-h-to-trd-hypoth}
        \|\hat \mu_h-\mu_h\|_2\leq \zeta,\quad \|\hat \Sigma_h -\Sigma_h\|_F \leq \zeta 
    \end{equation}
    where $\mu_h={\bf m}$ and $\Sigma_h=\frac{I+V}{2}$ denote the parameters of the heterodyne outcome distribution for pure Gaussian state $\ketbra{\psi}=\pi({\bf m},V)$. Define the estimate $\ketbra*{\hat \psi}\equiv \pi(\hat \mu_h,\hat V)$ where $\hat V=\hat S\hat S^\top$ from the decomposition of $\hat V_{\rm raw}\equiv 2\hat \Sigma_h-I=\hat  S \hat D\hat S^\top $. Then, 
    \begin{equation}
        d_{\rm tr}(\ketbra*{\hat \psi},\ketbra{\psi})\lesssim  \zeta 
    \end{equation}
    provided that $\zeta$ is sufficiently small.
<!-- end lemma-6 -->
<!-- proof-2: body_md -->
Run robust heterodyne tomography to obtain $(\hat \mu_h,\hat \Sigma_h)$ which, by Proposition~\ref{prop:robust-gaussian-learner}, satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:riuproj-simgah-relf}
    \|\Sigma_h^{-1/2}(\hat \mu_h-\mu_h)\|_2\leq \zeta \quad {\rm and}\quad \|\Sigma_h^{-1/2} \hat \Sigma_h \Sigma_h^{-1/2}-I\|_F\leq \zeta
\end{equation}
for $\zeta=\Tilde{O}({\mathrm{opt}_{\rm tr}})$ that satisfies $\zeta \leq 1/4$. Since $\frac 1 2 I\preceq V\preceq 2I$, $\frac 3 4 I\preceq \Sigma_h\preceq \frac 3 2 I $, meaning \eqref{eq:riuproj-simgah-relf} implies $\|\hat \mu_h-\mu_h\|_2\leq \sqrt{\frac 3 2}\zeta$ and $\|\hat \Sigma_h-\Sigma_h\|_F \leq \frac 3 2 \zeta $. 
Then, by triangle inequality,
\begin{equation}
d_{\rm tr}(\rho,\ketbra*{\hat\psi})\leq d_{\rm tr}(\rho,\ketbra{\psi})+d_{\rm tr}(\ketbra{\psi},\ketbra*{\hat\psi})\leq {\mathrm{opt}_{\rm tr}}+O(\zeta)=\Tilde{O}({\mathrm{opt}_{\rm tr}})
\end{equation}
where we use the absolute guarantees to invoke Lemma~\ref{lemma:frob-h-to-trd} and bound the second term.
<!-- end proof-2 -->
<!-- theorem-10: body_md -->
\label{thm:hf}
   Let $\rho$ be an arbitrary $n$-mode bosonic state and $\ket{\psi}$ be a pure Gaussian state with covariance $V$ such that $d_{\rm IF}(\rho,\ketbra{\psi})\leq \mathrm{opt}\leq \overline{\mathrm{opt}}$ for universal constant $\overline{\mathrm{opt}}$. Then, for given $\epsilon > 0$ and $\delta\in(0,1/2)$, there exists an algorithm which takes
   \[
       \Tilde{O}\left(n^2(\log\log \|V^{-1}\|_{\rm op} \log^5(1/\delta)+\epsilon^{-1})\right)
   \]                                                 
   copies and outputs a pure Gaussian state $\ket*{\hat \psi}$ satisfying
   \begin{equation}\label{thm:hf-bound}
      d_{\rm IF}(\rho,\ketbra*{\hat \psi})\leq \mathrm{opt} + \epsilon
   \end{equation}
   with probability at least $1-\delta$.
<!-- end theorem-10 -->
<!-- proposition-3: body_md -->
\label{prop:f-smooth-convex}
There exist universal constants $r_0,C>0$ such that for every $n$, every
density operator $\rho$, and every $\theta\in B_{r_0}$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:hessian-stability}
 \|\nabla^2f_\rho(\theta)-2I\|_{\rm op}
 \le C\bigl(\|\theta\|_{\mathcal P}+d_{\rm tr}(\rho,\ketbra 0)\bigr).
\end{equation}
where the operator norm is induced by $\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal P}$.
In particular, there are universal constants $\gamma_0>0$ and $R\in(0,1/16)$ such that, whenever
$d_{\rm tr}(\rho,\ketbra 0)\le\gamma_0$, the infidelity objective satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:convex-smooth}
 I\preceq\nabla^2f_\rho(\theta)\preceq3I
 \qquad (\theta\in B_R),
\end{equation}
and its unique minimizer $\theta_*$ over $B_R$ satisfies $\|\theta_*\|_{\mathcal P}\leq R/4$.
<!-- end proposition-3 -->
<!-- lemma-7: body_md -->
\label{eq:Ktheta-growth}
    Let $\Pi_{\leq m}$ be the projector onto states with total photon number at most $m$. For $\ket{\phi}\in \mathcal F_{\leq m}\equiv \Pi_{\leq m}\mathcal F$, we have that $\|K_\theta \ket{\phi}\|_2\leq 2(m+2)\|\theta \|_{\mathcal P}\|\ket{\phi}\|_2$.
<!-- end lemma-7 -->
<!-- proof-3: body_md -->
WLOG, we perform a unitary change of modes such that $b=\|b\|_2e_1$. Then, 
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:Lb-growth}
        \|L_b\phi\|_2^2=\|b\|_2^2 \bra{\phi}\hat{a}_1\hat{a}_1^\dagger \ket{\phi}=\|b\|_2^2 \bra{\phi}(\hat{a}_1^\dagger \hat{a}_1+I)\ket{\phi}\leq (m+1)\|b\|_2^2\|\ket{\phi}\|_2^2
    \end{equation}
    Similarly, we claim that:
    \begin{align}\label{eq:QA-growth}
        \|Q_A\ket{\phi}\|_2\leq \frac 1 2 \|A\|_F\sqrt{(m+1)(m+2)}\|\ket{\phi}\|_2
    \end{align}
    Since every term in $Q_A$ has two creation operators and $\ket{\phi}\in \mathcal F_{\leq m}$, we have that $Q_A\ket{\phi}\in \mathcal F_{\leq m+2}$. Considering the variational characterization of the left hand side for any normalized $\ket{\varphi}\in \mathcal F_{\leq m+2}$, 
    \begin{align}
        |\bra{\varphi}Q_A\ket{\phi}|=\frac 1 2 \left|\sum_{i,j=1}^n A_{ij}\bra{\varphi}\hat{a}_i^\dagger \hat{a}_j^\dagger \ket{\phi}\right|&\leq \frac 1 2 \|A\|_F\left(\sum_{i,j=1}^n|\bra{\varphi}\hat{a}_i^\dagger \hat{a}_j^\dagger \ket{\phi}|^2\right)^{1/2}\\
        &\leq \frac 1 2 \|A\|_F \|\ket{\phi}\|_2 \left(\sum_{i,j=1}^n \|\hat{a}_j\hat{a}_i \ket{\varphi}\|_2^2\right)^{1/2}\label{eq:QA-growth-1}
    \end{align}
    where we apply Cauchy-Schwarz twice. Since the ladder operators for different modes commute, for $i\neq j$, $\hat{a}_i^\dagger \hat{a}_j^\dagger \hat{a}_j \hat{a}_i=N_i N_j$ where $N_i=\hat{a}_i^\dagger \hat{a}_i $ is the number operator for the $i$-th mode, while for $i=j$, $\hat{a}_i^\dagger \hat{a}_j^\dagger \hat{a}_j \hat{a}_i=\hat{a}_i^\dagger (\hat{a}_i\hat{a}_i^\dagger-1)\hat{a}_i=N_i(N_i-1)$. Then, 
    \begin{align}
    \sum_{i,j=1}^n\|\hat{a}_j\hat{a}_i\ket{\varphi}\|_2^2 =\bra{\varphi}\left(\sum_{i,j=1}^na_i^\dagger \hat{a}_j^\dagger \hat{a}_j \hat{a}_i \right)\ket{\varphi }=\bra{\varphi}\left(\sum_{i\neq j}^n N_iN_j+\sum_{i=1}^n N_i(N_i-1)\right)\ket{\varphi}&=\bra{\varphi}N(N-1)\ket{\varphi}\\
    &\leq (m+2)(m+1)
    \end{align}
    where $N=\sum_{i=1}^n N_i$ is the total number operator and where we recall that $\ket{\varphi}\in \mathcal F_{\leq m+2}$ in the last equality. Substituting this into \eqref{eq:QA-growth-1} then proves \eqref{eq:QA-growth}. Thus, by triangle inequality,
    \begin{align}
        \|K_\theta \ket{\phi}\|_2\leq \|L_b \ket{\phi}\|_2+\|Q_A\ket{\phi}\|_2&\leq \left(\|b\|_2\sqrt{m+1}+\frac 1 2 \|A\|_F\sqrt{(m+1)(m+2)}\right)\|\ket{\phi}\|_2\\
        &\leq 2(m+2)\|\theta\|_{ \mathcal P}\|\ket{\phi}\|_2 \label{eq:L_b+Q_A-0}
    \end{align}
    which proves the bound.
<!-- end proof-3 -->
<!-- lemma-8: body_md -->
\label{lemma:barg-derivatives}
There is a universal constant $C_D$ such that, for every $k\geq 1$, $\|\theta\|_{\mathcal P}\leq 1/8$, and directions $h_1,\dots,h_k\in\mathcal P$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:barg-derivatives}
    \|\ket{D^k\psi(\theta)[h_1,\dots,h_k]}\|_2
    \leq C_D^k k!\prod_{i=1}^k\|h_i\|_{\mathcal P}\,.
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-8 -->
<!-- proof-4: body_md -->
Let $r_0=1/8$, and for brevity, denote $g_h(\theta)\equiv Dg(\theta)[h]$ and $g_{h_1h_2}(\theta)\equiv D^2 g(\theta)[h_1,h_2]$, where $g$ may be $Z, \Phi,$ or $\psi$. Further, let $C_j\equiv C_D^j j! $. In broad strokes, we first compute the Hessian of $f_{\ketbra{0}}(\theta)$ at $\theta=0$. Then, we use third derivative control to control the Hessian of $f_{\ketbra{0}}(\theta) $ for $\|\theta \|_{\mathcal P}\leq r_0$, and finally, we use second derivative control to handle the Hessian of $f_{\rho}(\theta)$. 

We begin with the Hessian for $\rho=\ketbra{0}$ at $\theta=0$. Since $K_\theta$ is linear in $\theta$ and all creation operators commute, $e^{K_{\theta+th}}=e^{K_\theta}e^{tK_h}$, so $D\Phi(\theta)[h]=K_he^{K_\theta}\ket0$. With \eqref{eq:barg-state}, this gives $\ket{\Phi(0)}=\ket 0$ and $D\Phi(0)[h]=K_h\ket{0}$, which is orthogonal to $\ket 0$. Then, by product rule, 
\begin{align}
    Z_h(0)&=2\Re\braket{\Phi(0)}{\Phi_h(0)}=0\\
    \ket{\psi_h(0)}&=\cancel{-\frac 1 2 Z(0)^{-3/2}Z_h(0)\ket{\Phi(0)}}+Z(0)^{-1/2}\ket{\Phi_h(0)}=K_h\ket 0
\end{align}
Applying product rule again, 
\begin{align}
   f_{\ketbra 0}{(0)} &=0\\
   Df_{\ketbra 0}(0)[h]&=-2\Re\braket{\psi(0)}{0}\cancel{\braket{0}{\psi_h(0)}}=0
   \label{eq:D1-vac-theta0}
   \\
   D^2  f_{\ketbra 0}(0)[h,h]&=-2\Re\braket{\psi(0)}{0}\braket{0}{\psi_{hh}(0)}-\cancel{2\|\braket{0}{\psi_h(0)}\|^2_2}=2\|\ket{\psi_h(0)}\|_2^2=2\|K_h\ket 0\|^2_2 = 2\|h\|_{\mathcal P }^2 \label{eq:D2-vac-theta0}
\end{align}
where the second equality in the last line uses the following manipulation of the normalization $\|\ket{\psi(\theta)}\|_2=1$: 
\begin{align}
0=D^2\|\ket{\psi(\theta)}\|_2^2[h,h]=2\Re\braket{\psi(\theta)}{\psi_{hh}(\theta)}+2\|\ket{\psi_h(\theta)}\|_2^2
\end{align}

With the vacuum Hessian at $\theta=0$ computed, we continue to control the change in the Hessian for $\|\theta\|_{\mathcal P }\leq r_0$ and then for $\rho$ beyond the vacuum state. Writing $s^{(H)}(\theta)=\bra{\psi(\theta)}H\ket{\psi(\theta)}$ for any bounded self-adjoint $H$, product rule yields:
\begin{align}
   s^{(H)}_{hh} (\theta)&=2\Re\bra{ \psi_{hh}(\theta)}H\ket{\psi(\theta)}+2\bra{\psi_h(\theta)}H\ket{\psi_h(\theta)}\\
   s^{(H)}_{uhh}(\theta)&=2\Re\bra{\psi_{uhh}(\theta)}H\ket{\psi(\theta)}+2\Re\bra{\psi_{hh}(\theta)}H\ket{\psi_u(\theta)}+4\Re\bra{\psi_{uh}(\theta)}H\ket{\psi_h(\theta)}
\end{align}
Since $\|\ket{\psi(\theta)}\|_2=1$ and Lemma~\ref{lemma:barg-derivatives} holds for $\|\theta\|_{\mathcal P}\leq 1/8=r_0$, by triangle inequality and these bounds,
\begin{align}
    {|s^{(H)}_{hh}(\theta)|}&\leq2(C_2+C_1^2)\|H\|_{\rm op}\|h\|_{\mathcal P}^2
    \label{eq:D2-sH-hh-size}
    \\
    {|s^{(H)}_{uhh}(\theta)|}&\leq(2C_3+6C_1C_2)\|H\|_{\rm op}\|u\|_{\mathcal P}\|h\|_{\mathcal P}^2\label{eq:D3-sH-uhh-size}
\end{align}
Then, by the fundamental theorem of calculus, \eqref{eq:D2-vac-theta0}, and \eqref{eq:D3-sH-uhh-size},
\begin{align}
    |D^2 f_{\ketbra 0}(\theta)[h,h]-2\|h\|_{\mathcal P}^2|=\left|\int_0^1D^3 f_{\ketbra 0}(t\theta)[\theta,h,h]dt\right|\leq (2C_3+6C_1 C_2)\|\theta\|_{\mathcal P }\|h\|_{\mathcal P }^2 \label{eq:D2-vac-0-to-vac-theta}
\end{align}
where we notice $f_{\ketbra{0}}(\theta)=1-s^{(H)}(\theta)$ for $H=\ketbra 0$ which has $\|H\|_{\rm op}=1$. Now all that is left is controlling the Hessian when $\Delta \equiv \rho-\ketbra 0\neq 0$. 
\begin{align}
    |D^2 f_\rho(\theta)[h,h]-D^2f_{\ketbra 0 }(\theta)[h,h]|=|s_{hh}^{(\Delta)}(\theta)|\leq 2(C_2+C_1^2)\|\Delta \|_{\rm op}\|h\|_{\mathcal P }^2\label{eq:D2-vac-th-to-rho-th}
\end{align}
Noting that $\|\Delta \|_{\rm op}\leq 2d_{\rm tr}(\rho,\ketbra{0})$, by triangle inequality, \eqref{eq:D2-vac-0-to-vac-theta}, \eqref{eq:D2-vac-th-to-rho-th},
\begin{align}
    |D^2 f_\rho(\theta)[h,h]-2\|h\|_{\mathcal P}^2|&\leq (2C_3+6C_1 C_2)\|\theta\|_{\mathcal P }\|h\|_{\mathcal P }^2+2(C_2+C_1^2)\|\Delta \|_{\rm op}\|h\|_{\mathcal P }^2\\
    &\leq C(\|\theta\|_{\mathcal P}+d_{\rm tr}(\rho,\ketbra 0))\|h\|_{\mathcal P}^2
\end{align}
where we choose $C$ according to $C_1,C_2,C_3$. Since this holds for all unit $h$, we have proven \eqref{eq:hessian-stability}. Then, taking
\[
    R=\min\{r_0/4,1/(2C)\},\qquad
    \gamma_0=\min\{1/(2C),R^2/64\},
\]
we have $\|\nabla^2f_\rho(\theta)-2I\|_{\rm op}\leq C(R+\gamma_0)\leq1$ for $\theta\in B_R$, proving \eqref{eq:convex-smooth}. Further, $\|A\|_{\rm op}\leq\sqrt2\|\theta\|_{\mathcal P}\leq\sqrt2R<1$ for $R<1/16$, so every point of $B_R$ describes a valid pure Gaussian state.

To show that the last part about the minimizer $\theta_*$ over $B_R$, we use the fundamental theorem of calculus twice and recall that $f_{\ketbra{0}}(0)=Df_{\ketbra{0}}(0)[\theta]=0$ from \eqref{eq:D1-vac-theta0}: 
\begin{align}
f_{\ketbra 0}(\theta)=\int_0^1 \int_0^s D^2 f_{\ketbra 0}(t\theta)[\theta,\theta] dt ds=\int_0^1 (1-t)D^2 f_{\ketbra 0}(t\theta)[\theta,\theta] dt\geq \int_0^1 (1-t)\|\theta\|_{\mathcal P}^2 dt \geq \frac 1 2 \|\theta\|_{\mathcal P}^2 
\end{align}
where we use the lower bound of \eqref{eq:convex-smooth}. Then, for $\theta$ on the boundary of $B_R$, by triangle inequality, ${\gamma_0\leq R^2/64}$, and $\|\Delta \|_{\rm op}\leq 2\gamma_0$,
\begin{align}
    f_\rho(\theta)\geq f_{\ketbra 0}(\theta)-|f_\rho(\theta)-f_{\ketbra 0}(\theta)|\geq R^2/2-\|\Delta\|_{\rm op}\geq R^2/2-2\gamma_0\geq 3R^2/8{>}2\gamma_0\geq f_\rho(0)
\end{align}
where we use the fact that $f_\rho(0)=|\bra{0}(\ketbra 0 -\rho)\ket 0 |\leq \|\Delta \|_{\rm op}\leq 2\gamma_0$. Then, by continuity of $f_\rho$, compactness of $B_R$, and the fact that the boundary is strictly worse than the origin, it follows that all minimizers are in the interior. Letting one minimizer be $\theta_*$ such that $\nabla f_\rho(\theta_*)=0$, by the fundamental theorem of calculus and \eqref{eq:convex-smooth} in the same manner as before,
\begin{align}
    f_\rho(\theta)\geq f_\rho(\theta_*)+\cancel{Df_\rho(\theta_*)[\theta-\theta_*]}+\int_0^1 (1-t)D^2 f_{\rho}(\theta_* (1-t)+t\theta)[\theta-\theta_*,\theta-\theta_*]dt \geq f_\rho(\theta_*)+\frac 1 2 \|\theta-\theta_*\|_{\mathcal P}^2
\end{align}
which implies that any $\theta \neq \theta_* $ has $f_\rho(\theta)>f_\rho(\theta_*)$, meaning the minimizer is unique. Finally, taking $\theta=0$, $\frac 1 2 \|\theta_*\|_{\mathcal P}^2 \leq f_\rho(0)\leq 2\gamma_0\leq R^2/32$ proves the $\|\theta_*\|_{\mathcal P}\leq R/4$ claim.
<!-- end proof-4 -->
<!-- lemma-9: body_md -->
\label{lemma:hf-value-oracle}
For $\theta\in B_R$, we can estimate $f_\rho(\theta)$ to additive error $\kappa$ with probability at least $1-\delta$ using $O(\kappa^{-2}\log(2/\delta))$ copies of $\rho$.
<!-- end lemma-9 -->
<!-- proof-5: body_md -->
Let $U_\theta$ be the Gaussian unitary which prepares $\ket{\psi(\theta)}=U_\theta \ket 0$. For each copy, we apply $U_\theta^\dagger$ and then measure the two-outcome POVM $\{\ketbra 0,I-\ketbra 0\}$. The latter outcome has the probability:
\begin{align}
    p_{I-\ketbra 0} =\tr \left((I-\ketbra 0)U_\theta^\dagger \rho U_\theta\right)=d_{\rm IF}(\rho,\ketbra{\psi(\theta)})
\end{align}
which is exactly the estimand. Thus, an empirical average with $O(\kappa^{-2}\log(2/\delta))$ copies is sufficient by Hoeffding's inequality.
<!-- end proof-5 -->
<!-- lemma-10: body_md -->
\label{lemma:hf-gradient-error}
   If $d_{\rm tr}(\rho,\ketbra 0)\leq \gamma_0$, $\theta \in B_{R/2}$, and $\kappa\in (0,R/4]$, we can estimate $\nabla f_\rho(\theta)$ to additive error $\kappa$ in $\|\cdot \|_{\mathcal P}$ with probability at least $1-\delta$ using $O(n^6 \kappa^{-4}\log(n/\delta))$.
<!-- end lemma-10 -->
<!-- proposition-4: body_md -->
\label{prop:hf-gd}\label{lemma:hf-pgd}
    Suppose $d_{\rm tr}(\rho,\ketbra 0)\leq\gamma_0$ and let $\mathrm{opt}\equiv\inf_{\ket\psi\in\mathcal G}d_{\rm IF}(\rho,\ketbra\psi)$. Then, given $\epsilon\in (0,1)$ and $\delta\in(0,1/2)$, there exists an algorithm which takes $\Tilde{O}(n^6\epsilon^{-2}\log(1/\delta))$ copies of $\rho$ and outputs a pure Gaussian state $\ket*{\hat\psi}$ such that
    \begin{align}
        d_{\rm IF}(\rho,\ketbra*{\hat\psi})\leq\mathrm{opt}+\epsilon
    \end{align}
    with probability at least $1-\delta$.
<!-- end proposition-4 -->
<!-- proof-6: body_md -->
We first show that $\mathrm{opt} =\min_{\theta\in B_R}d_{\rm IF}(\rho,\ketbra{\psi(\theta)})$, i.e. the minimizer is near-vacuum. Let $\ket{\psi}$ be any pure Gaussian state with $d_{\rm IF}(\rho,\ketbra{\psi})\leq d_{\rm IF}(\rho,\ketbra{0})$. Then, since $d_{\rm IF}(\rho,\ketbra{0})\leq 2d_{\rm tr}(\rho,\ketbra{0})$,
\begin{align}
    \xi\equiv d_{\rm tr}(\ketbra{\psi},\ketbra{0})\leq d_{\rm tr}(\rho,\ketbra{\psi})+d_{\rm tr}(\rho,\ketbra{0})\leq \sqrt{d_{\rm IF}(\rho,\ketbra{0})}+\gamma_0 \leq \sqrt{2\gamma_0}+\gamma_0\leq R/4
\end{align}
Then, provided $R\leq 4\xi_0$, the proof of Lemma~\ref{lemma:barg-param-L2-small} gives that $\|\theta\|_{\mathcal P}\leq\sqrt2(\xi+\xi^2)\leq2\xi\leq R/2$. This means that every pure Gaussian state with infidelity at least as good as the vacuum, including the minimizer, must lie in $B_R$. Thus, 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:hf-local-opt}
    \mathrm{opt}=\min_{\theta\in B_R}f_\rho(\theta)=f_\rho(\theta_*),
\end{equation}
We begin our algorithm by taking $\kappa =\min\{R/4,\sqrt{\epsilon/6}\}$ and $T=\max\{1,\log_{3/2}(R/(4\kappa))\}$. Starting with $\theta_0=0$, we then iterate the following gradient descent step for $t\in [T]$:
\begin{align}\label{eq:hf-gd-step}
    \theta_{t}=\theta_{t-1}-\frac 1 3 \widehat{\nabla f_{\rho}}(\theta_t)
\end{align}
where the step size is $1/3$ and $\widehat{\nabla f_{\rho}}(\theta_t)$ is the zeroth-order estimator from Lemma~\ref{lemma:hf-gradient-error}. After the last step, we simply output $\ket*{\hat \psi}=\ket{\psi(\theta_T)}$.

We first use our Hessian bounds to show that the iterates approach $\theta_*$. Since $\nabla f_\rho(\theta_*)=0$, the fundamental theorem of calculus gives:
\begin{align}\label{eq:grad-theta-t}
    \nabla f_\rho(\theta_t)=H_{\theta_t}(\theta_t-\theta_*),\quad H_{\theta_t}=\int_0^1 \nabla^2 f_\rho((1-s)\theta_*+s\theta_t)ds
\end{align}
where \eqref{eq:convex-smooth} implies $I\preceq H_{\theta_t}\preceq 3I $ and thus $\|I-H_{\theta_t}/3\|_{\rm op}\leq 2/3$. Then, by this fact, triangle inequality, \eqref{eq:hf-gd-step}, \eqref{eq:grad-theta-t}, and the additive error in \eqref{lemma:hf-gradient-error}, 
\begin{align}
    \|\theta_{t}-\theta_*\|_{\mathcal P }&\leq \left\|\theta_{t-1}-\theta_* -\frac 1 3 \nabla f_\rho(\theta_{t-1})\right\|_{\mathcal P }+\frac 1 3 \|\nabla f_\rho(\theta_{t-1})-\widehat{\nabla f_\rho}(\theta_{t-1})\|\leq \|(I-H_{\theta_{t-1}})(\theta_{t-1}-\theta_*)\|_{\mathcal P}+\frac \kappa 3 \\
    &\leq \frac 2 3 \|\theta_{t-1}-\theta_*\|_{\mathcal P}+\frac \kappa 3 \label{eq:thetat-conv-step}
\end{align}
Since $\|\theta_0-\theta_*\|_{\mathcal P}\leq R/4$ by Proposition~\ref{prop:f-smooth-convex}                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                              , if $\|\theta_{t-1}-\theta_*\|_{\mathcal P}\leq R/4$, $\theta_t\in B_{R/2}$. Substituting into \eqref{eq:thetat-conv-step} with $\kappa\leq R/4$, we have that $\|\theta_{t}-\theta_*\|_{\mathcal P}\leq R/4$ as well, meaning all iterates remain in $B_{R/2}$ by induction. Then, recurring \eqref{eq:thetat-conv-step} within itself, 
\begin{align}
    \|\theta_{T}-\theta_*\|_{\mathcal P }&\leq \frac 2 3 \|\theta_{T-1}-\theta_*\|_{\mathcal P}+\frac \kappa 3 \leq \frac 2 3 \left(\frac 2 3 \|\theta_{t-2}-\theta_*\|_{\mathcal P}+\frac \kappa 3 \right)+\frac \kappa 3 \leq \cdots\leq (2/3)^T \frac R 4 +\frac \kappa 3 \sum_{j=0}^{T-1}(2/3)^j \\
    &\leq 2\kappa\label{eq:thetat-conv}
\end{align}
Then, by the fundamental theorem of calculus and \eqref{eq:convex-smooth}, since $\nabla f_\rho(\theta_*)=0$,
\begin{align}
    f_\rho(\theta_T)-f_\rho(\theta_*)&=\int_0^1 (1-t) D^2 f_\rho((1-t)\theta_*+t\theta_T)[\theta_T-\theta_*,\theta_T-\theta_*]dt\leq 3\|\theta_T-\theta_*\|_{\mathcal P }^2 \int_0^1 (1-t)dt \\
    &\leq \frac 3 2 \|\theta_T-\theta_*\|_{\mathcal P }^2
\end{align}
Applying \eqref{eq:thetat-conv}, this gives $f_{\rho}(\theta_T)-f_{\rho}(\theta_*)\leq 6\kappa^2 \leq \epsilon $ which combines with \eqref{eq:hf-local-opt} to prove the main guarantee. By Lemma~\ref{lemma:hf-gradient-error}, each gradient estimate uses $O(n^6\kappa^{-4}\log(n/\delta))$ copies by Lemma~\ref{lemma:hf-gradient-error}. Substituting the values for $\kappa, R, T$, this reproduces the claimed copy complexity.
<!-- end proof-6 -->
<!-- proposition-5: body_md -->
\label{prop:hf-gd-n2}\label{lemma:hf-pgd-n2}
    Suppose $d_{\rm tr}(\rho,\ketbra 0)\leq\gamma_0$ and let $\mathrm{opt}\equiv\inf_{\ket\psi\in\mathcal G}d_{\rm IF}(\rho,\ketbra\psi)$. Then, given $\epsilon\in (0,1)$ and $\delta\in(0,1/2)$, there exists an algorithm which takes $\Tilde{O}(n^2\epsilon^{-1}\log(1/\delta))$ copies of $\rho$ and outputs a pure Gaussian state $\ket*{\hat\psi}$ such that
    \begin{align}
        d_{\rm IF}(\rho,\ketbra*{\hat\psi})\leq\mathrm{opt}+\epsilon
    \end{align}
    with probability at least $1-\delta$.
<!-- end proposition-5 -->
<!-- lemma-11: body_md -->
\label{lemma:hf-direct-gradient-est}
For state $\sigma$, $\kappa\in(0,1)$, and $\delta\in(0,1/2)$, we can
estimate $\nabla f_\sigma(0)$ to error $\kappa$ in $\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal P}$ using
$O(n^2\kappa^{-2}\log(2/\delta))$ copies and general single-copy measurements.
<!-- end lemma-11 -->
<!-- proof-7: body_md -->
Let $\Pi$ be the projector onto $\mathcal F_{\leq 2}$, and define $P=\Pi_{\leq 2}-\ketbra 0$ and $d_{\leq 2}=\dim \mathcal F_{\leq 2}=1+n+n(n+1)/2$. Then, from the proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:f-smooth-convex}, 
    \begin{align}
    \langle h,\nabla f_\sigma (0)\rangle_{\mathcal P}=D f_\sigma(0)[h]=-2\Re \bra{0}K_h^\dagger \sigma \ket{0}=\Re\langle K_h\ket 0,-2P\sigma \ket 0 \rangle 
\end{align}
where we use the fact that $K_h\ket 0\in \mathcal F_{\leq 2}$ is orthogonal to $\ket{0}$ giving $PK_h\ket 0=K_h\ket 0$. Since we know that $\langle h,g\rangle_{\mathcal P}=\Re\langle K_h\ket{0},K_g\ket{0}\rangle$, it follows that $K_{\nabla f_\sigma(0)}\ket 0 =-2P\sigma \ket{0}$ and that it suffices to estimate this quantity to $\ell_2$ error $O(\kappa)$, since we can simply read off the Bargmann parameters from the Fock coefficients of the state. 

    We do this using the POVM $\mathcal M =\{I-\Pi, d_{\leq 2}\ketbra{v} d\mu(v)\}$ where $\mu$ is the uniform measure on the unit sphere of $\mathcal F_{\leq 2}$. For outcome $v$, we record $X=(d_{\leq 2}+1)P\ket{v}\braket{v}{0}$, and for $I-\Pi$, we record $X=0$. Then, 
    \begin{align}
        \operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}[X]&=d_{\leq 2}(d_{\leq 2}+1)\int P\ket{v}\braket{v}{0}\cdot \bra{v}\sigma \ket{v}d\mu(v)\\
        &=(d_{\leq 2}+1)P\left(d_{\leq 2}\int \bra{v}\sigma\ket{v}\ketbra{v} d\mu(v)\right)\ket{0}\\
        &=(d_{\leq 2}+1)P\cdot \frac{\Pi\sigma\Pi +\tr(\Pi\sigma)\Pi }{d_{\leq 2}+1}\cdot \ket{0}\\
        &=P\sigma \ket{0}
    \end{align}
    where we use the standard Haar second-moment identity and use $P$ to annihilate the second term. For outcome $v$, $\|X\|_2^2\leq (d_{\leq 2}+1)^2\lvert \braket{v}{0}\rvert^2 $ since $\|P\ket{v}\|_2^2\leq 1$. Then, 
    \begin{align}
        \operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}[\|X\|_2^2]&\leq d_{\leq 2}(d_{\leq 2}+1)^2\int  \lvert \braket{v}{0}\rvert^2\bra{v}\sigma \ket{v}d\mu(v)\leq (d_{\leq 2}+1)^2\bra{0}\left(d_{\leq 2}\int \bra{v}\sigma\ket{v}\ketbra{v} d\mu(v)\right)\ket{0}\\
        &\leq (d_{\leq 2}+1)^2\bra{0}\cdot \frac{\Pi\sigma\Pi +\tr(\Pi\sigma)\Pi }{d_{\leq 2}+1}\cdot \ket{0}\\
        &\leq (d_{\leq 2}+1)(\bra{0}\sigma \ket{0}+\tr(\Pi\sigma))\\
        &\leq 2 (d_{\leq 2}+1)
    \end{align}
    A standard median-of-means estimator obtains error $\kappa$ with $O(d_{\leq 2}\kappa^{-2}\log(1/\delta))$ copies.
<!-- end proof-7 -->
<!-- theorem-11: body_md -->
\label{thm:lfpr-unsqueezing}
Fix $\alpha\in(0,1]$, $A_0\geq1$, and $\eta\in(0,1/2]$. There is an algorithm which, given copies of an arbitrary $n$-mode state $\rho$, returns a list of at most $W=\lceil8/(3\alpha)\rceil$ Gaussian bases, or reports failure. For every fixed best Gaussian approximator $\pi_*$ satisfying
\[
    F(\rho,\pi_*)\geq\alpha,\qquad \kappa(V_*)\leq A_0,
\]
with probability at least $1-\eta$ it returns a list containing a basis $G$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lfpr-final-frame}
    \nu(G\pi_*G^\dagger)\leq H_\alpha,
\end{equation}
where we may take $H_\alpha=e\mathsf K_\alpha^2\asymp\alpha^{-s}$ for a
universal exponent $s\asymp r_{\rm dec}$, with $\mathsf K_\alpha$ defined
in \eqref{eq:lfpr-contraction-constant}. The number of copies and total runtime of the algorithm are both at most
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lfpr-preprocessing-cost}
    \exp (C t_\alpha) n^{t_\alpha} \mathrm{poly}\bigl(\log(1/\eta),\,\log\log(e+A_0)\bigr),
\end{equation}
where $C_{\rm dec},r_{\rm dec}$ are as in Lemma~\ref{lemma:list-decodable-gaussian}.
<!-- end theorem-11 -->
<!-- lemma-12: body_md -->
\label{lemma:lfpr-list}
    
                                                  
                                     
               
                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                
In any adaptively
chosen basis, a classical list decoding algorithm run on
$s=O \left(  e^{t_\alpha}(3n)^{t_\alpha} \right)$ fresh heterodyne outcomes returns
at most $O(e^{t_\alpha})$ pairs, each with positive definite covariance. 
With probability at least $9/10$, one pair $(\hat\mu,\hat\Sigma)$ satisfies
all three bounds
\begin{align}\label{eq:lfpr-list-guarantees}
 t_\alpha^{-1}\Sigma\preceq\hat\Sigma\preceq t_\alpha\Sigma,\qquad
 \bigl\|\Sigma^{-1/2}(\hat\Sigma-\Sigma)\Sigma^{-1/2}\bigr\|_F
 &\leq t_\alpha^2,\\
 \bigl\|\Sigma^{-1/2}(\hat\mu-{\bf m})\bigr\|_2&\leq\sqrt{t_\alpha(1+t_\alpha)}.\notag
\end{align}
<!-- end lemma-12 -->
<!-- proof-8: body_md -->
Let $P$ and $Q=\mathcal N({\bf m},\Sigma)$ be the heterodyne laws of the input
and $\pi_*$ in the chosen basis. Measurement contractivity and the
pure-state fidelity bound imply
\[
 1-d_{\rm tv}(P,Q)\geq1-d_{\rm tr}(\rho,\pi)
 \geq1-\sqrt{1-\alpha}\geq\alpha/2.
\]
The result then directly follows from Lemma~\ref{lemma:list-decodable-gaussian}.
<!-- end proof-8 -->
<!-- lemma-13: body_md -->
\label{lemma:lfpr-warm-frame}
If $A^{-1}I\preceq V\preceq AI$ and the terminal pair satisfies
\eqref{eq:lfpr-list-guarantees}, then the fitted basis maps the
best Gaussian approximator to a state with total mean photon number at most
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lfpr-warm-energy}
 h_\alpha(A)=u_\alpha A^8,\qquad
 u_\alpha=1200\bigl(t_\alpha^6+t_\alpha(1+t_\alpha)\bigr)\asymp t_\alpha^6.
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-13 -->
<!-- lemma-14: body_md -->
\label{lemma:lfpr-compatibility}
Let $U=GH^\dagger$, and let $U\ket0$ have mean $\mathbf d$, covariance
$C$, and squeezing parameters $r_1,\ldots,r_n\geq0$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lfpr-compatibility-formula}
    \Delta(G,H)=\sum_{j=1}^n(\cosh r_j-1)
       +\frac12\mathbf d^\top(I+C)^{-1}\mathbf d.
\end{equation}
Thus $\Delta$ is computable from the relative Gaussian transformation
using polynomially many matrix operations.
<!-- end lemma-14 -->
<!-- proposition-6: body_md -->
\label{prop:lfpr-frame-comparison}
Let constants $C_{\rm tr}=32$, $d=32$, and a sufficiently large universal
$C_{\rm sep}\geq1$. 
                                                                                     
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
    \item For every pure Gaussian $\pi$ with $\nu_G(\pi)\leq h$ and $h\geq1$,
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:lfpr-energy-transfer}
        \nu_H(\pi)\leq C_{\rm tr}(\Delta(G,H)+1)^3h.
    \end{equation}
    \item For every integer $k\geq 1$ and $0<\mu\leq1/2$, set
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:lfpr-separation-threshold}
        T(k,\mu)=C_{\rm sep}\left(\frac{k}{\mu}\right)^d.
    \end{equation}
    If $\Delta(G,H)>T(k,\mu)$, then
    $\|P_G(k)P_H(k)\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\mu$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end proposition-6 -->
<!-- proof-9: body_md -->
Work in basis $G$. Let $(\mathbf m,V)$ be the moments of $\pi_*$
and $(\mathbf d,C)$ those of the $H$-vacuum in this basis.
Writing $C=SS^\top$, the passage to basis $H$ transforms these moments
to $S^{-1}(\mathbf m-\mathbf d)$ and $S^{-1}VS^{-\top}$, up to a passive
transformation. Hence
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lfpr-relative-energy}
    \nu_H(\pi)=\frac{\tr(C^{-1}V)-2n}{4}
       +\frac12(\mathbf m-\mathbf d)^\top C^{-1}(\mathbf m-\mathbf d).
\end{equation}
Put $\Delta=\Delta(G,H)$ and $b=(\tr C-2n)/4$.
By \eqref{eq:lfpr-compatibility-formula},
\begin{align}\label{eq:lfpr-relative-moment-bounds}
    b&=\sum_j\sinh^2r_j
       =\sum_j(\cosh r_j-1)^2+2\sum_j(\cosh r_j-1)
       \leq\Delta(\Delta+2),\\
    \|C^{\pm1}\|_{\mathrm{op}}&\leq4(\Delta+1)^2,
    \qquad Q:=\mathbf d^\top(I+C)^{-1}\mathbf d\leq2\Delta.\notag
\end{align}
Comparing eigenvalues of $C^{-1}$ and $(I+C)^{-1}$ then gives
\[
    \mathbf d^\top C^{-1}\mathbf d
    \leq(1+\|C^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{op}})Q
    \leq10(\Delta+1)^3.
\]

To control the trace term without introducing a factor of $n$, note
that a pure covariance $W$ with $u=(\tr W-2n)/4$ satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lfpr-pure-frobenius}
    \|W-I\|_F^2\leq16u^2+8u\leq16(u+1)^2.
\end{equation}
Indeed, its reciprocal eigenvalue pair $(\lambda_j,\lambda_j^{-1})$
contributes $16u_j^2+8u_j$, where
$u_j=(\lambda_j+\lambda_j^{-1}-2)/4\geq0$ and $\sum_j u_j=u$.
For $A=C^{-1}-I$ and $B=V-I$, purity and $h\geq1$ give
$\tr A=4b$, $\tr B\leq4h$,
$\|A\|_F\leq4(b+1)$, and $\|B\|_F\leq5h$.
Therefore
\[
    \tr(C^{-1}V)-2n
    =\tr A+\tr B+\tr(AB)
    \leq4b+4h+20(b+1)h
    \leq28(\Delta+1)^2h.
\]
Finally $\|\mathbf m\|_2^2\leq2h$, so
\[
    (\mathbf m-\mathbf d)^\top C^{-1}(\mathbf m-\mathbf d)
    \leq2\|C^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|\mathbf m\|_2^2
       +2\mathbf d^\top C^{-1}\mathbf d
    \leq36(\Delta+1)^3h.
\]
Substitution into \eqref{eq:lfpr-relative-energy} gives
$\nu_H(\pi)\leq25(\Delta+1)^3h\leq C_{\rm tr}(\Delta+1)^3h$,
proving (i).
<!-- end proof-9 -->
<!-- proof-10: body_md -->
With $U=GH^\dagger$, unitary invariance gives
\[
    \|P_G(k)P_H(k)\|_{\mathrm{op}}
       =\|\Pi_{\leq k}U\Pi_{\leq k}\|_{\mathrm{op}}.
\]
Suppose this is greater than $\mu$, and choose unit vectors
$\psi,\chi\in\operatorname{Im}(\Pi_{\leq k})$ with
$|\langle\psi,U\chi\rangle|>\mu$.
Write $(\mathbf d,C)$ for the moments of $U\ket0$ and $r_j$ for its
squeezes. We first bound the largest squeeze, then use the 
definition of $\Delta(G,H)$ to control the total compatibility cost.

For every real vector $x$ and every unit vector
$\varphi\in\operatorname{Im}(\Pi_{\leq k})$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lfpr-quadrature-moments}
    \langle\varphi,(x^\top\hat{\mathbf R})^2\varphi\rangle
       \lesssim k\|x\|_2^2.
\end{equation}
Indeed, a passive transformation sends
$x^\top\hat{\mathbf R}$ to $\|x\|_2\hat q_1$ and preserves the cutoff;
use $\hat q_1=(\hat{a}_1+\hat{a}_1^\dagger)/\sqrt2$ and
$\|\hat q_1\Pi_{\leq k}\|_{\mathrm{op}}
\lesssim\sqrt k$, since $k\geq1$.
We also need the following density bound: every unit quadrature has
probability density at most $C_H\sqrt k$ on a cutoff-$k$ state,
for a universal $C_H$. After a passive rotation and partial trace, its
density is at most $\sum_{j=0}^k|h_j(t)|^2$, where $h_j$ are normalized
Hermite functions. Mehler's identity gives, for $0<v<1$,
\[
    \sum_{j=0}^k|h_j(t)|^2
    \leq\frac{v^{-k}}{\sqrt{\pi(1-v^2)}}
        \exp\left(-\frac{1-v}{1+v}t^2\right).
\]
Taking $v=e^{-1/k}$ proves the bound. Scaling the quadrature by $s$
divides the density bound by $s$, and translation leaves it unchanged.

Let $s=e^{r_{\max}}$ and measure a unit quadrature corresponding to the
largest eigenvalue $s^2$ of $C$. Its densities $p,q$ in $\psi,U\chi$
satisfy $\int t^2p(t)\,dt\lesssim k$ and
$\|q\|_\infty\lesssim\sqrt k/s$.
Take $R=C_0\sqrt k/\mu$ for a sufficiently large universal $C_0$,
and let $I_R=[-R,R]$. Then
\[
    p(I_R^c)\leq c\mu^2,
    \qquad q(I_R)\lesssim k/(\mu s),
\]
where $c$ is a sufficiently small universal constant.
Fidelity monotonicity under this measurement and Cauchy--Schwarz give
\[
    \mu<|\langle\psi,U\chi\rangle|
       \leq\int\sqrt{pq}
       \leq\sqrt{q(I_R)}+\sqrt{p(I_R^c)}.
\]
For $s$ larger than a sufficiently large universal multiple of
$k\mu^{-3}$, the right-hand side is at most $\mu$, a contradiction. Thus
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lfpr-largest-squeeze}
    e^{r_{\max}}\lesssim k\mu^{-3}.
\end{equation}


Now set $P=\Pi_{\leq k}$, $B=UNU^\dagger$, $Q=UPU^\dagger$, and
$\Delta=\Delta(G,H)$. By the original definition,
$N+B\succeq\Delta I$. Since $P,Q$ have finite rank, there are unit vectors
$\phi\in\operatorname{Im}P$, $\eta\in\operatorname{Im}Q$ such that
\[
    P\eta=t\phi,\qquad Q\phi=t\eta,\qquad
    t=\|PQ\|_{\mathrm{op}}=\|PUP\|_{\mathrm{op}}>\mu.
\]
The operator $B$ commutes with $Q$, so
$\langle\phi,B\eta\rangle=t\langle\eta,B\eta\rangle$.
Expanding $\eta=t\phi+(I-P)\eta$ therefore gives
\[
    t\langle\phi,B\phi\rangle
       =t\langle\eta,B\eta\rangle
          -\langle(I-P)B\phi,(I-P)\eta\rangle.
\]
Since $\langle\phi,N\phi\rangle\leq k$ and
$\langle\eta,B\eta\rangle\leq k$, we obtain
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lfpr-variational-cutoff}
    \Delta\leq\langle\phi,(N+B)\phi\rangle
       \leq2k+\frac{\|(I-P)BP\|_{\mathrm{op}}}{t}
       \leq2k+\frac{\|(I-P)BP\|_{\mathrm{op}}}{\mu}.
\end{equation}
Thus it suffices to bound the part of $B$ that crosses the photon cutoff.

Write the quadratic Hamiltonian as
\[
    B=\hat{a}^\dagger M \hat{a}+\frac12
       \bigl(\hat{a}^{\dagger\top}\beta \hat{a}^\dagger+\hat{a}^\top\overline\beta \hat{a}\bigr)
       +\hat{a}^\dagger\ell+\ell^\dagger \hat{a}+c_0I,
       \qquad M=M^\dagger,\quad\beta=\beta^\top.
\]
Only the terms creating one or two photons contribute to $(I-P)BP$.
For $v$ supported on at most $k+2$ photons, Cauchy--Schwarz gives
\[
    \left\|\frac12\hat{a}^\top\overline\beta \hat{a}\,v\right\|_2
       \leq\frac12\|\beta\|_F
          \left(\sum_{i,j}\|\hat{a}_i \hat{a}_jv\|_2^2\right)^{1/2}
       \lesssim k\|\beta\|_F\|v\|_2,
\]
where the sum equals $\langle v,N(N-1)v\rangle$ and $k\geq1$.
Taking adjoints bounds the pair-creation term on $\operatorname{Im}P$;
the one-photon term satisfies
$\|\hat{a}^\dagger\ell P\|_{\mathrm{op}}\lesssim\sqrt k\|\ell\|_2$.
Consequently,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lfpr-quadratic-cutoff}
    \|(I-P)BP\|_{\mathrm{op}}
       \lesssim k\|\beta\|_F+\sqrt k\|\ell\|_2.
\end{equation}

Expanding
$B=\tfrac12(\hat{\mathbf R}-\mathbf d)^\top C^{-1}
(\hat{\mathbf R}-\mathbf d)-nI/2$ in ladder operators gives
\[
    \|\beta\|_F^2=\sum_j\sinh^2(2r_j),\qquad
    \|\ell\|_2^2=\frac12\mathbf d^\top C^{-2}\mathbf d.
\]
For the first identity, a passive change of modes diagonalizes $C$;
the pair coefficient in mode $j$ then has magnitude $\sinh(2r_j)$.
The second follows from the linear term
$-\mathbf d^\top C^{-1}\hat{\mathbf R}$.
By \eqref{eq:lfpr-compatibility-formula} and
$s^{-2}I\preceq C\preceq s^2I$,
\begin{align*}
    \|\beta\|_F^2
       &=4\sum_j(\cosh r_j-1)(\cosh r_j+1)\cosh^2r_j
         \lesssim s^3\Delta,\\
    \|\ell\|_2^2
       &\leq\frac{s^4+s^2}{2}
          \mathbf d^\top(I+C)^{-1}\mathbf d
         \lesssim s^4\Delta.
\end{align*}
Substituting into \eqref{eq:lfpr-quadratic-cutoff} and then
\eqref{eq:lfpr-variational-cutoff} yields
\[
    \Delta\leq2k+\frac{C_*}{\mu}
       \bigl(ks^{3/2}+\sqrt k\,s^2\bigr)\sqrt\Delta
\]
for a universal constant $C_*$. Young's inequality and
\eqref{eq:lfpr-largest-squeeze} now give
\[
    \Delta\lesssim k+\mu^{-2}(k^2s^3+ks^4)
       \lesssim k^5\mu^{-14}\leq(k/\mu)^{32}.
\]
Choosing $C_{\rm sep}$ sufficiently large proves (ii) by contradiction.
<!-- end proof-10 -->
<!-- lemma-15: body_md -->
\label{lemma:lfpr-packing}
Let $P_1,\ldots,P_s$ be orthogonal projections satisfying
$\tr(\rho P_i)\geq w$ and
$\|P_iP_j\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\mu$ for $i\ne j$, where $w>\mu$.
Then
\[
    s\leq\frac{1-\mu}{w-\mu}.
\]
<!-- end lemma-15 -->
<!-- proof-11: body_md -->
For $x_i\in\operatorname{Im}(P_i)$,
$|\langle x_i,x_j\rangle|\leq\mu\|x_i\|_2\|x_j\|_2$, so
\[
    \left\|\sum_i x_i\right\|_2^2
       \leq(1+(s-1)\mu)\sum_i\|x_i\|_2^2.
\]
The map $(x_1,\ldots,x_s)\mapsto\sum_i x_i$ thus has squared norm at
most $1+(s-1)\mu$. Its product with its adjoint is $\sum_iP_i$, giving
\[
    sw\leq\tr\left(\rho\sum_iP_i\right)
       \leq\left\|\sum_iP_i\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}
       \leq1+(s-1)\mu.
\]
This proves the lemma.
<!-- end proof-11 -->
<!-- lemma-16: body_md -->
\label{lemma:lfpr-heavy}
If $F(\rho,\pi_*)\geq\alpha$ and $\nu_G(\pi_*)\leq h$ for $h\geq1$, then
one may take $C_0=64$ in the bound
\[
    k\geq C_0h/\alpha
    \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
    \tr(\rho P_G(k))\geq3\alpha/4.
\]
<!-- end lemma-16 -->
<!-- proof-12: body_md -->
Write $\pi_*=\ketbra{\psi}$ and $P=P_G(k)$.
Markov's inequality gives
$\zeta=\|(I-P)\psi\|_2^2\leq h/k\leq c\alpha$,
where $c=C_0^{-1}=1/64$.
Since $0\preceq\rho\preceq I$,
\[
    \sqrt\alpha\leq\|\sqrt\rho\psi\|_2
    \leq\|\sqrt\rho P\psi\|_2+\|\sqrt\rho(I-P)\psi\|_2
    \leq\sqrt{\tr(\rho P)}+\sqrt\zeta.
\]
Here the squared first term is at most $\tr(P\rho P)$, and the second
is at most $\zeta$. Consequently
$\tr(\rho P)\geq(\sqrt\alpha-\sqrt\zeta)^2
\geq(1-\sqrt c)^2\alpha=49\alpha/64\geq3\alpha/4$.
<!-- end proof-12 -->
<!-- lemma-17: body_md -->
\label{lemma:lfpr-block}
Suppose $F(\rho,\pi_*)\geq\alpha$ and the input list contains a basis in which the best Gaussian approximator $\pi_*$ has squeezing at most $A$. If the estimation lists contain good pairs and the cutoff-mass estimates in Algorithm~\ref{alg:lfpr-block} are accurate, the next list has at most $8/(3\alpha)$ bases and contains a basis $J$ with
\[
    \nu_J(\pi_*)\leq h^+(A),\qquad
    \kappa(V_J)\leq A^+(A).
\]
<!-- end lemma-17 -->
<!-- proof-13: body_md -->
Follow a good list element at each of the $q$ levels. Equation~\eqref{eq:lfpr-block-unsqueeze}, obtained from Lemma~\ref{lemma:williamson-unsqueezing}, bounds $\pi_*$ squeezing at the resulting leaf by $B(A)$. A good terminal pair and Lemma~\ref{lemma:lfpr-warm-frame} then give a candidate $H_*$ with $\nu_{H_*}(\pi_*)\leq h(A)$.

By Lemma~\ref{lemma:lfpr-heavy}, its true cutoff mass is at least $3\alpha/4$, and its estimated mass is therefore at least $3\alpha/4-\alpha/16>\alpha/2$. It survives the mass test. If retained, it already has the required photon bound. If discarded during merging, some retained $J$ satisfies $\Delta(H_*,J)\leq T(A)$, and Proposition~\ref{prop:lfpr-frame-comparison} gives $\nu_J(\pi_*)\leq h^+(A)$. Equation~\eqref{eq:lfpr-energy-condition} gives the squeezing bound.
                                                                  

Every retained basis has true cutoff mass at least $w=7\alpha/16$, and distinct retained bases have projector overlap at most $\mu=\alpha/16$. Lemma~\ref{lemma:lfpr-packing} bounds their number by
\[
    \frac{1-\mu}{w-\mu}\leq\frac8{3\alpha}.
\]
Thus the failure condition is never triggered on the stated success event.
<!-- end proof-13 -->
<!-- proof-14: body_md -->
We analyze Algorithm~\ref{alg:lfpr-unsqueezing} in four steps: we count the random subroutine calls, show that with probability at least $1-\eta$ all of them succeed, follow a useful basis through the blocks on that event, and bound the cost.

\emph{Counting calls.} Each union of $b$ lists has at most $L_\alpha b$ pairs, so every basis has at most $L_\alpha b$ updated bases. Starting from at most $W$ bases, round $j$ of a block holds at most $W(L_\alpha b)^j$ bases. A block computes one union of lists for each basis in rounds $0,\ldots,q$, at most $(q+1)W(L_\alpha b)^q=12W(L_\alpha b)^{11}$ in total, and one cutoff-mass estimate for each of the at most $W(L_\alpha b)^{12}$ candidates. Over $R$ blocks, the total number of unions and mass estimates is therefore at most $13RW(L_\alpha b)^{12}=N_\alpha Rb^{12}=J$, including on runs that stop early with failure.

\emph{All calls succeed.} By Lemma~\ref{lemma:lfpr-list}, each union of $b$ lists fails to contain a good pair with probability at most $10^{-b}\leq e^{-b}$, and each mass estimate fails with probability at most $\tau=\eta/(2J)$. Both bounds hold conditionally on all earlier observations, because every call uses fresh copies, so a union bound over the at most $J$ calls applies. Our choice of $b$ gives $12\log b\leq b/2$, and therefore
\[
    Je^{-b}=N_\alpha Rb^{12}e^{-b}
       \leq N_\alpha Re^{-b/2}\leq\eta/2.
\]
So with probability at least $1-\eta$, every union of lists contains a good pair and every mass estimate is accurate.

\emph{Following a useful basis.} Condition on this event. The Fock basis $G=I$ is useful at level $A_0$. By Lemma~\ref{lemma:lfpr-block} and induction, every list $\mathcal L_r$ contains a basis useful at level $A_r$, and the algorithm never reports failure. It remains to bound $A_R$. Write $p=8$ and $d=32$ for the exponents in Lemma~\ref{lemma:lfpr-warm-frame} and Proposition~\ref{prop:lfpr-frame-comparison}, and set $v_\alpha=2^pu_\alpha\Lambda^{2p}\asymp t_\alpha^{2p+6}$. Substituting $B(A)=2\Lambda^2A^{2^{-q}}$ into $h_\alpha$ gives $h(A)=v_\alpha A^{p2^{-q}}$. Since $k(A)\lesssim h(A)/\alpha$ and $\mu\asymp\alpha$, we have $T(A)\lesssim\alpha^{-2d}h(A)^d$. Since $A^+(A)=48C_{\rm tr}T(A)^3h(A)$, we obtain, for a sufficiently large universal $C$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lfpr-contraction-constant}
    \mathsf K_\alpha
      =C\alpha^{-6d}v_\alpha^{3d+1}
      \asymp\alpha^{-s/2},\qquad s\asymp r_{\rm dec},
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lfpr-root-contraction}
    A^+(A)\leq\mathsf K_\alpha A^{p(3d+1)2^{-q}}
       \leq\mathsf K_\alpha\sqrt A.
\end{equation}
Here $s\asymp r_{\rm dec}$ because $p$ and $d$ are universal and $t_\alpha\asymp\alpha^{-r_{\rm dec}}$. The last inequality uses $p(3d+1)2^{-q}=776/2048\leq1/2$; this is why we take $q=11$, the smallest value for which it holds. Taking logarithms, each block maps $\log A$ to at most $\frac12\log A+\log\mathsf K_\alpha$, so
\[
    \log A_r\leq2^{-r}\log A_0+2\log\mathsf K_\alpha.
\]
By \eqref{eq:lfpr-round-count}, $2^{-R}\log A_0\leq1$, so $A_R\leq H_\alpha$ with
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lfpr-terminal-constant}
    H_\alpha=e\mathsf K_\alpha^2
      \asymp\alpha^{-s},\qquad s\asymp r_{\rm dec}.
\end{equation}
Since $R\geq1$, the last block also gives a kept basis $G$ with
\[
    \nu_G(\pi_*)\leq h^+(A_{R-1})\leq A_R\leq H_\alpha.
\]
This proves \eqref{eq:lfpr-final-frame} and the success probability.

\emph{Cost.} Each union of lists uses at most $b\lceil e^{t_\alpha}(3n)^{t_\alpha}\rceil$ copies and $b$ decoder calls. Each mass estimate uses $O(\alpha^{-2}\log(J/\eta))$ copies, independently of its photon cutoff. The remaining computations, namely Williamson decompositions, projections onto $\mathcal K_A$, and evaluations of $\Delta$ by Lemma~\ref{lemma:lfpr-compatibility}, take polynomially many arithmetic operations in $n$ per candidate, and each candidate is compared with at most $W$ kept bases. Enlarging $C_{\rm dec}$ if necessary, we may assume that $t_\alpha$ exceeds the degree of these polynomials. 
                                                                                                                                                                                                          
Since $L_\alpha=\lceil e^{t_\alpha}\rceil$, we have $N_\alpha\leq\exp(Ct_\alpha)$ and $b\leq Ct_\alpha\log(R/\eta)$. Multiplying these costs by the call bound $J=N_\alpha Rb^{12}$ shows that the number of copies, the number of decoder calls, and the number of arithmetic operations outside the decoder are each at most
\[
    \exp(Ct_\alpha)n^{t_\alpha}R\bigl(\log(R/\eta)\bigr)^{13}.
\]
Since $R\lesssim\log\log(e+A_0)$, this proves \eqref{eq:lfpr-preprocessing-cost}.
<!-- end proof-14 -->
<!-- theorem-12: body_md -->
\label{thm:lf-bounded-energy}
Let $\sigma$ be an $n$-mode state, $h\geq1$, $\epsilon \in(0,1/2]$, and $\eta\in(0,1/2]$. There is an algorithm which outputs a pure Gaussian state $\hat\pi$ satisfying
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lf-optimizer-guarantee}
    F(\sigma,\hat\pi)\geq\sup_{\pi\in\mathcal{G}:\nu(\pi)\leq h}F(\sigma,\pi)-\epsilon
\end{equation}
with probability at least $1-\eta$, using at most
                                                                                                  
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lf-optimizer-cost}
    C_{h,\epsilon}n^{O(h^3+h\log(1/\epsilon))}\log(2/\eta)
\end{equation}
copies and runtime.
                                                                                                                                                                
Here $C_{h,\epsilon}$ is a constant depends only on $h$ and $\epsilon$.
                                                                                           
                                                                 
Moreover, the output has mean photon number $\nu(\hat\pi)$ at most $17(h+1)^3$.
<!-- end theorem-12 -->
<!-- lemma-18: body_md -->
\label{lemma:lf-energy-domain}
For $h\geq1$, the set $\mathcal{D}_h$ in \eqref{eq:lf-parameter-domain} is convex and consists of valid pure Gaussian parameters. Every pure Gaussian state $\pi_{b,A}$ with $\nu(\pi_{b,A})\leq h$ has $(b,A)\in\mathcal{D}_h$. Conversely, every $(b,A)\in\mathcal{D}_h$ satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lf-domain-photon-bound}
    \nu(\pi_{b,A})\leq h(h+1)+16h(h+1)^2\leq17(h+1)^3.
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-18 -->
<!-- lemma-19: body_md -->
\label{lemma:lf-log-normalization}
The function $L(b,A)=\log Z(b,A)$ is smooth and convex on the real convex domain $\{(b,A)\in\mathcal P_n:\|A\|_{\rm op}<1\}$. For every $(b,A)\in\mathcal{D}_h$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lf-normalization-bounds}
\begin{split}
    0\leq L(b,A)&\leq B_h\equiv17(h+1)^2,\\
    \|\nabla L(b,A)\|_{\mathcal P}&\leq G_h\equiv
       \frac{6\sqrt h}{1-r_h}+\frac{8h}{(1-r_h)^2}.
\end{split}
\end{equation}
Here $B_h\asymp(h+1)^2$ and $G_h\asymp(h+1)^3$ for $h\geq1$.
The gradient uses the real inner product on $\mathcal P_n$, whose norm is $\|(v_b,v_A)\|_{\mathcal P}^2=\|v_b\|_2^2+\tfrac12\|v_A\|_F^2$.
<!-- end lemma-19 -->
<!-- proof-15: body_md -->
\emph{Convexity.} Think of \eqref{eq:lf-normalization-integral} as integration against the measure $d\mu(z)=\pi^{-n}e^{-\|z\|_2^2}d^{2n}z$ and write the exponent in the integral as as $\ell_\theta(z)=2\Re(b^\top z)+\Re(z^\top Az)$, where $\theta=(b,A)$. This is real-linear in $\theta$. For $0<t<1$, H\"older's inequality with exponents $1/(1-t)$ and $1/t$ gives
\[
    Z((1-t)\theta+t\theta')
       =\int (e^{\ell_\theta})^{1-t}(e^{\ell_{\theta'}})^t\,d\mu
       \leq Z(\theta)^{1-t}Z(\theta')^t.
\]
Convexity follows immediately. Smoothness follows from \eqref{eq:lf-normalization-formula}, since $M(A)$ is positive definite when $\|A\|_{\rm op}<1$.

\emph{Value bound.} Let $r=\|A\|_{\rm op}<1$. Lemma~\ref{lemma:lf-normalization-formula} implies that
\[
    L(b,A)=-\frac12\sum_j\log(1-s_j^2)+u^\top M^{-1}u.
\]
Both terms are nonnegative. Using $-\log(1-x)\leq x/(1-x)$ for $0\leq x<1$, $\|u\|_2=\|b\|_2$, and $\|M^{-1}\|_{\rm op}=(1-r)^{-1}$, we obtain
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lf-normalization-upper}
    0\leq L(b,A)\leq
       \frac{\|A\|_F^2}{2(1-r^2)}+\frac{\|b\|_2^2}{1-r}.
\end{equation}
On $\mathcal{D}_h$, recall that $1/(1-r_h^2)=h+1$ and $1/(1-r_h)=(1+r_h)(h+1)\leq2(h+1)$ to bound the RHS by
\[
    \frac12h(h+1)+8h(h+1)\leq17(h+1)^2.
\]

\emph{Gradient bound.} We bound the directional derivative in an arbitrary real direction $v=(v_b,v_A)$ with $\|v\|_{\mathcal P}=1$. Set
\[
    H=DM(A)[v_A]=\begin{pmatrix}-\Re v_A&\Im v_A\\ \Im v_A&\Re v_A\end{pmatrix},
    \qquad q=Du(b)[v_b]=\begin{pmatrix}\Re v_b\\-\Im v_b\end{pmatrix}.
\]
The block entries and the definition of the parameter norm imply
\[
    \tr H=0,\qquad
    \|H\|_F=\sqrt2\|v_A\|_F\leq2,\qquad
    \|q\|_2=\|v_b\|_2\leq1.
\]
Differentiating \eqref{eq:lf-normalization-formula}, using $D(M^{-1})[H]=-M^{-1}HM^{-1}$, gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lf-normalization-derivative}
    DL(b,A)[v]=
       -\tfrac12\tr(M^{-1}H)+2q^\top M^{-1}u
       -u^\top M^{-1}HM^{-1}u.
\end{equation}
Write $\delta=1-r_h>0$. On $\mathcal{D}_h$, $\|M^{-1}\|_{\rm op}\leq\delta^{-1}$ and $\|u\|_2\leq2\sqrt h$. To bound the trace term without introducing a factor depending on $n$, use $\tr H=0$ to replace $M^{-1}$ by $M^{-1}-I$. Since the block formula for $M$ gives $\|I-M\|_F=\sqrt2\|A\|_F$,
\[
    \|M^{-1}-I\|_F=\|M^{-1}(I-M)\|_F
       \leq\frac{\sqrt{2h}}{\delta}.
\]
The Frobenius Cauchy--Schwarz inequality now yields
\[
    \tfrac12|\tr(M^{-1}H)|
       =\tfrac12|\tr((M^{-1}-I)H)|
       \leq\tfrac12\|M^{-1}-I\|_F\|H\|_F
       \leq\frac{\sqrt{2h}}{\delta}.
\]
The other two terms in \eqref{eq:lf-normalization-derivative} satisfy
\begin{align*}
    |2q^\top M^{-1}u|&\leq2\|q\|_2\|M^{-1}\|_{\rm op}\|u\|_2
       \leq\frac{4\sqrt h}{\delta},\\
    |u^\top M^{-1}HM^{-1}u|&\leq\|H\|_{\rm op}\|M^{-1}u\|_2^2
       \leq\frac{8h}{\delta^2},
\end{align*}
where $\|H\|_{\rm op}\leq\|H\|_F\leq2$. Adding these bounds and using $\sqrt2+4\leq6$ gives $|DL(b,A)[v]|\leq G_h$. Finally, $\|\nabla L(b,A)\|_{\mathcal P}=\sup_{\|v\|_{\mathcal P}=1}|DL(b,A)[v]|$, proving the gradient bound.
<!-- end proof-15 -->
<!-- lemma-20: body_md -->
\label{lemma:lf-uniform-cutoff}
Let $h\geq1$ and $0<\epsilon\leq1/2$. With $B_h=17(h+1)^2$ as in Lemma~\ref{lemma:lf-log-normalization}, set
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lf-cutoff-choice}
    s_h=\frac14\log(1+1/h),\qquad
    K=K_{h,\epsilon}=\left\lceil\frac{B_h+2\log(64/\epsilon)}{s_h}\right\rceil.
\end{equation}
Thus $s_h\asymp(h+1)^{-1}$ and $K=K_{h,\epsilon}\asymp(h+1)^3+(h+1)\log(1/\epsilon)$. For every $\theta=(b,A)\in\mathcal{D}_h$, writing $\ket{\psi_\theta}=\ket{\psi(b,A)}$ and $\pi_\theta=\ketbra{\psi_\theta}$, we have
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lf-cutoff-tail}
    \|(I-P_K)\ket{\psi_\theta}\|^2\leq(\epsilon/64)^2.
\end{equation}
Consequently, for every density operator $\sigma$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lf-cutoff-error}
    \left|F(\sigma,\pi_\theta)
       -\bra{\psi_\theta}P_K\sigma P_K\ket{\psi_\theta}\right|
       \leq\epsilon/16.
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-20 -->
<!-- proof-16: body_md -->
Our goal will be to prove
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lf-photon-exponential-moment}
    \bra{\psi_\theta}e^{s_hN}\ket{\psi_\theta}\leq e^{B_h}
    \qquad(\theta\in\mathcal{D}_h).
\end{equation}
We first deduce the lemma from this inequality, then prove the inequality.

\emph{Proof of lemma using \eqref{eq:lf-photon-exponential-moment}}
Since $N$ has nonnegative integer eigenvalues, $I-P_K\preceq e^{-s_h(K+1)}e^{s_hN}$. Thus
\[
    t_\theta\equiv\|(I-P_K)\ket{\psi_\theta}\|^2
       \leq e^{-s_h(K+1)}\bra{\psi_\theta}e^{s_hN}\ket{\psi_\theta}
       \leq e^{B_h-s_h(K+1)}\leq(\epsilon/64)^2.
\]
To prove \eqref{eq:lf-cutoff-error}, let $\ket\phi=P_K\ket{\psi_\theta}$ and $\ket\chi=(I-P_K)\ket{\psi_\theta}$. Then
\[
    \bra{\psi_\theta}\sigma\ket{\psi_\theta}-\bra\phi\sigma\ket\phi
       =\bra\chi\sigma\ket{\psi_\theta}+\bra\phi\sigma\ket\chi.
\]
The magnitude of this is at most $\epsilon/16$.

\emph{Proof of \eqref{eq:lf-photon-exponential-moment}}
Multiplying each $m$-photon coefficient by $e^{sm/2}$ corresponds in Bargmann space to replacing $z$ by $e^{s/2}z$. Applied to $e^{b^\top z+z^\top Az/2}$, this replaces $(b,A)$ by $(e^{s/2}b,e^sA)$. Taking squared norms therefore gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lf-photon-mgf}
    \bra{\psi(b,A)}e^{sN}\ket{\psi(b,A)}
       =\frac{Z(e^{s/2}b,e^sA)}{Z(b,A)},
       \qquad e^s\|A\|_{\rm op}<1.
\end{equation}
The condition ensures that the scaled Bargmann function is square integrable, so this also justifies finiteness of the expectation.

Write $r=r_h=\sqrt{h/(h+1)}$ and take $s=s_h=-\tfrac12\log r$. For $\tilde b=e^{s/2}b$ and $\tilde A=e^sA$, the domain constraints give
\[
    \|\tilde A\|_{\rm op}\leq\sqrt r<1,\qquad
    \|\tilde A\|_F^2\leq h/r\leq h+1,\qquad
    \|\tilde b\|_2^2\leq4h/\sqrt r\leq4(h+1).
\]
Also $(1-r)^{-1}=(1+r)(h+1)\leq2(h+1)$ and $(1-\sqrt r)^{-1}=(1+\sqrt r)/(1-r)\leq4(h+1)$. The general normalization bound \eqref{eq:lf-normalization-upper}, which does not require the scaled parameters to lie in $\mathcal{D}_h$, now yields
\[
    \log Z(\tilde b,\tilde A)
       \leq\frac{h+1}{2(1-r)}+\frac{4(h+1)}{1-\sqrt r}
       \leq17(h+1)^2=B_h.
\]
Since $Z(b,A)\geq1$, we are done.
<!-- end proof-16 -->
<!-- lemma-21: body_md -->
\label{lemma:lf-cutoff-tomography}
Let $K\geq0$ be an integer, $0<\epsilon\leq1/2$, and $0<\eta\leq1/2$. Given copies of an arbitrary state $\sigma$, measurements on individual copies suffice to construct a matrix $\hat\sigma_K$ supported on the cutoff space such that, with probability at least $1-\eta$,
\[
    \hat\sigma_K\succeq0,\qquad \tr\hat\sigma_K\leq1,\qquad
    \|\hat\sigma_K-P_K\sigma P_K\|_{\rm op}\leq\epsilon/16.
\]
The number of copies is $O(D_K^4\epsilon^{-2}\log(2D_K/\eta))$.
<!-- end lemma-21 -->
<!-- theorem-13: body_md -->
\label{thm:lf-normalized-optimizer}
Fix $h\geq1$, an integer $K\geq1$, and $0<\delta\leq1/2$. Given a matrix $\hat\sigma_K\succeq0$ supported on $P_K\mathcal F_n$ with $\tr\hat\sigma_K\leq1$, there exists a deterministic algorithm that returns $\hat\theta\in\mathcal{D}_h$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lf-normalized-optimizer}
    f(\hat\theta)\geq\max_{\theta\in\mathcal{D}_h}f(\theta)-\delta.
\end{equation}
It uses at most $C_{h,K,\delta}(n+1)^{cK}$ real-arithmetic operations, for a universal constant $c$. One sufficient choice of the prefactor is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lf-normalized-cost-constant}
\begin{split}
    \Gamma&=(1+5h)^K(K+1)^4K!,\\
    C_{h,K,\delta}
       &=\exp\!\left[
          C(K+1)^6\frac{\Gamma^2}{\delta^2}
          \log\!\left(\frac{C(h+1)(K+1)\Gamma}{\delta}\right)
          \right],
\end{split}
\end{equation}
for a sufficiently large universal $C$. These are sufficient,
nonoptimized choices for the constants in the stated algorithm.
<!-- end theorem-13 -->
<!-- theorem-14: body_md -->
\label{thm:lf-tensor-reduction}
Let $H=\mathbb{C}^n$ and let $\operatorname{Sym}^2H$ be the symmetric subspace of $H^{\otimes2}$ with its inherited norm. Identify a symmetric matrix with its coefficient tensor, so its tensor norm is its Frobenius norm. Put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lf-tensor-scaling}
\begin{split}
    \mathcal X&=\mathbb{C}\oplus H\oplus\operatorname{Sym}^2H,
    \qquad R_h=\sqrt{1+5h},\\
    y(b,A)&=\frac{(1,b,A/\sqrt2)}{R_h},
    \qquad \Gamma=R_h^{2K}(K+1)^4K!.
\end{split}
\end{equation}
From $\hat\sigma_K$ one can construct a positive semidefinite operator $B$ on $\mathcal X^{\otimes K}$ with $\|B\|_F\leq\Gamma$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lf-tensor-numerator}
    p(\theta)=g(y(\theta)),\qquad
    g(v)=\langle v^{\otimes K},Bv^{\otimes K}\rangle.
\end{equation}
For $\theta,\theta'\in\mathcal{D}_h$,
\[
    \|y(\theta)\|_2\leq1,\qquad
    \|y(\theta)-y(\theta')\|_2=\frac{\|\theta-\theta'\|_{\mathcal P}}{R_h}.
\]
The polynomial $g$ is $2K\Gamma$-Lipschitz on the unit ball of $\mathcal X$. For every $0<\xi<\Gamma$, one can also construct a complex subspace $W\subseteq\mathcal X$ satisfying
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lf-effective-coordinates}
    \dim_{\mathbb{C}}W\leq8(K+1)^6(\Gamma/\xi)^2,\qquad
    \sup_{\|v\|_2\leq1}|g(v)-g(P_Wv)|\leq\xi.
\end{equation}
The constructions cost at most $C(K+1)^C K!(n+1)^{cK}$ arithmetic
operations for universal $C,c$, counting spectral factorizations in the
real-arithmetic model. The accuracy $\xi$ sets the singular-value
threshold and the dimension bound in \eqref{eq:lf-effective-coordinates};
it introduces no further factor in this construction cost.
<!-- end theorem-14 -->
<!-- lemma-22: body_md -->
\label{lemma:lf-ratio-search}
Set
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lf-penalty-parameters}
    t=\frac{\xi}{3\Lambda},\qquad \lambda=\frac{B_h+1}{t},\qquad
    J_z(\theta)=\ell(\theta)+\lambda[\|P_Wy(\theta)-z\|_2-t]_+.
\end{equation}
Let $\mathcal N$ be a finite $t$-net of the unit ball of $W$. For each $z\in\mathcal N$, let $\theta_z\in\mathcal{D}_h$ be such that
\[
    J_z(\theta_z)\leq\min_{\theta\in\mathcal{D}_h}J_z(\theta)+\zeta,
    \qquad 0<\zeta\leq1.
\]
Let
\[
    \hat z\in\operatorname*{argmax}_{z\in\mathcal N}f(\theta_z),
    \qquad \hat\theta=\theta_{\hat z}.
\]
Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lf-ratio-search-error}
    f(\hat\theta)\geq\max_{\theta\in\mathcal{D}_h}f(\theta)
       -(3\xi+\zeta).
\end{equation}
Moreover, each $J_z$ is convex on $\mathcal{D}_h$.
<!-- end lemma-22 -->
<!-- proof-17: body_md -->
The domain $\mathcal{D}_h$ is compact and $f$ is continuous, so it has a maximizer $\theta^*$. Choose $z\in\mathcal N$ with $\|P_Wy(\theta^*)-z\|_2\leq t$. This makes the penalty at $\theta^*$ zero, and hence
\[
    J_z(\theta_z)\leq J_z(\theta^*)+\zeta
       =\ell(\theta^*)+\zeta\leq B_h+1.
\]
Both terms of $J_z$ are nonnegative. Bounding each of them inidividually by $B_h+1$ yields
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lf-cell-controls}
    \ell(\theta_z)\leq\ell(\theta^*)+\zeta,\qquad
    \|P_Wy(\theta_z)-z\|_2\leq t+\frac{B_h+1}{\lambda}=2t.
\end{equation}
The projected coordinates of $\theta_z$ and $\theta^*$ are therefore at distance at most $3t$. Applying \eqref{eq:lf-effective-coordinates} at both points, followed by the Lipschitz bound for $g$, yields
\[
    |p(\theta_z)-p(\theta^*)|\leq2\xi+3\Lambda t=3\xi.
\]
Since $p\geq0$, the denominator bound in \eqref{eq:lf-cell-controls} implies
\begin{align*}
    f(\theta_z)&\geq e^{-\ell(\theta^*)-\zeta}p(\theta_z)\\
       &\geq e^{-\zeta}f(\theta^*)-3\xi e^{-\ell(\theta^*)-\zeta}
        \geq f(\theta^*)-\zeta-3\xi.
\end{align*}
Here we used $0\leq f\leq1$, $\ell\geq0$, and $1-e^{-\zeta}\leq\zeta$. By the choice of $\hat z$, $f(\hat\theta)\geq f(\theta_z)$, proving \eqref{eq:lf-ratio-search-error}.

$J_z$ is a sum of convex functions, so it is convex.
<!-- end proof-17 -->
<!-- fact-4: body_md -->
\label{fact:lf-convex-minimization}
Let $\mathcal H$ be a finite-dimensional real Hilbert space with inner product $\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathcal H}$ and induced norm $\|x\|_{\mathcal H}=\sqrt{\langle x,x\rangle_{\mathcal H}}$. Let $D\subseteq\mathcal H$ be compact and convex, with $0\in D$ and $\|\theta\|_{\mathcal H}\leq D_0$ on $D$, where $D_0>0$. Let $J:D\to\mathbb{R}$ be continuous and convex. Suppose an oracle returns, at every $\theta\in D$, a subgradient $v\in\partial J(\theta)$ with $\|v\|_{\mathcal H}\leq G$, where $G>0$; that is,
\[
    J(\theta')\geq J(\theta)+\langle v,\theta'-\theta\rangle_{\mathcal H}
    \qquad\text{for every }\theta'\in D.
\]
Suppose we also have an oracle for orthogonal projection onto $D$ in this Hilbert-space norm:
\[
    \operatorname{proj}_D(x)
       =\operatorname*{argmin}_{y\in D}\|x-y\|_{\mathcal H}^2.
\]
Given an accuracy $\zeta>0$, let $T=\max\{1,\lceil(D_0G/\zeta)^2\rceil\}, \alpha=\frac{D_0}{G\sqrt T}$.
Now consider the following algorithm. Initialize $\theta_0=0$, and consider the iterates $\theta_{j+1}=\operatorname{proj}_D(\theta_j-\alpha v_j)$, where $v_j$ is returned by the subgradient oracle. Then $\bar\theta=T^{-1}\sum_{j=0}^{T-1}\theta_j\in D$ satisfies
\[
    J(\bar\theta)\leq\min_DJ+\frac{D_0G}{\sqrt T}\leq\min_DJ+\zeta.
\]
Clearly, this algorithm requires $T$ subgradient and $T$ projection oracle queries.
<!-- end fact-4 -->
<!-- proposition-7: body_md -->
\label{prop:lf-convex-oracles}
For every net point $z$, the function $J_z$ in \eqref{eq:lf-penalty-parameters} has a subgradient oracle on $\mathcal{D}_h$ with norm bound $G_h+\lambda/R_h$. Projection onto $\mathcal{D}_h$ in the parameter norm is also computable. Both operations have polynomial arithmetic cost in $n$ and $\dim_{\mathbb{C}}W$.
<!-- end proposition-7 -->
<!-- proof-18: body_md -->
We analyze Algorithm~\ref{alg:lf-normalized-overlap}. With its choices $\xi=\delta/6$ and $\zeta=\delta/2$, Lemma~\ref{lemma:lf-ratio-search} gives the desired error $3\xi+\zeta=\delta$, provided each convex minimization attains error $\zeta$. We verify this guarantee and the runtime below.

\emph{Constructing the net.}
Identify $W$ with $\mathbb{R}^q$, where $q=2\dim_{\mathbb{C}}W$. For $q>0$, set $m=\lceil2\sqrt q/t\rceil$, take the grid $\{-1+2j/m:0\leq j\leq m\}^q$, and project its points radially onto the unit ball. Every point in the ball is within $t/2$ of a grid point; radial projection moves that grid point by at most another $t/2$. This gives a $t$-net with
\[
    |\mathcal N|\leq(m+1)^q\leq(2+2\sqrt q/t)^q.
\]
If $W=\{0\}$, take $\mathcal N=\{0\}$. Explicitly, the choices in the
algorithm give
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lf-net-cost-parameters}
\begin{split}
    q&\leq Q_{h,K,\delta}
       :=\left\lceil576(K+1)^6\frac{\Gamma^2}{\delta^2}\right\rceil,
       \qquad t=\frac{\delta}{36K\Gamma},\\
    |\mathcal N|&\leq
       \left(2+\frac{72K\Gamma\sqrt{Q_{h,K,\delta}}}{\delta}\right)^{Q_{h,K,\delta}}.
\end{split}
\end{equation}

\emph{Applying the convex optimization black box.}
The domain $\mathcal{D}_h$ is compact and convex, contains $0$, and has $\|\theta\|_{\mathcal P}\leq\sqrt{5h}$. Lemma~\ref{lemma:lf-ratio-search} establishes convexity of $J_z$, which is also continuous. Proposition~\ref{prop:lf-convex-oracles} supplies the required subgradients and projections with $G=G_h+\lambda/R_h$. Thus Fact~\ref{fact:lf-convex-minimization}, applied with $D_0=\sqrt{5h}$ and the $T,\alpha$ in Algorithm~\ref{alg:lf-normalized-overlap}, gives
\[
    J_z(\theta_z)\leq\min_{\mathcal{D}_h}J_z+\zeta.
\]
Every candidate therefore meets the premise of Lemma~\ref{lemma:lf-ratio-search}, using $O(T)$ oracle calls each. Here the iteration count is explicitly
\[
    T=\left\lceil\frac{20h}{\delta^2}
       \left(G_h+\frac{36K\Gamma(B_h+1)}{R_h\delta}\right)^2\right\rceil
       \asymp (h+1)^7\delta^{-2}+(h+1)^4K^2\Gamma^2\delta^{-4}.
\]

\emph{Objective evaluation and total cost.}
Evaluate $f(\theta_z)=p(\theta_z)e^{-\ell(\theta_z)}$ directly using the finite quadratic form for $p$ and \eqref{eq:lf-normalization-formula} for $\ell$. 
Constructing the tensor and $W$, and evaluating each numerator, costs
$C(K+1)^C K!(n+1)^{cK}$ operations. Each convex iteration has polynomial
cost in $n,q$. Multiplying by the net-size and iteration bounds above,
and absorbing their polynomial factors, gives the explicit prefactor
\eqref{eq:lf-normalized-cost-constant} and hence
$C_{h,K,\delta}(n+1)^{cK}$ operations for universal $c$, as claimed.
<!-- end proof-18 -->
<!-- proof-19: body_md -->
Choose a large enough $K$ by \eqref{eq:lf-cutoff-choice} and perform Lemma~\ref{lemma:lf-cutoff-tomography} with accuracy parameter $\epsilon$ and failure probability $\eta$. On its success event, \eqref{eq:lf-surrogate-error} holds simultaneously for all $\theta\in\mathcal{D}_h$, so optimizing on the estimated density matrix in the subspace $P_K$ suffices to approximately optimize the true fidelity with $\sigma$. Apply Theorem~\ref{thm:lf-normalized-optimizer} with $\delta=\epsilon/2$ to get $\hat\pi=\pi_{\hat\theta}$, which we claim is the approximately maximizing pure Gaussian state. Every $\pi$ with $\nu(\pi)\leq h$ has parameters in $\mathcal{D}_h$ by Lemma~\ref{lemma:lf-energy-domain}, so
\begin{align*}
    F(\sigma,\hat\pi)
       &\geq f(\hat\theta)-\epsilon/8\\
       &\geq\max_{\mathcal{D}_h}f-\epsilon/2-\epsilon/8
        \geq F(\sigma,\pi)-3\epsilon/4.
\end{align*}
Taking the supremum over $\pi$ with bounded photon number proves the fidelity guarantee. Moreover, $\nu(\hat\pi)\leq17(h+1)^3$.

Tomography uses $O(D_K^4\epsilon^{-2}\log(2D_K/\eta))$ copies and polynomial classical processing. Since $D_K\leq(n+1)^K$, its copy count is at most
$C(K+1)\epsilon^{-2}(n+1)^{4K+1}\log(2/\eta)$.
An explicit choice of the prefactor is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lf-bounded-cost-parameters}
    C_{h,\epsilon}
       =C\bigl((K_{h,\epsilon}+1)\epsilon^{-2}
           +C_{h,K_{h,\epsilon},\epsilon/2}\bigr),
\end{equation}
where $C$ is universal and $C_{h,K,\delta}$ is specified in
\eqref{eq:lf-normalized-cost-constant}. The exponent of $n+1$ can be taken
to be $c_*K_{h,\epsilon}$ for a sufficiently large universal $c_*$.
Taking $K=K_{h,\epsilon}$ and adding the optimization cost
gives \eqref{eq:lf-optimizer-cost}.
<!-- end proof-19 -->
<!-- theorem-15: body_md -->
\label{thm:lf-agnostic}\label{thm:lf-outline-target}
Let $\rho$ be an arbitrary $n$-mode bosonic state. Let $\mathrm{opt}=\inf_{\pi\in\mathcal{G}}d_{\rm IF}(\rho,\pi)$, let $\pi_*\in\mathcal{G}$ attain this optimum value, and let $E_0\geq3$ be an upper bound on $\nu(\pi_*)$.
Given $\epsilon\in(0,1]$ and $\delta\in(0,1/2]$, there is an algorithm which outputs $\hat\pi\in\mathcal{G}$ such that, with probability at least $1-\delta$,
\[
    d_{\rm IF}(\rho,\hat\pi)\leq\mathrm{opt}+\epsilon,
\]
using at most
\[
    n^{\mathrm{poly}(1/\epsilon)}\cdot\mathrm{poly}\bigl(\log(1/\delta),\,\log\log(E_0)\bigr)
\]
copies of $\rho$. The runtime of our algorithm is
\[
    \exp\bigl(\exp(\mathrm{poly}(1/\epsilon))\bigr)\cdot n^{\mathrm{poly}(1/\epsilon)}\cdot\mathrm{poly}\bigl(\log(1/\delta),\,\log\log E_0\bigr).
\]
<!-- end theorem-15 -->
<!-- proof-20: body_md -->
Set $\alpha=\epsilon/4$, $A_0=4E_0+2$, and $W=\lceil8/(3\alpha)\rceil$. Apply Theorem~\ref{thm:lfpr-unsqueezing} with failure probability $\delta/3$. If it reports failure, return vacuum. Otherwise, for every returned $G$, apply Theorem~\ref{thm:lf-bounded-energy} to $G\rho G^\dagger$ with $h=H_\alpha$, accuracy $\epsilon/4$, and failure probability $\delta/(3W)$. Rotate the resulting state back by applying $G^\dagger$ to obtain $\tilde\pi_G$.

Estimate each $F(\rho,\tilde\pi_G)$ to additive error $\epsilon/4$ and failure probability $\delta/(3W)$ on fresh copies, and return the candidate with the largest estimate.
