For any pure Gaussian candidate $\tilde\pi_G=U\ketbra0U^\dagger$, applying $U^\dagger$ and testing vacuum has success probability $F(\rho,\tilde\pi_G)$. Hoeffding's inequality therefore gives the stated accuracy with $O(\epsilon^{-2}\log(6W/\delta))$ copies, without any proximity-to-vacuum assumption on $\rho$.

If $F(\rho,\pi_*)<\alpha$, every output satisfies $F(\rho,\hat\pi)\geq0>F(\rho,\pi_*)-\epsilon$. Otherwise, \eqref{eq:lfpr-energy-condition} gives $\kappa(V_*)\leq A_0$. With probability at least $1-\delta$, preprocessing, every optimizer call, and every validation succeed. Conditional failure bounds are valid because these routines use fresh copies. On this event a useful basis $G_*$ satisfies $\nu(G_*\pi_*G_*^\dagger)\leq H_\alpha$, and hence
\[
    F(\rho,\tilde\pi_{G_*})\geq F(\rho,\pi_*)-\epsilon/4.
\]
Selecting the largest estimate loses at most two validation errors, so
\[
    F(\rho,\hat\pi)\geq F(\rho,\tilde\pi_{G_*})-2(\epsilon/4)
      \geq1-\mathrm{opt}-3\epsilon/4\geq1-\mathrm{opt}-\epsilon.
\]
This proves the desired infidelity guarantee.

The preprocessing cost is \eqref{eq:lfpr-preprocessing-cost}, with $c_\alpha=C_{\rm dec}(16/\epsilon)^{r_{\rm dec}}$ and $H_\alpha$ given by \eqref{eq:lfpr-terminal-constant}. The $W=\lceil32/(3\epsilon)\rceil$ optimizer calls use in total at most
\[
    W C_{H_\alpha,\epsilon/4}
       (n+1)^{c_*K_{H_\alpha,\epsilon/4}}
       \log(6W/\delta)
\]
copies and arithmetic operations, using the constants from the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:lf-bounded-energy}. Validation costs $O(W\epsilon^{-2}\log(6W/\delta))$ copies. To record the parameter dependence, set
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lf-cost-parameters}
\begin{split}
    K_\epsilon&=K_{H_\alpha,\epsilon/4},\qquad
       k_\epsilon=\max\{c_\alpha,c_*K_\epsilon\},\\
    C_\epsilon&=C\Bigl[C_\alpha
       +W\bigl(C_{H_\alpha,\epsilon/4}+\epsilon^{-2}\bigr)
          (1+\log(6W))\Bigr].
\end{split}
\end{equation}
Here $C_\alpha,c_\alpha$ are from \eqref{eq:lfpr-preprocessing-cost}, and
$K_{h,a},C_{h,a},c_*$ from \eqref{eq:lf-cutoff-choice} and \eqref{eq:lf-bounded-cost-parameters}.
The total arithmetic cost is bounded by
$C_\epsilon(n+1)^{k_\epsilon}$ times a universal polynomial in
$1+\log(1/\delta)$ and $1+\log\log(e+E_0)$. Since
$H_{\epsilon/4}\asymp\epsilon^{-s}$ with $s\asymp r_{\rm dec}$, we have
\[
    K_\epsilon\asymp k_\epsilon\asymp\epsilon^{-3s},\qquad
    C_\epsilon\leq
       \exp\!\left(\exp\!\left[C\epsilon^{-3s}\log(2/\epsilon)\right]\right).
\]
For the copy count, the $W$ optimizer calls only require the tomography
cost from Lemma~\ref{lemma:lf-cutoff-tomography}, totaling
\[
    C W(K_\epsilon+1)\epsilon^{-2}
       (n+1)^{4K_\epsilon+1}\log(6W/\delta).
\]
Adding preprocessing and validation, and absorbing their
$\exp(\mathrm{poly}(1/\epsilon))$ factors into $(n+1)^{\mathrm{poly}(1/\epsilon)}$,
gives the stated copy and decoder-call bounds. The net search contributes
only arithmetic operations. These choices have no dependence on $\mathrm{opt}$;
it enters only the target fidelity $1-\mathrm{opt}-\epsilon$.
Substituting $A_0=4E_0+2$ proves the complexity bounds in
Theorem~\ref{thm:lf-outline-target}.
<!-- end proof-20 -->
<!-- theorem-16: body_md -->
\label{thm:hf-g}
   Let $\rho$ be an $n$-mode state where $d_{\rm IF}(\rho,\ketbra{\psi})\leq \mathrm{opt}\leq \overline{\mathrm{opt}}$ for pure Gaussian state $\ket{\psi}$ with covariance $V$. Then, given $\epsilon > 0$ and $\delta \in (0,1/2)$, there exists an algorithm takes $\Tilde{O} (n^2\epsilon^{-1}\log\log \|V^{-1}\|_{\rm op}\log^5 (1/\delta))$ copies of $\rho$ and uses only Gaussian measurements to output a pure Gaussian state $\ket*{\hat \psi}$ satisfying
   \begin{equation}\label{eq:hf-g-guarantee}
       d_{\rm IF}(\rho,\ketbra*{\hat \psi})\leq O(\mathrm{opt})+\epsilon
   \end{equation}
   with probability at least $1-\delta$.
<!-- end theorem-16 -->
<!-- proof-21: body_md -->
We achieve this by making a petite modification to the final estimation step of the naive warm start algorithm which proceeds by iteratively unsqueezing the state, estimating it via robust heterodyne tomography, and then projecting the estimate to a pure Gaussian state. As before, WLOG, we ignore the unsqueezing steps and assume $\|V^{-1}\|_{\rm op}\leq 2$ so that we may use the notation of the original state for simplicity along with the fact that $\frac 1 2 I\preceq V\preceq 2I$.                 

Now, recall that in the final estimation step of the naive warm start algorithm, we use $d_{\rm tr}(\rho,\ketbra{\psi})\leq \sqrt{\mathrm{opt}}$ from Fuchs-van de Graaf to view the outcome distribution, $P$, of heterodyning $\rho$ as being $\sqrt{\mathrm{opt}}$-close in total variation distance to $Q\equiv \mathcal{N}(\mu_h, \Sigma_h)$ such that we can apply the robust Gaussian learner in Proposition~\ref{prop:robust-gaussian-learner} to learn $(\mu_h,\Sigma_h)$ to absolute Frobenius error $\Tilde{O}(\sqrt{\mathrm{opt}})$. By Lemma~\ref{lemma:frob-h-to-trd}, this means that the pure state projection is $\Tilde{O}(\sqrt{\mathrm{opt}})$-close in trace distance to $\ketbra{\psi}$, and by the manipulation in \eqref{eq:tr-to-F-transfer}, this gives an estimate which is $\Tilde{O}(\mathrm{opt})$-close in infidelity to $\rho$. Thus, by the same machinery, proving Theorem~\ref{thm:hf-g} can be reduced to learning $(\mu_h,\Sigma_h)$ to absolute Frobenius error $O(\sqrt{\mathrm{opt}})$ from the heterodyne outcomes. 

We begin by introducing a different classical robust estimation primitive. 
\begin{proposition}[Proposition 1.5 of \citep{diakonikolas2020outlier}] \label{prop:robust-bounded-cov-learner}                                                                          
    Let $\mathcal D $ be a distribution over $\mathbb{R}^d$ with mean $\mu$ and bounded covariance $\Sigma\preceq BI$ for some known constant $B>0$. Then, there exists a polytime algorithm which given $N=\Tilde{O} (\epsilon^{-2}d \log(1/\delta))$ $\eta$-corrupted samples from $\mathcal D$ outputs $\hat \mu$ such that 
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:robust-bounded-cov-learner-guarantee}
        \|\hat \mu -\mu\|_2\leq O(\sqrt{\eta})+\epsilon
    \end{equation}
    with probability at least $1-\delta$. 
\end{proposition}
                                           

\noindent At first glance, Proposition~\ref{prop:robust-bounded-cov-learner} seems worse than Proposition~\ref{prop:robust-gaussian-learner}: $d_{\rm tv}(P,Q)\lesssim \sqrt{\mathrm{opt}}$ means that taking $\mathcal D=Q$ and $\eta=\sqrt{\mathrm{opt}}$ gives only $O(\mathrm{opt}^{1/4})$ absolute error in estimating the mean of the heterodyne distribution with this learner. However, unlike Proposition~\ref{prop:robust-gaussian-learner}, Proposition~\ref{prop:robust-bounded-cov-learner} does not require $\mathcal D $ to be Gaussian. The key insight is then to write $P$ as some $O(\mathrm{opt})$-corruption of a distribution $\mathcal D$ where instead of taking $\mathcal D=Q $, we allow $\mathcal D$ to be a non-Gaussian distribution but require the mean of $\mathcal D$ to be $O(\sqrt \mathrm{opt}) $-close to the mean of $ Q $. Then, by triangle inequality and the guarantee in \eqref{eq:robust-bounded-cov-learner-guarantee}, the estimated mean is $O(\sqrt \mathrm{opt})$-close to the mean of $\mathcal D$ and also the mean of $Q $. 

We first recall that $d_{\rm IF}(\rho,\ketbra{\psi})\leq \mathrm{opt}$ implies not just $\sqrt{\mathrm{opt}}$-closeness in total variation distance but also $\mathrm{opt}$-closeness in squared Hellinger distance:                         
\begin{align}\label{eq:F-to-H2}
    d_H^2(P,Q)=1-\int \sqrt{p(x)q(x)}\; dx \leq 1-\sqrt{F(\rho,\ketbra{\psi})}\leq 1-\sqrt{1-\mathrm{opt}}\leq  \mathrm{opt}
\end{align}
where the first inequality uses the monotonicity of fidelity under measurement and where the last inequality uses the elementary fact $1-\sqrt{1-x}\leq x$ on $[0,1]$. The main technical lemma is then the following conversion from Hellinger closeness to the target Gaussian and Huber closeness to a perhaps non-Gaussian distribution that is close in mean to the original target distribution. 


\begin{lemma}\label{lemma:hel-to-huber}
Let $P,Q$ be distributions over $\mathbb{R}^d$ such that $d_H^2(P,Q)<h$ for universal constant $h$, with $Q$ having mean $\mu$ and bounded covariance $\Sigma\preceq BI$ for known constant $B>0$. Then, there exists $\eta \lesssim d_H^2(P,Q)$ and distributions $\mathcal D,\mathcal R$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:hel-to-huber-guarantee}
    P=(1-\eta)\mathcal D+\eta R,  \quad\|\mu_{\mathcal D}-\mu\|_2\lesssim d_H(P,Q),\quad  \|\Sigma_{\mathcal D}\|_{\rm op}\leq B'
\end{equation}
where $\mu_{\mathcal D},\Sigma_{\mathcal D}$ are the mean and covariance of $\mathcal D$ and $B'$ is a known function of $B$. 
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
We form $\mathcal D$ by taking $P$ and removing portions where the density significantly exceeds the density of $Q$. Specifically, fixing an $\alpha \geq 4$, let $S=\{x:p(x)>\alpha q(x)\}$ be the subset of the support we will remove, and decompose $P=(1-\eta)\mathcal D+\eta R$ with $\mathcal D =P(\cdot |S^c)$, $\mathcal R=P(\cdot | S)$, and $\eta=\Pr_P[x\in S]$. Then, 
\begin{align}\label{eq:hel-to-huber-eta-dh}
    \eta=\int_S p(x)dx \leq \alpha \int_S (\sqrt{p(x)}-\sqrt{q(x)})^2dx\leq 2\alpha d_H^2(P,Q)
\end{align}
where the first inequality uses the fact that $(\sqrt{p(x)}-\sqrt{q(x)})^2\geq (1-\alpha^{-1/2})^2p(x)\geq p(x)/\alpha$ on $S$ for $\alpha \geq 4$ and where the second inequality uses the definition of Hellinger distance. Now, set $h=1/(4\alpha)$ such that $\eta \leq 1/2$.

We now bound the difference in means. Let $u\in \mathbb{R}^d$ be a unit vector. Then, 
\begin{align}
    |\langle u,\mu_{\mathcal D}-\mu\rangle |&=\frac{1}{1-\eta}\left|\int_{S^c}\langle u,x-\mu\rangle p(x)dx \right|=\frac{1}{1-\eta}\left|\int_{S^c}\langle u,x-\mu\rangle (p(x)-q(x))dx-\int_S \langle u,x-\mu\rangle q(x)dx\right|\\
    &\leq \frac{1}{1-\eta } \left(\int_{S^c}\langle u,x-\mu\rangle^2 (\sqrt{p(x)}+\sqrt{q(x)})^2dx\right)^{1/2}\left(\int_{S^c}(\sqrt{p(x)}-\sqrt{q(x)})^2dx\right)^{1/2}\\
    &\quad +\frac{1}{1-\eta}\bigg(\int_S\langle u,x-\mu\rangle^2 q(x)dx\bigg)^{1/2}\left(\int_S q(x)dx\right)^{1/2}\\
    &\leq \frac{1}{1-\eta}\cdot (\sqrt{\alpha}+1)\left(\int_{S^c}\langle u,x-\mu\rangle^2q(x)dx\right)^{1/2}\cdot \sqrt{2}d_H(P,Q)+\frac{1}{1-\eta}\sqrt{B\Pr_Q[x\in S]}\\
    &\leq \frac{\sqrt{B}}{1-\eta}\left((\sqrt{\alpha}+1)\sqrt{2} d_H(P,Q)+\sqrt{\eta/\alpha}\right)\\
    &\lesssim d_H(P,Q)
\end{align}
where the first line uses the fact that $\int_{\mathbb{R}^d}\langle u,x-\mu\rangle q(x)dx=0$, the second line uses triangle inequality and Cauchy-Schwarz, and the final line uses $\eta\lesssim d_H^2(P,Q)$ from \eqref{eq:hel-to-huber-eta-dh}. Since this holds for any unit vector $u\in \mathbb{R}^d $, by the variational characterization of the $\ell_2$ norm, $\|\mu_{\mathcal D}-\mu\|_2 \lesssim d_H(P,Q)$ as desired.

We finally show that that $\mathcal D$ has bounded covariance as well. For unit vector $u$, 
\begin{align}
    u^\top \Sigma_{\mathcal D}u=\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathcal D}[\langle u,x-\mu_{\mathcal D}\rangle^2 ]\leq \operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathcal D}[\langle u,x-\mu\rangle ^2]\leq \frac{\alpha}{1-\eta}\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{Q}[\langle u,x-\mu\rangle^2]\leq 2\alpha B\equiv B'(B)
\end{align}
where the first inequality uses the fact that the mean minimizes the MSE and where the second inequality uses the fact that $p_{\mathcal D}(x)=\frac{p(x)\mathbbm{1}[x\in S^c]}{1-\eta}\leq\frac{ \alpha }{1-\eta}q(x) $.
Then, taking the supremum over all such $u$, $\|\Sigma_{\mathcal D}\|_{\rm op}\leq B'(B)$ as well. 
\end{proof}

\noindent Collating Lemma~\ref{lemma:hel-to-huber} and Proposition~\ref{prop:robust-bounded-cov-learner} together gives the following mean estimator for bounded covariance distributions that is robust to Hellinger misspecification. 
\begin{corollary}\label{cor:robust-mean-H2-bounded-cov}
   Let $P,Q$ be distributions over $\mathbb{R}^d $ such that $d_H^2(P,Q)\leq \eta\leq \overline{\eta}$ for some universal constant $\overline{\eta}>0$, with $Q$ having mean $\mu$ and bounded covariance $\Sigma \preceq BI$ for known constant $B>0$. Then, there exists a polytime algorithm which given $N=\Tilde{O}(\epsilon^{-2}d\log(1/\delta))$ samples from $P$ outputs $\hat \mu$ such that $$\|\hat \mu-\mu\|_2\leq O(\sqrt{\eta})+\epsilon$$ with probability at least $1-\delta$. 
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Applying Lemma~\ref{lemma:hel-to-huber}, Proposition~\ref{prop:robust-bounded-cov-learner} and identifying $\mathcal{D}$, we have a $\hat \mu$ such that:
\begin{align}
    \|\hat \mu-\mu\|_2\leq \|\hat \mu-\mu_{\mathcal D}\|_2+\|\mu_{\mathcal D}-\mu\|_2 \leq (O(\sqrt{\eta })+\epsilon) +\sqrt{\eta}=O(\sqrt{\eta})+\epsilon
\end{align}
where the first term is from \eqref{eq:robust-bounded-cov-learner-guarantee} and the second term is from \eqref{eq:hel-to-huber-guarantee}. 
\end{proof}
\noindent When $Q$ is Gaussian, we can also learn the covariance with a touch more work. 
\begin{corollary}\label{cor:robust-H2-bounded-cov-gaussian-learner}
    Let $P,Q$ be distributions over $\mathbb{R}^d $ such that $d_H^2(P,Q)\leq \eta\leq \overline{\eta}$ for some universal constant $\overline{\eta}>0$, with $Q=\mathcal N(\mu,\Sigma)$ having bounded covariance $\Sigma \preceq BI$ for known constant $B>0$. Then, there exists a polytime algorithm which given $N=\Tilde{O}(\epsilon^{-2}d^2\log(1/\delta))$ samples from $P$ outputs $\hat \mu,\hat \Sigma$ such that $$\|\hat \mu-\mu\|_2\leq O(\sqrt{\eta})+\epsilon,\quad \|\hat \Sigma-\Sigma\|_F\leq O(\sqrt \eta)+\epsilon$$ with probability at least $1-\delta$.   
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
    Corollary~\ref{cor:robust-mean-H2-bounded-cov} uses $\Tilde{O}(\epsilon_1^{-2}d\log(1/\delta))$ samples to give a $\hat \mu$ such that $\|\Delta\|_2\leq O(\sqrt \eta)+\epsilon_1$, where $\Delta\equiv \hat \mu-\mu$. Now, taking fresh samples $X_i$, we form $Y_i=(X_i-\hat \mu)(X_i-\hat \mu)^\top$, which has the distribution $Q_Y$ or $P_Y$ if $X_i\stackrel{{\rm iid}}{\sim} Q$ or $X_i\stackrel{{\rm iid}}{\sim} P$ respectively. Viewing $Y_i$ as a vector such that the $\ell_2$ guarantee corresponds with the Frobenius norm, we claim that Corollary~\ref{cor:robust-mean-H2-bounded-cov} gives an estimate $\hat \Sigma$ with $\|\hat \Sigma-\Sigma\|_F\leq O(\sqrt \eta)+\epsilon$. 

     First, by data processing, we have that 
    \begin{align}
        d_H^2(P_Y,Q_Y)\leq d_H^2(P,Q)\leq \eta
    \end{align}
    as desired. Then, we can view $X_i=\mu+Z$ for $Z_i\stackrel{{\rm iid}}{\sim} \mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma)$, meaning $\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{Q_Y}[Y]=\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}[(Z-\Delta)(Z-\Delta)^\top]=\Sigma +\Delta\Delta^\top$. Assuming for now that the conditions of Corollary~\ref{cor:robust-mean-H2-bounded-cov} are valid,
    \begin{align}
        \|\hat \Sigma-\Sigma\|_F\leq \|\hat \Sigma-\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{Q_Y}[Y]\|_F+\|\Delta \Delta^\top \|_F\leq (O(\sqrt{\eta})+\epsilon_1) +\|\Delta \|_2^2\leq O(\sqrt{\eta})+\epsilon_1+(O(\sqrt{\eta})+\epsilon_1)^2\leq O(\sqrt{\eta})+\epsilon
    \end{align}
    where both calls of Corollary~\ref{cor:robust-mean-H2-bounded-cov} use slack $\epsilon_1$, which we define such that the final slack is $\epsilon$.  Thus, it suffices to show that $Q_Y$ has bounded covariance. We do this by showing that ${\rm Var}_{Q_Y}(\langle M,Y\rangle_F)\leq B$ for all $M\in \mathbb{R}^{d\times d}$ with $\|M\|_F=1$, for some $B>0$. Since $Y$ is symmetric, it suffices to consider symmetric $M$. Then, by standard moment identities regarding multivariate Gaussians, specifically (379) of \citep{petersen2008matrix}, 
    \begin{align}
        {\rm Var}_{Q_Y}(\langle M,Y\rangle_F)={\rm Var}_{Q_Y}((Z-\Delta)^\top M(Z-\Delta))= 2\tr(M\Sigma M\Sigma )+4\Delta^\top M \Sigma M\Delta &\leq (2B^2+4B\|\Delta\|_2^2)\cancel{\|M\|_F^2}\\&\leq 2B^2+4B
    \end{align}
    where we use the bounds $\Sigma \preceq BI$ and $\|\Delta\|_2^2\leq 1$ in the final two inequalities. Thus, $Q_Y$ has bounded covariance, meaning we are done. 
\end{proof}

\noindent Thus, \eqref{eq:F-to-H2} and Corollary~\ref{cor:robust-H2-bounded-cov-gaussian-learner} give estimators $(\hat \mu_h,\hat \Sigma_h)$ that resolve $(\mu_h,\Sigma_h)$ to additive absolute error $O(\sqrt{\mathrm{opt}})+\epsilon$, meaning we are done.
<!-- end proof-21 -->
<!-- proposition-8: body_md -->
\label{prop:robust-bounded-cov-learner}                                                                          
    Let $\mathcal D $ be a distribution over $\mathbb{R}^d$ with mean $\mu$ and bounded covariance $\Sigma\preceq BI$ for some known constant $B>0$. Then, there exists a polytime algorithm which given $N=\Tilde{O} (\epsilon^{-2}d \log(1/\delta))$ $\eta$-corrupted samples from $\mathcal D$ outputs $\hat \mu$ such that 
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:robust-bounded-cov-learner-guarantee}
        \|\hat \mu -\mu\|_2\leq O(\sqrt{\eta})+\epsilon
    \end{equation}
    with probability at least $1-\delta$.
<!-- end proposition-8 -->
<!-- lemma-23: body_md -->
\label{lemma:hel-to-huber}
Let $P,Q$ be distributions over $\mathbb{R}^d$ such that $d_H^2(P,Q)<h$ for universal constant $h$, with $Q$ having mean $\mu$ and bounded covariance $\Sigma\preceq BI$ for known constant $B>0$. Then, there exists $\eta \lesssim d_H^2(P,Q)$ and distributions $\mathcal D,\mathcal R$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:hel-to-huber-guarantee}
    P=(1-\eta)\mathcal D+\eta R,  \quad\|\mu_{\mathcal D}-\mu\|_2\lesssim d_H(P,Q),\quad  \|\Sigma_{\mathcal D}\|_{\rm op}\leq B'
\end{equation}
where $\mu_{\mathcal D},\Sigma_{\mathcal D}$ are the mean and covariance of $\mathcal D$ and $B'$ is a known function of $B$.
<!-- end lemma-23 -->
<!-- proof-22: body_md -->
We form $\mathcal D$ by taking $P$ and removing portions where the density significantly exceeds the density of $Q$. Specifically, fixing an $\alpha \geq 4$, let $S=\{x:p(x)>\alpha q(x)\}$ be the subset of the support we will remove, and decompose $P=(1-\eta)\mathcal D+\eta R$ with $\mathcal D =P(\cdot |S^c)$, $\mathcal R=P(\cdot | S)$, and $\eta=\Pr_P[x\in S]$. Then, 
\begin{align}\label{eq:hel-to-huber-eta-dh}
    \eta=\int_S p(x)dx \leq \alpha \int_S (\sqrt{p(x)}-\sqrt{q(x)})^2dx\leq 2\alpha d_H^2(P,Q)
\end{align}
where the first inequality uses the fact that $(\sqrt{p(x)}-\sqrt{q(x)})^2\geq (1-\alpha^{-1/2})^2p(x)\geq p(x)/\alpha$ on $S$ for $\alpha \geq 4$ and where the second inequality uses the definition of Hellinger distance. Now, set $h=1/(4\alpha)$ such that $\eta \leq 1/2$.

We now bound the difference in means. Let $u\in \mathbb{R}^d$ be a unit vector. Then, 
\begin{align}
    |\langle u,\mu_{\mathcal D}-\mu\rangle |&=\frac{1}{1-\eta}\left|\int_{S^c}\langle u,x-\mu\rangle p(x)dx \right|=\frac{1}{1-\eta}\left|\int_{S^c}\langle u,x-\mu\rangle (p(x)-q(x))dx-\int_S \langle u,x-\mu\rangle q(x)dx\right|\\
    &\leq \frac{1}{1-\eta } \left(\int_{S^c}\langle u,x-\mu\rangle^2 (\sqrt{p(x)}+\sqrt{q(x)})^2dx\right)^{1/2}\left(\int_{S^c}(\sqrt{p(x)}-\sqrt{q(x)})^2dx\right)^{1/2}\\
    &\quad +\frac{1}{1-\eta}\bigg(\int_S\langle u,x-\mu\rangle^2 q(x)dx\bigg)^{1/2}\left(\int_S q(x)dx\right)^{1/2}\\
    &\leq \frac{1}{1-\eta}\cdot (\sqrt{\alpha}+1)\left(\int_{S^c}\langle u,x-\mu\rangle^2q(x)dx\right)^{1/2}\cdot \sqrt{2}d_H(P,Q)+\frac{1}{1-\eta}\sqrt{B\Pr_Q[x\in S]}\\
    &\leq \frac{\sqrt{B}}{1-\eta}\left((\sqrt{\alpha}+1)\sqrt{2} d_H(P,Q)+\sqrt{\eta/\alpha}\right)\\
    &\lesssim d_H(P,Q)
\end{align}
where the first line uses the fact that $\int_{\mathbb{R}^d}\langle u,x-\mu\rangle q(x)dx=0$, the second line uses triangle inequality and Cauchy-Schwarz, and the final line uses $\eta\lesssim d_H^2(P,Q)$ from \eqref{eq:hel-to-huber-eta-dh}. Since this holds for any unit vector $u\in \mathbb{R}^d $, by the variational characterization of the $\ell_2$ norm, $\|\mu_{\mathcal D}-\mu\|_2 \lesssim d_H(P,Q)$ as desired.

We finally show that that $\mathcal D$ has bounded covariance as well. For unit vector $u$, 
\begin{align}
    u^\top \Sigma_{\mathcal D}u=\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathcal D}[\langle u,x-\mu_{\mathcal D}\rangle^2 ]\leq \operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathcal D}[\langle u,x-\mu\rangle ^2]\leq \frac{\alpha}{1-\eta}\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{Q}[\langle u,x-\mu\rangle^2]\leq 2\alpha B\equiv B'(B)
\end{align}
where the first inequality uses the fact that the mean minimizes the MSE and where the second inequality uses the fact that $p_{\mathcal D}(x)=\frac{p(x)\mathbbm{1}[x\in S^c]}{1-\eta}\leq\frac{ \alpha }{1-\eta}q(x) $.
Then, taking the supremum over all such $u$, $\|\Sigma_{\mathcal D}\|_{\rm op}\leq B'(B)$ as well.
<!-- end proof-22 -->
<!-- corollary-1: body_md -->
\label{cor:robust-mean-H2-bounded-cov}
   Let $P,Q$ be distributions over $\mathbb{R}^d $ such that $d_H^2(P,Q)\leq \eta\leq \overline{\eta}$ for some universal constant $\overline{\eta}>0$, with $Q$ having mean $\mu$ and bounded covariance $\Sigma \preceq BI$ for known constant $B>0$. Then, there exists a polytime algorithm which given $N=\Tilde{O}(\epsilon^{-2}d\log(1/\delta))$ samples from $P$ outputs $\hat \mu$ such that $$\|\hat \mu-\mu\|_2\leq O(\sqrt{\eta})+\epsilon$$ with probability at least $1-\delta$.
<!-- end corollary-1 -->
<!-- proof-23: body_md -->
Applying Lemma~\ref{lemma:hel-to-huber}, Proposition~\ref{prop:robust-bounded-cov-learner} and identifying $\mathcal{D}$, we have a $\hat \mu$ such that:
\begin{align}
    \|\hat \mu-\mu\|_2\leq \|\hat \mu-\mu_{\mathcal D}\|_2+\|\mu_{\mathcal D}-\mu\|_2 \leq (O(\sqrt{\eta })+\epsilon) +\sqrt{\eta}=O(\sqrt{\eta})+\epsilon
\end{align}
where the first term is from \eqref{eq:robust-bounded-cov-learner-guarantee} and the second term is from \eqref{eq:hel-to-huber-guarantee}.
<!-- end proof-23 -->
<!-- corollary-2: body_md -->
\label{cor:robust-H2-bounded-cov-gaussian-learner}
    Let $P,Q$ be distributions over $\mathbb{R}^d $ such that $d_H^2(P,Q)\leq \eta\leq \overline{\eta}$ for some universal constant $\overline{\eta}>0$, with $Q=\mathcal N(\mu,\Sigma)$ having bounded covariance $\Sigma \preceq BI$ for known constant $B>0$. Then, there exists a polytime algorithm which given $N=\Tilde{O}(\epsilon^{-2}d^2\log(1/\delta))$ samples from $P$ outputs $\hat \mu,\hat \Sigma$ such that $$\|\hat \mu-\mu\|_2\leq O(\sqrt{\eta})+\epsilon,\quad \|\hat \Sigma-\Sigma\|_F\leq O(\sqrt \eta)+\epsilon$$ with probability at least $1-\delta$.
<!-- end corollary-2 -->
<!-- proof-24: body_md -->
Corollary~\ref{cor:robust-mean-H2-bounded-cov} uses $\Tilde{O}(\epsilon_1^{-2}d\log(1/\delta))$ samples to give a $\hat \mu$ such that $\|\Delta\|_2\leq O(\sqrt \eta)+\epsilon_1$, where $\Delta\equiv \hat \mu-\mu$. Now, taking fresh samples $X_i$, we form $Y_i=(X_i-\hat \mu)(X_i-\hat \mu)^\top$, which has the distribution $Q_Y$ or $P_Y$ if $X_i\stackrel{{\rm iid}}{\sim} Q$ or $X_i\stackrel{{\rm iid}}{\sim} P$ respectively. Viewing $Y_i$ as a vector such that the $\ell_2$ guarantee corresponds with the Frobenius norm, we claim that Corollary~\ref{cor:robust-mean-H2-bounded-cov} gives an estimate $\hat \Sigma$ with $\|\hat \Sigma-\Sigma\|_F\leq O(\sqrt \eta)+\epsilon$. 

     First, by data processing, we have that 
    \begin{align}
        d_H^2(P_Y,Q_Y)\leq d_H^2(P,Q)\leq \eta
    \end{align}
    as desired. Then, we can view $X_i=\mu+Z$ for $Z_i\stackrel{{\rm iid}}{\sim} \mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma)$, meaning $\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{Q_Y}[Y]=\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}[(Z-\Delta)(Z-\Delta)^\top]=\Sigma +\Delta\Delta^\top$. Assuming for now that the conditions of Corollary~\ref{cor:robust-mean-H2-bounded-cov} are valid,
    \begin{align}
        \|\hat \Sigma-\Sigma\|_F\leq \|\hat \Sigma-\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{Q_Y}[Y]\|_F+\|\Delta \Delta^\top \|_F\leq (O(\sqrt{\eta})+\epsilon_1) +\|\Delta \|_2^2\leq O(\sqrt{\eta})+\epsilon_1+(O(\sqrt{\eta})+\epsilon_1)^2\leq O(\sqrt{\eta})+\epsilon
    \end{align}
    where both calls of Corollary~\ref{cor:robust-mean-H2-bounded-cov} use slack $\epsilon_1$, which we define such that the final slack is $\epsilon$.  Thus, it suffices to show that $Q_Y$ has bounded covariance. We do this by showing that ${\rm Var}_{Q_Y}(\langle M,Y\rangle_F)\leq B$ for all $M\in \mathbb{R}^{d\times d}$ with $\|M\|_F=1$, for some $B>0$. Since $Y$ is symmetric, it suffices to consider symmetric $M$. Then, by standard moment identities regarding multivariate Gaussians, specifically (379) of \citep{petersen2008matrix}, 
    \begin{align}
        {\rm Var}_{Q_Y}(\langle M,Y\rangle_F)={\rm Var}_{Q_Y}((Z-\Delta)^\top M(Z-\Delta))= 2\tr(M\Sigma M\Sigma )+4\Delta^\top M \Sigma M\Delta &\leq (2B^2+4B\|\Delta\|_2^2)\cancel{\|M\|_F^2}\\&\leq 2B^2+4B
    \end{align}
    where we use the bounds $\Sigma \preceq BI$ and $\|\Delta\|_2^2\leq 1$ in the final two inequalities. Thus, $Q_Y$ has bounded covariance, meaning we are done.
<!-- end proof-24 -->
<!-- theorem-17: body_md -->
\label{thm:gaussian-measurement-hardness}
For universal constant $c>0$, sufficiently large $n$, and $\eta\in (0,1/4]$, there exist two $(n+1)$-mode states $\rho_{+},\rho_-$ satisfying 
$$\mathrm{opt}_{+}=\mathrm{opt}_-=O (\eta),\quad d_{\rm tr}(\rho_{\pm},\ket{0}^{\otimes (n+1)})\leq \eta$$
where $\mathrm{opt}_{\pm}\equiv \inf_{\ket \psi\in \mathcal G}d_{\rm IF}(\rho_{\pm},\ket{\psi})$, such that any algorithm using only Gaussian measurements that outputs $\ket*{\hat \psi_{\pm }}$ with the guarantee $d_{\rm IF}(\rho_{\pm},\ket*{\hat \psi_{\pm}})\leq \mathrm{opt}_{\pm }+c\eta^2 $ with probability at least $2/3$ must have copy complexity $N\geq \eta^{-1}\exp(\Omega(n^{1/3}))$.
<!-- end theorem-17 -->
<!-- lemma-24: body_md -->
\label{eq:lb-match-wu}
   For every integer $k\geq 1$, there exist random variables $U_+,U_-$ supported on $\{0\}\cup [1,Ck^2]$ with the following properties:
   \begin{align}
       \operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}[U_\pm]=1,\quad \operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}[U_+^j]=\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}[U_-^j] \quad (j\in [k]), \quad \Pr[U_+=0]-\Pr[U_-=0]\geq c
   \end{align}
   where $C,c>0$ are universal constants.
<!-- end lemma-24 -->
<!-- lemma-25: body_md -->
\label{lemma:gm-hidden-vacuum}
There exists $c>0$ such that for every sufficiently large $n$, there exist $n$-mode states $\sigma_+,\sigma_-$ that are separable, diagonal in the Fock basis and have nonnegative Wigner functions such that:
\begin{align}\label{eq:gm-hidden-vacuum}
    \bra{0}^{\otimes n}\sigma_+\ket{0}^{\otimes n}-\bra{0}^{\otimes n}\sigma_-\ket{0}^{\otimes n}\geq c,\qquad
    d_{\rm tv}(W_{\sigma_+},W_{\sigma_-})\leq\exp(-\Omega(n^{1/3}))
\end{align}
<!-- end lemma-25 -->
<!-- proof-25: body_md -->
Choosing $k=c_k n^{1/3}$ for suitably small constant $c_k>0$, define the following mixed state:
    \begin{align}\label{eq:gm-signal-states}
    \sigma_\pm=\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{u\sim U_{\pm}}\left[
       \left(\left(1-\frac{su}{2n}\right)\ketbra0
                    +\frac{su}{2n}\ketbra1\right)^{\otimes n}
    \right]
\end{align}
for suitably large scaling constant $s$, where we take $n$ large enough such that $su/(2n)<1/2$. Considering the vacuum probabilities, 
\begin{align}
     \bra{0}^{\otimes n}\sigma_+\ket{0}^{\otimes n}-\bra{0}^{\otimes n}\sigma_-\ket{0}^{\otimes n}&=\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{u\sim U_+}\left[\left(1-\frac{su}{2n}\right)^n \right]-\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{u\sim U_-}\left[\left(1-\frac{su}{2n}\right)^n \right]\\
     &\geq \Pr[U_+=0]-(\Pr[U_-=0]+e^{-s/2}\Pr[U_->0])\\
     &\geq c-e^{-s/2}
\end{align}
where we use the fact that $(1-su/(2n))^n \leq e^{-su/2}\leq e^{-s/2}$ when $u\geq 1$. Taking suitable $c,s$, the vacuum probability is bounded away from $0$. 

We now show that the Wigner functions are close in total variation distance. Defining $g(q,p)=\pi^{-1}e^{-q^2-p^2}$, it is a known fact that the vacuum and one-photon Wigner functions are given by $W_{\ketbra{0}}=g$ and $W_{\ketbra{1}}=(2q^2+2p^2-1)g$ respectively, where the distribution of $q^2+p^2$ under joint density $g(q,p)$ can be viewed as an exponential random variable of unit mean.                   Using these facts and linearity, the $i$-th mode of $\sigma_\pm$ has Wigner function:
\begin{align}
   W_{\left(1-\frac{su}{2n}\right)\ketbra0
                    +\frac{su}{2n}\ketbra1} =g(q_i,p_i)\left(1-\frac{su}{2n}+\frac{su}{2n}(2q_i^2+2p_i^2-1)\right)=g(q_i,p_i)\left(1+\frac{su}{n}Z_i\right)
\end{align}
where $Z_i=q_i^2+p_i^2-1$ gives $\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{g_i}[Z_i]=0$ and $\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{g_i}[Z_i^2]=1$. Then, by linearity and the tensor-product rule for the Wigner function,
\begin{align}\label{eq:Wsigmapm}
   W_{\sigma_\pm} &=\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{u\sim U_\pm}\left[\prod_{i=1}^n g(q_i,p_i)\left(1+\frac{su}{n}Z_i\right)\right]=g^{\otimes n}\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{u\sim U_\pm }\left[\prod_{i=1}^n \left(1+\frac{su}{n}Z_i\right)\right]
\end{align}
where $g^{\otimes n}(q,p)=\prod_{i=1}^n g(q_i,p_i)$ is the natural product density. By moment matching,
\begin{align}
    \frac{W_{\sigma_+}-W_{\sigma_-}}{g^{\otimes n}}=\sum_{S\subseteq[n]:|S|>k}\left(\frac s n\right)^{|S|}\left(\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}[U_+^{|S|}]-\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}[U_-^{|S|}]\right)\prod_{i\in S}Z_i 
\end{align}
Then, by Cauchy-Schwarz,
\begin{align}
    &\|W_{\sigma_+}-W_{\sigma_-}\|_1^2\\
    &=\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{g^{\otimes n}}\left[\left| \frac{W_{\sigma_+}-W_{\sigma_-}}{g^{\otimes n}}\right|\right]^2\leq \operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{g^{\otimes n}}\left[\left( \frac{W_{\sigma_+}-W_{\sigma_-}}{g^{\otimes n}}\right)^2\right]\\
    &=\sum_{S_1,S_2\subseteq[n]:|S_1|>k,|S_2|>k}\left(\frac s n\right)^{|S_1|+|S_2|}\left(\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}[U_+^{|S_1|}]-\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}[U_-^{|S_1|}]\right)\left(\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}[U_+^{|S_2|}]-\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}[U_-^{|S_2|}]\right)\underbrace{\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}_{g^{\otimes n}}\left[\prod_{i\in S_1}Z_i \prod_{i\in S_2}Z_i\right]}_{\mathbbm{1}[S_1=S_2]}\\
    &=\sum_{j=k+1}^{n}\binom{n}{j}\left(\frac s n\right)^{2j}(\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}[U_+^j]-\operatorname*{\mathbb{E}}[U_-^j])^2\\
    &\leq 4\sum_{j=k+1}^n (eC^2 s^2 k^3/n)^j
\end{align}
where we use the independence, zero mean, and unit variance properties of $Z_i$ as well as boundedness $U_\pm \in [0,Ck^2]$ and the standard fact $\binom{n}{j}\leq n^j/j!\leq (en/j)^j$. Then, if $k=c_k n^{1/3}$ for suitably small $c_k$, we can bound $e C^2s^2 k^3/n\leq 1/4$, giving $\|W_{\sigma_+}-W_{\sigma_-}\|_1^2\leq O(4^{-k})$ by bounding the tail sum of a geometric series. Since $W_{\sigma_\pm}\geq 0$ from \eqref{eq:Wsigmapm}, $d_{\rm tv}(W_{\sigma_+},W_{\sigma_-})\leq \exp(-\Omega(k))$ which proves \eqref{eq:gm-hidden-vacuum}.
<!-- end proof-25 -->
<!-- lemma-26: body_md -->
\label{lemma:barrho-bound}
$1-\mathrm{opt}_\pm > \|\bar \rho\|_\mathrm{op} +\Omega(\eta^2)$.
<!-- end lemma-26 -->
<!-- proof-26: body_md -->
From \eqref{eq:gm-extra-mode}, 
\begin{align}
    \bar \rho=(1-\eta)\ketbra{0}^{\otimes (n+1)}+\frac \eta 2 (\sigma_++\sigma_-)\otimes \left(\frac 3 4 \ketbra{0}+\frac 1 4 \ketbra{1}\right)
\end{align}
which is diagonal in the Fock basis. For $\eta \leq 1/4$, since $\|\sigma_\pm \|_\mathrm{op} \leq 1$ and $\bra{0}\omega_+\ket{0}=\bra{0}\omega_-\ket{0}$, it follows that 
\begin{align}\label{eq:rhobarop}
\|\bar \rho\|_{\mathrm{op}}=\bra{0}^{\otimes (n+1)}\bar \rho\ket{0}^{\otimes (n+1)}=\bra{0}^{\otimes (n+1)}\rho_\pm \ket{0}^{\otimes (n+1)}
\end{align}
We now exhibit a pure Gaussian state that achieves a fidelity that surpasses this vacuum fidelity by $\Omega(\eta^2)$. Using the fact that $\bra{0}\omega_\pm \ket{1}=\pm 1/4$ and $\bra{1}\omega_\pm \ket{1}=1/4$, consider the following elements of $\rho_\pm$: 
\begin{align}\label{eq:last-mode-t}
    (\bra{0}^{\otimes n}\otimes \bra{0})\rho_\pm (\ket{0}^{\otimes n}\otimes \ket 1 )&=\pm \frac \eta 8 \bra{0}^{\otimes n}(\sigma_+-\sigma_-)\ket{0}^{\otimes n}\equiv \pm t
        
                                                                                                                                                               
\end{align}
We then consider the state $\ket{\phi_\pm}=\ket{0}^{\otimes n}\otimes \ket{\psi(\pm t,0)}$ where $\ket{\psi(\pm t,0)}=e^{-t^2/2}\sum_{j\geq 0}\frac{(\pm t)^j}{\sqrt{j!}}\ket{j}$ from \eqref{eq:barg-state} is a coherent state with this displacement on the last mode. Then,  
\begin{align}
    1-\mathrm{opt}_\pm \geq \bra{\phi_\pm }\rho_\pm \ket{\phi_\pm }&=e^{-t^2}\bigg(\bra{0}^{\otimes (n+1)}\rho_\pm \ket{0}^{\otimes (n+1)}+2(\pm t) (\bra{0}^{\otimes n}\otimes \bra{0})\rho_\pm (\ket{0}^{\otimes n}\otimes \ket 1 )
    \\&\quad \quad \quad \quad + t^2(\bra{0}^{\otimes n}\otimes \bra{1})\rho_\pm (\ket{0}^{\otimes n}\otimes \ket 1 )\bigg)\\
    &\geq e^{-t^2}(\|\bar \rho\|_{\rm op}+2t^2)\\
    &\geq \|\bar \rho\|_{\mathrm{op}}+t^2 -2t^4
\end{align}
where we drop the last term, recall \eqref{eq:rhobarop}, and use the fact that $e^{-t^2}\geq 1-t^2$ and $\|\bar\rho\|_\mathrm{op}\leq 1$. Since $t=\Theta(\eta)$ by Lemma~\ref{eq:gm-hidden-vacuum} and \eqref{eq:last-mode-t}, we can take $t\leq 1/2$ such that $t^2-2t^4\geq t^2/2>\Omega(\eta^2)$, proving the claim.
<!-- end proof-26 -->
<!-- fact-5: body_md -->
Let $G=([n],E)$ be a graph with vertices labeled by the integers. Then,  
   \begin{equation}\label{eq:motzkin}
      \max_{\|x\|_2=1}\sum_{\{i,j\}\in E}|x_i|^2|x_j|^2=\max_{\stackrel{p_i\geq 0}{\sum_i p_i=1}}\sum_{\{i,j\}\in E} p_i p_j=\frac 1 2 \left(1-\frac{1}{\omega(G)}\right)
   \end{equation}
   where the maximizer assigns weight $1/\omega(G)$ to each vertex of a maximum clique.
<!-- end fact-5 -->
<!-- theorem-18: body_md -->
\label{thm:lf-hardness}
    Unless $\mathrm{NP}\subseteq \mathrm{BQP}$, there does not exist a quantum algorithm which takes any $\epsilon >0$, copies of any $n$-mode state $\rho$, and  $\mathrm{poly}(n,1/\epsilon)$ time to output a pure Gaussian state $\ket*{\hat \psi}$ such that $d_{\rm IF}(\rho,\ketbra*{\hat \psi})\leq \mathrm{opt} +\epsilon $ with probability at least $0.99$.
<!-- end theorem-18 -->
<!-- lemma-27: body_md -->
\label{lemma:P1-stability}
    Let $(\beta^*,\alpha^*)=(\sqrt{3/2},-1/2)$ and abbreviate $\ket{\psi^*(x)}\equiv \ket{\psi(x;\beta^*,\alpha^*)}$ for $\|x\|_2=1$. Then, for any $\ket{\psi(b,A)}\in \mathcal G$ with $b\neq 0$,
    \begin{align}\label{eq:p1-stability-guarantee}
    p_1(\ket{\psi})\leq p_1^*,\quad \inf_{\|x\|_2=1}d_{\rm IF}(\ket\psi,\ket{\psi^*(x)})\lesssim p_1^*-p_1(\ket{\psi})
    \end{align}
    where $p_1^*=3\sqrt{3}/(4e)$ is the maximal value achieved by states exactly of the form $\ket{\psi(x)}$.
<!-- end lemma-27 -->
<!-- lemma-28: body_md -->
Let $\mathrm{OPT}_G=\max_{\|x\|_2=1}\sum_{\{i,j\}\in E}|x_i|^2|x_j|^2$ and $\mathrm{OPT}_{\rho_G}\equiv   \max_{\ket{\psi}\in \mathcal G }F(\rho_G,\ketbra{\psi})$. Then, 
        \begin{align}\label{eq:optG-to-optRhoG}
        p_1^*+\frac{2}{3}p_1^*\lambda \mathrm{OPT}_G\leq (n+\lambda |E|)\; \mathrm{OPT}_{\rho_G}\leq  p_1^*+\frac{2}{3}p_1^*\lambda \mathrm{OPT}_G+C\lambda^2
        \end{align}
        for universal constant $C>0$.
<!-- end lemma-28 -->
<!-- proof-27: body_md -->
Define $p_G(\ket{\psi})\equiv \bra{\psi}P_G\ket{\psi}$ such that:
        \begin{align}
            (n+\lambda |E|)\mathrm{OPT}_{\rho_G}=\max_{\ket{\psi}\in \mathcal G } (p_1(\ket{\psi})+\lambda p_G(\ket{\psi}))
        \end{align}
        Then, the lower bound immediately follows from restricting to the family $\ket{\psi^*(x)}$:
        \begin{align}
            \max_{\ket{\psi^*(x)}} (p_1(\ket{\psi^*(x)})+\lambda p_G(\ket{\psi^*(x)}))&=p_1^*+\lambda\sqrt{1-|\alpha^*|^2}\exp(-\frac{(\beta^*)^2(1+\Re \alpha^*)}{1-|\alpha^*|^2})|\alpha^*+(\beta^*)^2|^2 \mathrm{OPT}_G\\
            &=p_1^*+\lambda\frac{\sqrt 3}{2e}\mathrm{OPT}_G\\
            &=p_1^*+\frac 2 3 \lambda p_1^*\mathrm{OPT}_G \label{eq:pg-opt-in-family}
        \end{align}
        where the first term is given by Lemma~\ref{lemma:P1-stability} and the second follows from \eqref{eq:motzkin-psiPg-1} and \eqref{eq:Z(beta,alpha)}. 
        
        For the upper bound, we leverage Lemma~\ref{lemma:P1-stability} to control the performance of $\ket{\psi}\in \mathcal G$. Specifically, suppose $\ket{\psi}$ fails to meet the optimum value of $p_1(\cdot)$ by $\Delta_1 =p_1^*-p_1(\ket{\psi})$. Then, by Lemma~\ref{lemma:P1-stability}, there exists an $x\in \mathbb{C}^n$ with $\|x\|_2=1$ such that $d_{\rm IF}(\ket{\psi},\ket{\psi^*(x)})\lesssim \Delta_1$. Using this to control the deviation of $p_G(\cdot)$ from the optimum:
        \begin{align}
            |p_G(\ket{\psi^*(x)})-p_G(\ket{\psi})|\leq d_{\rm tr}(\ketbra{\psi},\ketbra{\psi^*(x)})=\sqrt{d_{\rm IF}(\ket{\psi},\ket{\psi^*(x)})}\leq C_1 \sqrt{\Delta_1}
        \end{align}
        for some constant $C_1>0$. Applying this, 
        \begin{align}
           p_1(\ket{\psi})+\lambda p_G(\ket{\psi})\leq p_1^*-\Delta +\lambda p_G(\ket{\psi^*(x)})+\lambda C_1\sqrt{\Delta_1}&\leq p_1^*+\frac{2}{3}\lambda p_1^*\mathrm{OPT}_G -(\sqrt{\Delta_1}-\lambda C_1/2)^2+\frac{C_1^2}{4}\lambda^2\\
           &\leq p_1^*+\frac 2 3 \lambda p_1^*\mathrm{OPT}_G +C_1 \lambda^2
        \end{align}
        where we bound $p_G(\ket{\psi^*(x)})\leq \max_{\ket{\psi^*(x)}}p_G(\ket{\psi^*(x)})=\frac 2 3 \lambda p_1^*\mathrm{OPT}_G $ from the computation in \eqref{eq:pg-opt-in-family}, complete the square on the error terms, and define $C$ accordingly. This proves the claim.
<!-- end proof-27 -->
<!-- proof-28: body_md -->
Suppose we have a polytime agnostic learner for pure Gaussian states to error $\mathrm{opt}+\epsilon$. From \eqref{eq:lf-lb-rhoG}, given $G$, we can prepare a copy of $\rho$ by preparing $\hat{a}_i^\dagger \hat{a}_j^\dagger \ket{0}$ with probability $\frac{\lambda}{n+\lambda |E|}$ for $\{i,j\}\in E$ and $\hat{a}_i^\dagger \ket{0}$ with probability $\frac{n}{n+\lambda |E|}$ for $i\in [n]$. Feeding ${\rm poly}(n,1/\epsilon_1)$ copies of $\rho_G$ to our learner gives a $\ket{\hat \psi}$ such that $F(\rho_G,\ketbra*{\hat \psi})\geq \mathrm{OPT}_{\rho_G}-\epsilon $. Since $\ket*{\hat \psi}$ is a pure Gaussian state, we can then use Lemma~\ref{lemma:hf-value-oracle} with $\Tilde{O} (\epsilon^{-2})$ copies of $\rho_G$ to estimate $F(\rho_G,\ketbra*{\hat \psi})$ to additive error $\epsilon$, using Gaussian unitaries and on-off photon detection. It follows that we can estimate $\mathrm{OPT}_{\rho_G} $ to additive error $\epsilon$ in $\mathrm{poly}(n,1/\epsilon)$ time.

   Calling this estimate $\widehat{\mathrm{OPT}}_{\rho_G}$, form the following estimates:
   \begin{align}
   \widehat{\mathrm{OPT}}_G&=\frac{3}{2p_1^*\lambda}((n+\lambda |E|)\widehat{\mathrm{OPT}}_{\rho _G}-p_1^*)\\
   \widehat{\omega(G)}&={\rm round}\left(\frac{1}{1-2\widehat{\mathrm{OPT}}_G}\right)
   \end{align}
   Viewing $\mathrm{OPT}_G$ as a function of $\omega(G)$,we have that $\mathrm{OPT}_G(\omega+1)-\mathrm{OPT}_G(\omega)=\frac 1 {2\omega(\omega+1)}\gtrsim n^{-2}$. 
   Since $\omega(G)$ is an integer, $\widehat{\omega(G)}=\omega(G)$ if $|\widehat{\mathrm{OPT}}_G-\mathrm{OPT}_G|\leq c n^{-2}$ for small constant $c>0$. 
   Re-arranging \eqref{eq:optG-to-optRhoG}, 
   \begin{align}
       |\widehat{\mathrm{OPT}}_G-\mathrm{OPT}_G|\leq \frac{3}{2p_1^*\lambda }(C\lambda^2 +(n+\lambda |E|)\epsilon )\lesssim \lambda + (n/\lambda+n^2)\epsilon 
   \end{align}
   Thus, taking $\lambda=O(n^{-2})$ and $\epsilon=O(n^{-5})$ suffices. This gives an efficient algorithm for computing $\omega(G)$, which is NP-hard.
<!-- end proof-28 -->
<!-- proof-29: body_md -->
In the Bargmann representation, $\hat{a}_j$ acts as $\partial_{z_j}$ and $\hat{a}_k^\dagger$ acts as multiplication by $z_k$. Differentiating the exponential in \eqref{eq:barg-state}, using the symmetry of $A$, gives
\[
    \partial_{z_j}e^{b^\top z+z^\top Az/2}
       =\left(b_j+\sum_k A_{jk}z_k\right)e^{b^\top z+z^\top Az/2}.
\]
Consequently, with $\hat{a}=(\hat{a}_1,\ldots,\hat{a}_n)^\top$ and $\hat{a}^\dagger$ denoting the column of creation operators,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lf-bargmann-annihilation}
    (a-Aa^\dagger)\ket\psi=b\ket\psi.
\end{equation}
Hitting this whole expression by $\bra{\psi}$ yields $b=\beta-A\bar\beta$. Its conjugate gives $\bar\beta=\bar b+\bar A\beta$, so substitution yields $(I-A\bar A)\beta=b+A\bar b$. Since $A$ is symmetric, $\bar A=A^\dagger$ and $I-A\bar A$ is positive definite, proving the inverse formula in \eqref{eq:lf-displacement-relation}. The triangle and reverse triangle inequalities applied to $\beta-A\bar\beta$ prove \eqref{eq:lf-displacement-bounds}.

We next prove the photon-number formula \eqref{eq:lf-bargmann-energy}. By definition, $\nu(\pi_{b,A})$ is the expectation of the total number operator in the normalized state $\ket\psi$:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lf-photon-expectation}
    \nu(\pi_{b,A})=\tr\left(\pi_{b,A}\sum_{j=1}^n \hat{a}_j^\dagger \hat{a}_j\right)
       =\sum_{j=1}^n\bra\psi \hat{a}_j^\dagger \hat{a}_j\ket\psi
       =\sum_{j=1}^n\|\hat{a}_j\ket\psi\|^2.
\end{equation}
To evaluate this sum, center the annihilation operators by setting $d_j=\hat{a}_j-\beta_j I$. They satisfy $\langle d_j\rangle_\psi=0$ and $[d_j,d_k^\dagger]=\delta_{jk}I$. Now expand $\hat{a}_j=d_j+\beta_j I$:
\begin{align*}
    \bra\psi \hat{a}_j^\dagger \hat{a}_j\ket\psi
       &=\bra\psi d_j^\dagger d_j\ket\psi
         +\beta_j\bra\psi d_j^\dagger\ket\psi
         +\bar\beta_j\bra\psi d_j\ket\psi+|\beta_j|^2\\
       &=\|d_j\ket\psi\|^2+|\beta_j|^2.
\end{align*}
Thus \eqref{eq:lf-photon-expectation} becomes
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lf-centered-photon-number}
    \nu(\pi_{b,A})=\|\beta\|_2^2+\sum_{j=1}^n\|d_j\ket\psi\|^2.
\end{equation}
It remains to show that $\sum_{j=1}^n \|d_j \ket{\psi} \|^2 = \sum_{j=1}^n s_j^2/(1-s_j^2)$.
Substituting $\hat{a}_j = d_j + \beta_j I$ into \eqref{eq:lf-bargmann-annihilation} yields
\[
    (d-Ad^\dagger)\ket\psi=0.
\]
Now, choose a Takagi factorization $A=USU^\top$, where $U$ is unitary and $S=\operatorname{diag}(s_1,\ldots,s_n)$. Define $c=U^\dagger d$, so the column of adjoints is $c^\dagger=U^\top d^\dagger$. Multiplication by $U^\dagger$ therefore gives, for each $j$,
\[
    c_j\ket\psi=s_jc_j^\dagger\ket\psi,
    \qquad [c_j,c_k^\dagger]=\delta_{jk}I.
\]
                                                                                                                                           
                                                                                                                               
Letting $u_j=\|c_j\ket\psi\|^2$, we find that
\[
    u_j=s_j^2\|c_j^\dagger\ket\psi\|^2
       =s_j^2\bra\psi c_jc_j^\dagger\ket\psi
       =s_j^2(u_j+1),
    \qquad u_j=\frac{s_j^2}{1-s_j^2},
\]
where the third equality follows from the fact that $[c_j,c_j^\dagger] = I$. Since $c=U^\dagger d$ and $U$ is unitary, it preserves the norm of the vector $(d_1\ket\psi,\ldots,d_n\ket\psi)$ in $\mathcal F_n^{\oplus n}$. Thus
\[
    \sum_j\|d_j\ket\psi\|^2
       =\sum_j\|c_j\ket\psi\|^2
       =\sum_j u_j
       =\sum_j\frac{s_j^2}{1-s_j^2}.
\]
Substituting this into \eqref{eq:lf-centered-photon-number} proves \eqref{eq:lf-bargmann-energy}.
<!-- end proof-29 -->
<!-- proof-30: body_md -->
\emph{Spectrum of $M(A)$.} Write $A=P+iQ$ and take Takagi vectors $u_j=p_j+iq_j$ as in \eqref{eq:takagi-factorization}. Taking real and imaginary parts of $A\bar u_j=s_j u_j$ gives
\[
    Pp_j+Qq_j=s_jp_j,\qquad Qp_j-Pq_j=s_jq_j.
\]
Direct multiplication by $M(A)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}I-P&Q\\Q&I+P\end{smallmatrix}\right)$ now shows
\[
    M(A)\begin{pmatrix}p_j\\-q_j\end{pmatrix}
       =(1-s_j)\begin{pmatrix}p_j\\-q_j\end{pmatrix},\qquad
    M(A)\begin{pmatrix}q_j\\p_j\end{pmatrix}
       =(1+s_j)\begin{pmatrix}q_j\\p_j\end{pmatrix}.
\]
These $2n$ real vectors form an orthonormal basis: the real and imaginary parts of $u_j^\dagger u_k=\delta_{jk}$ give
\[
    p_j^\top p_k+q_j^\top q_k=\delta_{jk},\qquad
    p_j^\top q_k-q_j^\top p_k=0.
\]
Thus we have exhibited all the eigenvalues of $M(A)$, namely $1-s_j$ and $1+s_j$. They are positive because $s_j\leq\|A\|_{\rm op}<1$.

\emph{Normalization formula.} To express \eqref{eq:lf-normalization-integral} as a real integral, write $z=x+iy$ and $b=b_R+ib_I$, keeping $A=P+iQ$ as above, with all components real. Since $A$ is symmetric, so are $P$ and $Q$, and expansion gives
\[
    \Re(z^\top Az)=x^\top Px-y^\top Py-2x^\top Qy,
    \qquad \Re(b^\top z)=b_R^\top x-b_I^\top y.
\]
Consequently, for $w=(x^\top,y^\top)^\top$, the exponent in \eqref{eq:lf-normalization-integral} is
\begin{align*}
    &-x^\top(I-P)x-y^\top(I+P)y-2x^\top Qy
       +2b_R^\top x-2b_I^\top y\\
    &\hspace{2em}=-w^\top M(A)w+2u(b)^\top w.
\end{align*}
Here $d^{2n}z=dx\,dy=dw$: we are simply identifying $\mathbb{C}^n$ with $\mathbb{R}^{2n}$. Hence
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lf-real-normalization-integral}
    Z(b,A)=\pi^{-n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}
        e^{-w^\top M(A)w+2u(b)^\top w}\,dw.
\end{equation}

Abbreviating $M=M(A)$ and $u=u(b)$, complete the square:
\[
    -w^\top Mw+2u^\top w
       =-(w-M^{-1}u)^\top M(w-M^{-1}u)+u^\top M^{-1}u.
\]
Translating by $v=w-M^{-1}u$ and using the standard Gaussian integral for $M\succ0$ gives
\[
    Z(b,A)=\pi^{-n}e^{u^\top M^{-1}u}
       \int_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}e^{-v^\top Mv}\,dv
       =\frac{e^{u^\top M^{-1}u}}{\sqrt{\det M}}.
\]
Taking logarithms proves \eqref{eq:lf-normalization-formula}.
<!-- end proof-30 -->
<!-- proof-31: body_md -->
We perform a heterodyne measurement on each copy to obtain samples from a distribution $P$ that is ${\mathrm{opt}_{\rm tr}}$-close in total variation distance to the classical Gaussian $\mathcal N(\mu_h,\Sigma_h)$ where $\mu_h={\bf m}$ and $\Sigma_h=\frac{V+I}{2}$. Taking $\overline{\mathrm{opt}}_{\rm tr}=\overline{\mathrm{opt}}_{\rm TV}$, we apply Proposition~\ref{prop:robust-gaussian-learner} to obtain estimates $\hat \mu_h$ and $\hat \Sigma_h$ which satisfy the relative $\ell_2$ and Frobenius guarantees in \eqref{eq:robust-gaussian-learner-relative-guarantee-F}.
    Then, we construct the estimator $\hat V\equiv \frac{2\hat \Sigma_h}{1-\zeta}-I$. 
    
    Now, from the relative Frobenius guarantee in \eqref{eq:robust-gaussian-learner-relative-guarantee-F} for $\hat \mu_h$ and $\hat \Sigma_h$, we have that $(1-\zeta)\Sigma_h\preceq \hat \Sigma_h\preceq (1+\zeta)\Sigma_h$. Applying this, 
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:rht-V-err-psd}
        0\preceq \hat V -V \preceq \frac{4\zeta}{1-\zeta}\Sigma_h\preceq \frac{16}{3}\zeta \Sigma_h 
    \end{equation}
    where the last inequality follows from $\zeta \leq 1/4$. This means that $\hat V+i\Omega \succeq V+i\Omega \succeq 0$, so $\pi(\hat \mu_h, \hat V)$ is a valid Gaussian state. Then, by Fact~\ref{fact:trd-to-param}, 
    \begin{align}
        d_{\rm tr}(\pi,\hat \pi)&\lesssim \|V^{-1/2}(\hat \mu_h -\mu_h)\|_2+\tr ((V^{-1}+\hat V^{-1})|\hat V-V|)\\
        &\leq \|V^{-1/2}\Sigma_h^{1/2}\|_{\rm op}\|\Sigma_h^{-1/2}(\hat \mu_h-\mu_h)\|_2+2\tr(V^{-1}(\hat V-V))\\
        &\lesssim \zeta \|V^{-1/2}\Sigma_h^{1/2}\|_{\rm op}+\zeta \tr (V^{-1}\Sigma_h)\\
        &\lesssim \zeta(n+\tr V^{-1})
    \end{align}
    where the second term in the second inequality comes from $\hat V^{-1}\preceq V^{-1}$ and where the fourth inequality uses the bound $\|V^{-1/2}\Sigma_h^{1/2}\|_{\rm op}\leq \|V^{-1/2}\Sigma_h^{1/2}\|_F=\sqrt{\tr(V^{-1}\Sigma_h)}$ and the fact that $\tr(V^{-1}\Sigma_h)=n+\frac 1 2 \tr V^{-1}$. Then, \eqref{eq:rht-bound} follows by triangle inequality:
    \begin{equation}
        d_{\rm tr}(\rho,\hat \pi)\leq d_{\rm tr}(\rho,\pi)+d_{\rm tr}(\pi,\hat \pi)\mathrel{\lesssim}{\mathrm{opt}_{\rm tr}} +\zeta (n+\tr V^{-1})\lesssim (n+\tr V^{-1})\cdot \Tilde{O}({\mathrm{opt}_{\rm tr}})\lesssim E(\pi)\cdot \Tilde{O}({\mathrm{opt}_{\rm tr}})
    \end{equation}
    where the final inequality comes from $\tr V^{-1}=\tr(\Omega V^{-1}\Omega^\top)\leq \tr V \leq 4 E(\pi)$, with the second inequality coming from taking the Schur complement of the top right block of $V+i\Omega \succeq 0$.
<!-- end proof-31 -->
<!-- proof-32: body_md -->
Lemmas 12 and 13 in \citep{bittel2025energy} prove the following unsqueezing property which substantiates the whole algorithm in Theorem~\ref{thm:eit}:
    \begin{equation}
    \|V_{k+1}^{-1}\|_{\rm op}\leq \sqrt{1+\frac{2\zeta}{1-\zeta}(\|V_k^{-1}\|_\infty+1)}\leq \sqrt{\frac 5 3 +\frac 2 3 \|V^{-1}\|_\infty}
    \Rightarrow 
    \|V_K^{-1}\|_{\rm op}\leq \sqrt{7/3}\cdot \|V^{-1}\|_{\rm op}^{1/2^K}
    \end{equation}
    Observing the proofs, we notice that the two sufficient claims for our purposes are showing that both $0\preceq \hat V_k-V_k\preceq \frac{2\zeta}{1-\zeta}(V_k+I)$ and $\zeta \leq 1/4$ hold for our robust estimators. Luckily, this is immediate. First, unitary invariance preserves the trace-distance bound $d_{\rm tr}(\rho_k,\pi_k)\leq\mathrm{opt}_{\rm tr}\leq \overline{\mathrm{opt}}_{\rm tr}=\overline{\mathrm{opt}}_{\rm TV}$ across iterations, meaning these two conditions directly follow from \eqref{eq:rht-V-err-psd} and Proposition~\ref{prop:robust-gaussian-learner}. The claimed copy complexity then comes from the $O(n^2 {\mathrm{opt}_{\rm tr}}^{-2}\log^51/\delta)$ copies needed by each usage of robust heterodyne tomography.
<!-- end proof-32 -->
<!-- proof-33: body_md -->
We begin by converting the absolute error guarantees of \eqref{eq:frob-h-to-trd-hypoth} for the heterodyne outcome distribution into the following relative guarantees on the raw, uninflated parameter estimates for the state:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:riuproj-V-relf}
     \|V^{-1/2}(\hat {\bf m}-{\bf m})\|_2\leq \sqrt 2 \zeta \quad {\rm and }\quad  \|V^{-1/2}(\hat V_{\rm raw}-V)V^{-1/2}\|_{F}\leq 4\zeta 
\end{equation}
To show this, we first recall that the eigenvalues of a pure Gaussian state's covariance $V=SS^\top $ come in pairs of the form $(\lambda,1/\lambda)$, meaning $\|V^{-1}\|_{\rm op}\leq 2$ implies $I/2\preceq V \preceq 2I $ and $0\preceq \Sigma_h\preceq 3V/2$. Then, by standard properties of $\|\cdot\|_{\rm op}$, 
\begin{align}
    \|V^{-1/2}(\hat {\bf m}-{\bf m})\|_2&\leq \sqrt 2 \zeta \\
    \|V^{-1/2}(\hat V_{\rm raw}-V)V^{-1/2}\|_{F}&\leq 2\|\hat V_{\rm raw}-V\|_F=4\|\hat \Sigma_h-\Sigma_h\|_F\leq 4\zeta 
\end{align}
proving \eqref{eq:riuproj-V-relf}. This gives $\hat V_{\rm raw}\succeq(1/2-2\zeta)I\succ0$ for $\zeta<1/4$ and $\|\hat V_{\rm raw}-V\|_F \leq 2\zeta $.

We now prove the key inequality, $\|\hat V-\hat V_{\rm raw}\|_F\lesssim \zeta $, which argues that removing the diagonal matrix in $\hat V_{\rm raw}=\hat S\hat D\hat S^\top $ perturbs the estimate by $O(\zeta)$ in Frobenius norm. Manipulating,
\begin{align}\label{eq:riuproj-proj-frob-err-1}
    \|\hat V_{\rm raw}-\hat V\|_F=\|\hat S(\hat D-I)\hat S^\top \|_F \leq \|\hat S\|_{\rm op}^2 \|\hat D-I\|_F=\|\hat S\hat S^\top \|_{\rm op}\|\hat D-I\|_F 
\end{align}

We do this by taking inspiration from \citep{chen2026towards} and introducing the auxiliary matrix $A(W)\equiv -\Omega W\Omega W$, where $W$ is any real positive-definite matrix. Since $\hat S$ and $S$ are symplectic, $\Omega \hat S =\hat S^{-\top}\Omega$ and $\Omega S=S^{-\top }\Omega$. Then, 
\begin{align}
    A(\hat V_{\rm raw})&=-\Omega \hat S \hat D \hat S^\top \Omega \hat S\hat D \hat S^\top =-\hat S^{-\top }\Omega \hat D \Omega \hat D \hat S^{\top }=\hat S^{-\top }\hat D^2 \hat S^\top 
\end{align}
where we use the fact that $\Omega^2=-I$ and $\hat D, \Omega $ share the same block structure and commute. A similar calculation gives that $A(V)=S^{-\top } I^2S^\top=I $, meaning $A(\hat V_{\rm raw})-A(V)=\hat S^{-\top }(\hat D^2-I)\hat S^{\top }$. Then, writing the symplectic eigenvalues of $\hat V_{\rm raw}$, $\hat D={\rm diag}(\hat \lambda_1,\dots,\hat \lambda_n,\hat \lambda_1,\dots,\hat \lambda_n)$ and both $A(\hat V_{\rm raw })-A(V)$ and $\hat D^2-I$ have the eigenvalues $\{\hat\lambda_1^2-1,\dots,\hat \lambda_n^2-1,\hat \lambda_1^2-1,\dots,\hat \lambda_n^2-1\}$. Then, since $\hat V_{\rm raw}\succ 0$ implies $\hat \lambda_i>0$, 
\begin{align}
\|\hat D-I\|_F^2&=2\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\hat \lambda_i-1)^2\leq 2\sum_{i=1}^n(\hat \lambda_i^2-1)^2=\sum_{j=1}^{2n}|\lambda_j(A(\hat V_0)-A(V))|^2\\
&\leq \|A(\hat V_{\rm raw})-A(V)\|_F^2\\
&\leq \|-\Omega (\hat V_{\rm raw}-V) \Omega V-\Omega V \Omega (\hat V_{\rm raw}-V) -\Omega (\hat V_{\rm raw}-V) \Omega (\hat V_{\rm raw}-V)\|_{F}^2\\
    &\leq (2\|V\|_{\rm op}\|\hat V_{\rm raw}-V\|_{F}+\|\hat V_{\rm raw}-V\|_{F}^2)^2\\
    &\leq 9^2 \zeta^2 \label{eq:riuproj-D-I-frob}
\end{align}
Here, the first line uses the elementary facts that $|x-1|\leq |x^2-1|$ for any $x>0$ and $\sum_j |\lambda_j(M)|^2\leq \|M\|_F^2$ for any square matrix $M$. The remainder of the manipulation proceeds from the prior absolute bound $\|\hat V_{\rm raw}-V\|_F\leq 2\zeta $ and $\zeta < 1/4$. 

We now switch our focus to the first term of \eqref{eq:riuproj-proj-frob-err-1}. Taking $\zeta < 1/18$, from \eqref{eq:riuproj-D-I-frob}, $\hat D \succeq (1-9\zeta)I\succeq I/2$ and $\hat V_{\rm raw}\preceq V+2\zeta I\leq 3I$, from $\|\hat V_{\rm raw}-V\|_F\leq 2\zeta$ and $V\preceq 2I$. Then, 
\begin{align}\label{eq:riuproj-sst-psd-ub}
    \hat S \hat S^\top \leq 2 \hat S \hat D \hat S^\top =2\hat V_{\rm raw}\preceq 6I
\end{align}
Substituting \eqref{eq:riuproj-sst-psd-ub} and \eqref{eq:riuproj-D-I-frob} into \eqref{eq:riuproj-proj-frob-err-1}, we have that $\|\hat V_{\rm raw}-\hat V\|_F\lesssim \zeta$. Then, by triangle inequality, 
\begin{align}
    \|\hat V-V\|_F\leq \|\hat V-\hat V_{\rm raw}\|_F+\|\hat V_{\rm raw}-V\|_F \lesssim \zeta+2\zeta\lesssim \zeta 
\end{align}
meaning $\|V^{-1/2}(\hat V-V)V^{-1/2}\|_F\lesssim \zeta $ since $I/2\preceq V$. Then, this relative bound on the covariance combined with the relative bound on the mean in \eqref{eq:riuproj-V-relf} gives that $d_{\rm tv}(\mathcal N ({\bf m},V/2),\mathcal N (\hat {\bf m},\hat V/2))\lesssim \zeta$ as well. Applying Lemma B.3 of \citep{chen2026towards} to translate this closeness in phase space to trace distance closeness between the two states,
\begin{equation}
    d_{\rm tr}(\ketbra{\psi},\ketbra*{\hat\psi})\leq 150\cdotd_{\rm tv}(\mathcal{N}({\bf m},V/2),\mathcal{N}(\hat {\bf m},\hat V/2))\lesssim \zeta
\end{equation}
provided $\zeta$ is sufficiently small such that this total variation distance is less than $1/600$.
<!-- end proof-33 -->
<!-- proof-34: body_md -->
WLOG, by multilinearity, $h_1,\dots,h_k$ each have unit norm $\|\cdot \|_{ \mathcal P}=1$. We work with the unnormalized vector $\ket{\Phi(\theta)}=e^{K_\theta}\ket{0}$ for now. Since $K_\theta$ is linear in $\theta$ and all creation operators commute,
\begin{align}
{e^{K_{\theta+th}}}&=e^{K_\theta+tK_h}=e^{K_\theta}e^{tK_h}\\
   \ket{ D\Phi(\theta)[h_1]}&=\frac{d}{dt}\bigg|_{t=0}e^{K_{\theta +th_1}}\ket 0={K_{h_1}}e^{K_\theta }\ket 0
   \label{eq:D-Phi-single}
   \\
    \ket{D^k \Phi(\theta)[h_1,\dots,h_k]}&=K_{h_1}\cdots K_{h_k}e^{K_\theta}{\ket0}=\sum_{j=0}^\infty\frac{K_{h_1}\cdots K_{h_k}K_\theta^j\ket0}{j!} \label{eq:Dk-Phi-series}
\end{align}
Focusing on bounding this numerator, we sequentially apply Lemma~\ref{eq:Ktheta-growth} to the $j+k$ operators in the product, recalling that each term increases the total photon count by at most two:
\begin{align}
    \|K_{h_1}\cdots K_{h_k}K_\theta^j\ket0\|_2
    &{\leq}\left(\prod_{s=0}^{j+k-1}2(2s+2)\right)\|\theta\|_{\mathcal P}^j\cancel{\prod_{i=1}^k\|h_i\|_{\mathcal P}}\\
    &=4^{j+k}(j+k)!\|\theta\|_{\mathcal P}^j\label{eq:creator-product}
\end{align}
Applying triangle inequality to \eqref{eq:Dk-Phi-series} and then using \eqref{eq:creator-product} to bound each term, since $\|\theta\|_{\mathcal P}\leq 1/8$, 
\begin{align}\label{eq:Dk-l2}
    \| \ket{D^k \Phi(\theta)[h_1,\dots,h_k]}\|_2 \leq 4^k k! \sum_{j=0}^\infty \binom{j+k}{k}(4\|\theta\|_{\mathcal P})^j = \frac {4^k k!} {(1-4r)^{k+1}}\leq 2\cdot 8^k k
\end{align}
where the equality uses the generalized binomial theorem $\sum_{j= 0}^\infty\binom{j+k}{k}x^j=(1-x)^{-k-1}$ for $|x|<1$. 

We now revive the normalization $Z(\theta)=\braket{\Phi(\theta)}{\Phi(\theta)}$ and use the multivariable product rule for the Fr\`echet derivative:
\begin{align}
   |D^k Z(\theta)[h_1,\dots,h_k]|&=\left|\sum_{S\subseteq [k]} \braket{D^{|S|}\Phi(\theta)[h_i:i\in S] }{D^{k-|S|}\Phi(\theta)[h_i:i\not\in S]}\right|\\
   &\leq \sum_{j=0}^k \binom{k}{j}\left\|\bra{D^j\Phi(\theta)}\right\|_{\rm op}\left\|\ket{D^{k-j}\Phi(\theta)}\right\|_{\rm op}\\
   &{\leq}\sum_{j=0}^k \binom{k}{j}(2\cdot 8^j j! ) (2\cdot 8^{k-j}(k-j)!)\\
   &=4\cdot 8^k  (k+1)! \label{eq:Dk-Z-bound}
\end{align}
where we group by $|S|=j$ in the second line after triangle inequality and also apply \eqref{eq:Dk-l2} in the third line, with $\|\cdot \|_{\rm op}$ denoting the operator norm with respect to $\|\cdot \|_{\mathcal P}$. 

We now analyze $N(\theta)=g\circ Z$ where $g(x)=x^{-1/2}$. Since $Z\geq 1$ from the vacuum term, we restrict to $x\geq 1$ and can bound $|g^{(m)}(x)|=x^{-m-1/2}\prod_{j=0}^{m-1}(j+1/2)\leq m!$. 
Then, applying the higher-order chain rule for the Fr\`echet derivative \citep{constantine1996multivariate} along with \eqref{eq:Dk-Z-bound} and $|g^{(m)}(x)|\leq m!$, 
\begin{align}
    |D^k N(\theta)[h_1,\dots,h_k]|
    &=\left |\sum_{\mathcal S\in\mathfrak P_k}g^{(|\mathcal S|)}(Z(\theta))
      \prod_{S\in\mathcal S}D^{|S|}Z(\theta)[h_i:i\in S]\right|\\
      &\leq \sum_{\mathcal S\in\mathfrak P_k}\underbrace{|g^{(|\mathcal S|)}(Z(\theta))|}_{\leq |\mathcal S|!}
      \prod_{S\in\mathcal S}\underbrace{\|D^{|S|}Z(\theta)\|_{\rm op}}_{4\cdot 8^{|S|} (|S|+1)!}\\
      &\leq \sum_{j=1}^k 4^j\sum_{\mathcal S\in\mathfrak P_k: |\mathcal S|=j} j! \prod_{S\in \mathcal S } 16^{|S|}|S|!\\
      &\leq 16^k\sum_{j=1}^k 4^j\underbrace{\sum_{\mathcal  S\in \mathfrak P_k:|\mathcal S|=j}j!\prod_{S\in \mathcal S} |S|!}_{k!\binom{k-1}{j-1}}\\
      &\leq 16^k k! \cdot 4\cdot 5^{k-1}\\
     \|D^k N(\theta)\|_{\rm op} &\leq 80^k k! \label{eq:Dk-N-op}
\end{align}
where ${\mathfrak P}_k$ is the set of partitions of $[k]$. The bracketed term in the fourth line simplifies by noticing that both quantities count the number of ways to group $k$ distinguishable elements into $j$ groups, either by enumerating over all partitions of size $j$ and permuting within each group and then permuting the groups themselves, or alternatively permuting all the elements and then choosing the $j-1$ cuts that define the groups. The fifth line follows from the binomial theorem ${\sum_{r=1}^{k}\binom{k-1}{r-1}x^r=x(1+x)^{k-1}}$ with $x=4$.

Then, since $\ket{\psi(\theta)}=N\ket{\Phi(\theta)}$, we apply the multivariable product rule one last time:
\begin{align}
    \|\ket{D^k \psi(\theta)[h_1,\dots,h_k]}\|_{ 2}&=\left\|\sum_{S\subseteq [k]}D^{|S|}N(\theta)[h_i:i\in S] \cdot \ket{D^{k-|S|}\Phi(\theta)[h_i:i\not\in S]}\right\|_{2}\\
    &\leq {\sum_{j=0}^k}\binom{k}{j}\underbrace{\|D^{j}N(\theta)\|_{\rm op}}_{{\leq}80^j j!}\underbrace{{\|D^{k-j}\Phi(\theta)\|_{\rm op}}}_{{\leq}2\cdot 8^{k-j}(k-j)!}\\
    &\leq 2\cdot 88^k k! 
\end{align}
where we use \eqref{eq:Dk-l2}, \eqref{eq:Dk-N-op}, and binomial theorem for $(80+8)^k $. Thus, the lemma holds for $C_D= 176$ or better.
<!-- end proof-34 -->
<!-- proof-35: body_md -->
Let $e_1,\dots,e_{d_{\mathcal P}}$ be a real orthonormal basis for $\mathcal P $, where $d_{\mathcal P}=n^2+3n$. Taking $h=\kappa/(3\sqrt d) $, estimate $f_\rho(\cdot)$ at each of $\theta,\theta+he_1,\dots,\theta+h e_d$ in $B_R$ to accuracy $\kappa_0\equiv \kappa^2/(12d)$ and failure probability $\delta/(d+1)$. Then, construct the zeroth-order estimator:
    \begin{align}\label{eq:hf-finite-difference}
        \widehat{\nabla f_\rho}(\theta)
      =\sum_{i=1}^d\frac{\hat f_\rho(\theta+he_i)-\hat f_\rho(\theta)}{h}\,e_i.
    \end{align}
    Then, by the fundamental theorem of calculus and \eqref{eq:convex-smooth}, 
\begin{align}
    f_\rho(\theta+he_i)&=f_\rho(\theta)+hD f_\rho(\theta)[e_i]+\int_0^h (h-t)g''(t) dt \\
    \left|\frac{f_\rho (\theta+he_i)-f_\rho(\theta)}{h}-D f_\rho(\theta)[e_i]\right|&=\frac 1 h \int_0^h (h-t)D^2 f_\rho(\theta+te_i)[e_i,e_i]dt\leq \frac 3 h \int_0^h (h-t)dt =\frac {3h}{2}
\end{align}
Since estimating $f_\rho(\cdot)$ contributes at most $2\kappa_0/h$ error to the zeroth-order estimate in each coordinate, by triangle inequality, 
\begin{align}
    \|\widehat{\nabla f_\rho}(\theta)-\nabla f_\rho(\theta)\|_{\mathcal P}
    &\leq\sqrt d\left(\frac{3h}{2}+\frac{2\kappa_0}{h}\right)
      =\kappa.
\end{align}
The copy complexity and failure probability are immediate.
<!-- end proof-35 -->
<!-- proof-36: body_md -->
The true precision $K=\Sigma^{-1}$ belongs to $\mathcal K_A$.
Moreover, $\Sigma\succeq I/2$, $\hat\Sigma\succeq\Sigma/t_\alpha$,
and $\|\hat\Sigma-\Sigma\|_F\leq At_\alpha^2$. The resolvent identity
therefore gives
\[
 \|\hat K-K\|_F\leq
 \|\hat\Sigma^{-1}\|_{\rm op}
 \|\hat\Sigma-\Sigma\|_F\|\Sigma^{-1}\|_{\rm op}
 \lesssim At_\alpha^3.
\]
Projection optimality and the triangle inequality imply
$\|K_0-K\|_F\lesssim At_\alpha^3$. The spectral constraints give
$\|K_0^{-1}\|_{\rm op}\lesssim A$ and
$\|V_0^{\pm1}\|_{\rm op}\lesssim A$. Since
$\|K^{-1}\|_{\rm op}=\|\Sigma\|_{\rm op}\leq A$, we obtain
\begin{align*}
 \|V_0-V\|_F
 &=2\|K_0^{-1}(K-K_0)K^{-1}\|_F
       \lesssim A^3t_\alpha^3,\\
 \|Z-I\|_F
 &=\|S_0^{-1}(V-V_0)S_0^{-\top}\|_F
       \lesssim A^4t_\alpha^3,
 \qquad Z=S_0^{-1}VS_0^{-\top}.
\end{align*}
For the transformed mean $e=S_0^{-1}({\bf m}-\hat\mu)$, the mean
guarantee similarly gives
\[
 \|e\|_2^2\leq\|V_0^{-1}\|_{\rm op}\|{\bf m}-\hat\mu\|_2^2
 \lesssim A^2t_\alpha(1+t_\alpha).
\]
The eigenvalues of the pure covariance $Z$ occur in reciprocal pairs.
For each pair, writing $x=\lambda+\lambda^{-1}-2\geq0$ gives
\[
 (\lambda-1)^2+(\lambda^{-1}-1)^2=x^2+2x\geq2x.
\]
Thus $\tr Z-2n\leq\|Z-I\|_F^2/2$. The transformed photon number is
therefore bounded by
\[
 \nu=\frac{\tr Z-2n}{4}+\frac{\|e\|_2^2}{2}
 \lesssim t_\alpha^6A^8+t_\alpha(1+t_\alpha)A^2
 \leq u_\alpha A^8.
\]
For example, the preceding estimates give
$\|Z-I\|_F\leq96A^4t_\alpha^3$ and
$\|e\|_2^2\leq3A^2t_\alpha(1+t_\alpha)$, so the stated value of $u_\alpha$ suffices.
<!-- end proof-36 -->
<!-- proof-37: body_md -->
Conjugating $N_G+N_H$ by $G$ gives $N+UNU^\dagger$, where
\[
    UNU^\dagger
       =\frac12(\hat{\mathbf R}-\mathbf d)^\top C^{-1}
                    (\hat{\mathbf R}-\mathbf d)-\frac n2 I.
\]
Set
\[
    M=I+C^{-1},\qquad
    u=M^{-1}C^{-1}\mathbf d=(I+C)^{-1}\mathbf d.
\]
Expanding the quadratic terms and using $Mu=C^{-1}\mathbf d$ gives
\begin{align*}
    N+UNU^\dagger
       &=\frac12\hat{\mathbf R}^\top M\hat{\mathbf R}
          -\mathbf d^\top C^{-1}\hat{\mathbf R}
          +\frac12\mathbf d^\top C^{-1}\mathbf d-nI\\
       &=\frac12(\hat{\mathbf R}-u)^\top M(\hat{\mathbf R}-u)
          +\frac12\mathbf d^\top
             [C^{-1}-C^{-1}M^{-1}C^{-1}]\mathbf d-nI\\
       &=\frac12(\hat{\mathbf R}-u)^\top M(\hat{\mathbf R}-u)
          +\frac12\mathbf d^\top(I+C)^{-1}\mathbf d-nI.
\end{align*}
Here scalar terms multiply the identity. For the last equality, use
$M^{-1}=(I+C)^{-1}C$ and
$I-(I+C)^{-1}=C(I+C)^{-1}$ to obtain
\[
    C^{-1}-C^{-1}M^{-1}C^{-1}
       =C^{-1}[I-(I+C)^{-1}]=(I+C)^{-1}.
\]
A displacement removes $u$ without changing the spectrum.
A passive change of modes diagonalizes the pure covariance $C$ into
reciprocal pairs $(e^{2r_j},e^{-2r_j})$. The corresponding symplectic eigenvalues of $M=I+C^{-1}$ are
\[
    \omega_j=\sqrt{(1+e^{2r_j})(1+e^{-2r_j})}=2\cosh r_j.
\]
The Gaussian transformation obtained from the Williamson decomposition of $M$ turns the centered quadratic Hamiltonian into
$\sum_j\omega_j(\hat{a}_j^\dagger \hat{a}_j+1/2)$, whose ground energy is
$\tfrac12\sum_j\omega_j=\sum_j\cosh r_j$.
Adding the scalar terms above proves \eqref{eq:lfpr-compatibility-formula}.
<!-- end proof-37 -->
<!-- proof-38: body_md -->
The domain is the intersection of three norm balls in the real vector space $\mathcal P_n$, so it is convex. Its operator-norm constraint ensures $\|A\|_{\rm op}<1$, as required for the Bargmann state.

Suppose first that $\nu(\pi_{b,A})\leq h$. Each term in \eqref{eq:lf-bargmann-energy} is nonnegative, so $\|\beta\|_2^2\leq h$ and $s_j^2/(1-s_j^2)\leq h$ for every $j$. Rearranging the latter inequality gives $s_j^2\leq h/(h+1)$, and therefore $\|A\|_{\rm op}\leq r_h$. Moreover,
\[
    \|A\|_F^2=\sum_j s_j^2
       \leq\sum_j\frac{s_j^2}{1-s_j^2}\leq h,
    \qquad
    \|b\|_2\leq(1+\|A\|_{\rm op})\|\beta\|_2
       \leq2\sqrt h.
\]
These are precisely the three constraints defining $\mathcal{D}_h$.

Conversely, suppose $(b,A)\in\mathcal{D}_h$. Since $s_j\leq r_h$ and $1-r_h^2=1/(h+1)$,
\[
    \sum_j\frac{s_j^2}{1-s_j^2}
       \leq\frac{\sum_j s_j^2}{1-r_h^2}
       =\frac{\|A\|_F^2}{1-r_h^2}
       \leq h(h+1).
\]
Moreover, \eqref{eq:lf-displacement-bounds} and the identity
\[
    \frac1{1-r_h}=\frac{1+r_h}{1-r_h^2}
       =(1+r_h)(h+1)\leq2(h+1)
\]
give
\[
    \|\beta\|_2^2
       \leq\frac{\|b\|_2^2}{(1-r_h)^2}
       \leq16h(h+1)^2.
\]
The conclusion immediately follows.
<!-- end proof-38 -->
<!-- proof-39: body_md -->
\emph{Estimate matrix entries}
Let $D=D_K$ and let $\ket{e_1},\ldots,\ket{e_D}$ be the canonical orthonormal basis for the cutoff space. For each $i$ measure $E_i=\ket{e_i}\bra{e_i}$, and for each $i<j$ measure
\[
    R_{ij}=\frac{\ket{e_i}\bra{e_j}+\ket{e_j}\bra{e_i}}2,
    \qquad
    J_{ij}=\frac{\mathrm i\ket{e_i}\bra{e_j}-\mathrm i\ket{e_j}\bra{e_i}}2.
\]
The expectations of these observables on $\sigma$ are, respectively, $(\sigma_K)_{ii}$, $\Re(\sigma_K)_{ij}$, and $\Im(\sigma_K)_{ij}$. Their measurement outcomes lie in $[-1,1]$.

There are $D+2\binom D2=D^2$ quantities to estimate. Let $\delta=\epsilon/(64D)$ and measure each observable on
\[
    m=\left\lceil2\delta^{-2}\log(2D^2/\eta)\right\rceil
\]
fresh copies. Hoeffding's inequality bounds the error probability for each empirical mean by $2e^{-m\delta^2/2}\leq\eta/D^2$, so by the union bound all errors are at most $\delta$ with probability at least $1-\eta$.

Let $\tilde\sigma_K$ be defined using these empirical estimates, and make it Hermitian by taking conjugates. On this event the diagonal errors have magnitude at most $\delta$, and the squared magnitude of each off-diagonal error is at most $2\delta^2$. Hence
\[
    \|\tilde\sigma_K-\sigma_K\|_F^2
       \leq D\delta^2+2D(D-1)\delta^2
       \leq2D^2\delta^2,
    \qquad
    \|\tilde\sigma_K-\sigma_K\|_F\leq\epsilon/32.
\]
The copy count is $D^2m=O(D^4\epsilon^{-2}\log(2D/\eta))$.

\emph{Project onto set of subnormalized states}
Let $\hat\sigma_K$ be a nearest matrix to $\tilde\sigma_K$ in Frobenius norm in the closed convex set
\[
    \mathcal S_D=\{X=X^\dagger:X\succeq0,\ \tr X\leq1\}.
\]
It is not hard to see that this projection can be computed by diagonalizing $\tilde\sigma_K$ and projecting its eigenvalues onto $\{x\in\mathbb{R}^D:x_i\geq0,\ \sum_i x_i\leq1\}$. Since $\sigma_K\in\mathcal S_D$, $\tilde\sigma_K$ is at most $\epsilon/32$ from $\mathcal S_D$, so $\hat \sigma_K$ is at most $\epsilon/16$ from $\sigma_K$.
<!-- end proof-39 -->
<!-- proof-40: body_md -->
From Lemma 7 of \citep{wu2019chebyshev} and 2.11.1 of \citep{timan2014theory}, there exist two unit mean random variables on $\{0\}\cup [1+\nu,\lambda]$who match the first $k$ moments and have:
\begin{align}
    \Pr[U_+>0]-\Pr[U_->0]=\inf_{p\in \mathcal P_L}\sup_{x\in [1,Ck^2]}|1/x-p(x)|&=\frac{\left(1+\sqrt{\frac {1+\nu}{\lambda}}\right)^2}{1+\nu}\left(1-\frac{2\sqrt{\frac {1+\nu}{\lambda}}}{1+\sqrt{\frac {1+\nu}{\lambda}}}\right)^k\\
    &=(1+\lambda^{-1/2})^2\left(1-\frac{2}{1+\sqrt \lambda}\right)^k
\end{align}
where $\mathcal P_L$ is the set of real polynomials of degree at most $L$ and where we take $\nu=0$ and $\lambda=Ck^2$.                  Bounding, 
\begin{align}
    \Pr[U_+>0]-\Pr[U_->0]=(1+\lambda^{-1/2})^2\left(1-\frac{2}{1+\sqrt \lambda}\right)^k\geq 1-\frac{2k}{1+\sqrt \lambda }\geq 1-\frac{2}{\sqrt C}\equiv c
\end{align}
for sufficiently large $C$.
<!-- end proof-40 -->
<!-- proof-41: body_md -->
We begin with the one-mode setting where $b,A\in \mathbb{C}$. Since $p_1(\ket{\psi})$ does not change if we rotate $\ket{\psi}\mapsto e^{-i\theta \hat{a}^\dagger \hat{a}}\ket{\psi}=\ket*{\psi(e^{-i\theta}b,e^{-2i\theta}A)}$, WLOG, we can take $b>0$ and $A\in \mathbb{C}$. Identifying $b=\beta$ and $A=\alpha$, we see then that all pure Gaussian states are in the family, and using \eqref{eq:barg-state-expand} and \eqref{eq:Z(beta,alpha)},
        \begin{align}\label{eq:p1-bounding}
        p_1(\ket{\psi})=\frac{\beta^2}{Z(\beta,\alpha)}=\beta^2\sqrt{1-|\alpha|^2}\exp(-\frac{\beta^2(1+\Re \alpha)}{1-|\alpha|^2})&\leq \beta^2\sqrt{1-|\alpha|^2}\exp(-\frac{\beta^2\cancel{(1-|\alpha|)}}{\cancel{(1-|\alpha|)}(1+|\alpha|)})\\
        &\leq e^{-1}(1+|\alpha|)\sqrt{1-|\alpha|^2}\\
        &\leq p_1^*
        \end{align}
        where the first inequality uses $\Re \alpha \geq -|\alpha|$, the second inequality uses the fact that $te^{-t/a}$ is maximized at $t=a$, and third inequality uses the fact that $(1+t)\sqrt{1-t^2}$ is maximized at $t=1/2$. Then, equality throughout gives that $\Re \alpha=-|\alpha|=-1/2$ and $\beta^2=1+|\alpha|=3/2$, meaning $(\beta,\alpha)=(\beta^*,\alpha^*)$. 
        
        We now show the desired stability bound near the maximizer, beginning with stability in parameter distance. Packaging $\theta=(\beta,\Re \alpha,\Im \alpha)\in \mathbb{R}^3$ and $\theta^*=(\beta^*,\alpha^*,0)$, define the objective:
        \begin{align}
        -\log p_1(\ket\psi)\equiv f(\theta)&=-2\log \theta_1 -\frac 1 2 \log (1-\theta_2^2-\theta_3^2)+\frac{\beta^2(1+\theta_2)}{1-\theta_2^2-\theta_3^2}
        \end{align}
        Differentiating, one can verify that $\nabla f(\theta^*)=0$, $\nabla^2 f(\theta^*)=\begin{bmatrix}
        8/3&4\sqrt6/9&0\\
        4\sqrt6/9&28/9&0\\
        0&0&4
    \end{bmatrix}\succeq \frac{16}{9}I$, and every entry of $\nabla^2 f(\theta)$ is continuous when $\theta_1>0$ and $\theta_2^2+\theta_3^2<1$. Then, by continuity of the Hessian and $p(\theta)$, there exists a universal constant $\xi$ for which any $\theta$ with $\|\theta-\theta^*\|_2< \xi$ has $\nabla^2 f(\theta)\succeq I$ and $p_1(\theta)\geq p_1^*/2$. For now, restrict $\theta$ to this ball. Using this and the fundamental theorem of calculus applied twice,
    \begin{align}
       f(\theta)-f(\theta^*)&=\int_0^1(1-t)(\theta-\theta^*)^\top \nabla^2 f(\theta^*+t(\theta-\theta^*))(\theta-\theta^*)dt \geq \int_0^1 (1-t)\|\theta-\theta^*\|_2^2dt\\
       &\geq \frac 1 2 \|\theta-\theta^*\|_2^2
    \end{align}
    This then gives stability in parameter distance:
    \begin{align}\label{eq:p1-param-stable}
        |\beta-\beta^*|^2+|\alpha-\alpha^*|^2\leq f(\theta)-f(\theta^*)=\log \frac{p_1^*}{p_1(\ket{\psi})}\leq \frac{p_1^*-p_1(\ket{\psi})}{p_1(\ket{\psi})}\lesssim p_1^*-p_1(\ket{\psi})
    \end{align}
    where use the fact that $\log t \leq t-1$ and $p_1(\theta)\geq p_1^*/2$. 
    
    We now convert this parameter distance control into stability in infidelity. From \eqref{eq:lf-bargmann-annihilation}, we have that $(\hat{a}-\alpha^*\hat{a}^\dagger-\beta^*)\ket{\psi^*}=0$ where $[\hat{a}-\alpha^*\hat{a}^\dagger-\beta^*,\hat{a}^\dagger-\alpha^*\hat{a}-\beta^*]=(1-(\alpha^*)^2)I$. Then, define the operator:
    \begin{align}
        \hat{a}_{\alpha^*,\beta^*}\equiv \frac{\hat{a}-\alpha^*\hat{a}^\dagger-\beta^*}{\sqrt{1-(\alpha^*)^2}}
    \end{align}
    such that $\hat{a}_{\alpha^*,\beta^*}\ket{\psi^*}=0$ and $[\hat{a}_{\alpha^*,\beta^*},\hat{a}_{\alpha^*,\beta^*}^\dagger]=1$, meaning. Since this operator is just formed by squeezing and displacement, $\hat{a}_{\alpha^*,\beta^*}=U\hat{a}U^\dagger$ for some Gaussian unitary $U$ where $U\ket{0}=\ket{\psi^*}$. Then, since $I-\ketbra{0}\preceq \hat{a}^\dagger \hat{a}$, conjugating by $U$ gives $I-\ketbra{\psi^*}\preceq \hat{a}_{\alpha^*,\beta^*}^\dagger \hat{a}_{\alpha^*,\beta^*} $. Considering the infidelity, 
    \begin{align}
        d_{\rm IF}(\ket{\psi},\ket{\psi^*})&=\bra{\psi}(I-\ketbra{\psi^*})\ket{\psi}\leq \|\hat{a}_{\alpha^*,\beta^*}\ket{\psi}\|_2^2=\frac 4 3 \|(\beta-\beta^*)\ket{\psi}+(\alpha-\alpha^*)\hat{a}^\dagger \ket{\psi}\|_2^2\\
        &\leq \frac 83 |\beta-\beta^*|^2+|\alpha-\alpha^*|^2\|\hat{a}^\dagger \ket{\psi}\|_2^2\\
        &\lesssim (p_1^*-p_1(\ket{\psi}))(1+\|\hat{a}^\dagger \ket{\psi}\|_2^2)
    \end{align}
    where the second inequality uses the fact $\|u+v\|_2^2\leq 2 \|u\|_2^2+2\|v\|_2^2$ and the final bound applies \eqref{eq:p1-param-stable}. Then, since $\hat{a}\hat{a}^\dagger=\hat{a}^\dagger \hat{a}+I$, 
    $$\|\hat{a}^\dagger \ket{\psi}\|_2=\sqrt{\|\hat{a}\ket{\psi}\|_2^2+1}\leq \|\hat{a}\ket{\psi}\|_2+1=\|(\alpha \hat{a}^\dagger +\beta)\ket{\psi}\|_2+1\leq \beta+|\alpha|\|\hat{a}^\dagger \ket{\psi}\|_2+1$$
    meaning $\|\hat{a}^\dagger \ket{\psi}\|_2\leq \frac{1+\beta}{1-|\alpha|}\lesssim 1$ since $\alpha$ is bounded away from $1$ by $\|\theta-\theta^*\|< \xi $. Thus, there exists a constant $C$ for which $d_{\rm IF}(\ket{\psi},\ket{\psi^*})\leq C(p_1^*-p_1(\ket{\psi}))$ when $\|\theta-\theta^*\|< \xi$. 

    Then, to finish the claim, it suffices to show that for $\|\theta-\theta^*\|_2\geq  \xi$, $p_1^*-p_1(\ket{\psi})\geq \gamma$ for some $\gamma > 0$ so that $d_{\rm IF}(\ket{\psi},\ket{\psi^*})\leq C'(p_1^*-p_1(\ket{\psi}))$ for $C'=\max \{C,1/\gamma\}$. Outside the ball, if $p_1<p_1^*/2$, we are already bounded away. Further, from the bounding \eqref{eq:p1-bounding}, it is clear that $\{\theta:p_1(\ket{\psi(\theta)})\geq p_1^*/2\}$ is compact. Then, the intersection of this set with the complement of the ball is also compact, making $\theta^*$ the unique minimizer on this region, which finishes the one-mode case.                                

    We now generalize to arbitrary modes. Consider $d_{\rm IF}(\ket{\psi},\ket{\psi^*(x)})$ for $x=b/\|b\|$. Then, applying a passive unitary, by the unitary invariance of fidelity, 
    \begin{align}\label{eq:p1-multi-passive-infd}
        d_{\rm IF}(\ket{\psi(b,A)},\ket{\psi^*(x)})=d_{\rm IF}(\ket{\psi'(b',A')},\ket{\psi^*(e_1)})
    \end{align}
    where $b'=\|b\|_2 e_1=Wb$ for unitary matrix $W$ and $A'=WAW^\top$. Looking at \eqref{eq:barg-state}, 
    $$\ket{\psi^*(e_1)}=\exp(\beta^* \hat{a}_1^\dagger+ \frac{\alpha^*}{2}(\hat{a}_1^\dagger)^2)\ket{0}^{\otimes n}= \ket{\psi^*}\otimes \ket{0}^{\otimes (n-1)}$$
    where $\ket{\psi^*}$ is the single-mode state in the family that maximizes $p_1(\cdot)$. Then, considering the projection of $\ket{\psi'}$ onto the first mode given by projector $P_{e_1}=I\otimes \ketbra{0}^{\otimes (n-1)}$ which projects onto first-mode states,
    \begin{align}
        P_{e_1}\ket{\psi'}&=\frac{1}{\sqrt{Z(b',A')}}\exp(\|b\|_2\hat{a}_1^\dagger + \frac{A_{11}'}{2}(\hat{a}_1^\dagger)^2)\ket{0}^{\otimes n}=\kappa \ket{\psi(\beta,\alpha)}\otimes \ket{0}^{\otimes  (n-1)}
    \end{align}
    for $\kappa=\frac{Z(\beta,\alpha)}{Z(b',A')}=\|P_{e_1}\ket{\psi'}\|_2^2\leq 1$. Then, 
    \begin{align}
        d_{\rm IF}(\ket{\psi'},\ket{\psi^*(e_1)})&=1-\lvert \bra{\psi^*(e_1)}P_{e_1}\ket{\psi'}\rvert^2=1-\kappa\lvert \braket{\psi^*}{\psi(\beta,\alpha)}\rvert^2=(1-\kappa)+\kappa d_{\rm IF}(\ket{\psi(\beta,\alpha)}, \ket{\psi^*})\\
        &\leq (1-\kappa)+\kappa C'(p_1^*-p_1(\ket{\psi(\beta,\alpha)}))\\
        &\leq \max\{1/p_1^*,C'\}(p_1^*(1-\kappa)+\kappa(p_1^*-p_1(\ket{\psi(\beta,\alpha)})))\\
        &\lesssim p_1^*-\kappa p_1(\ket{\psi(\beta,\alpha)}) \label{eq:p1-multi-final-1}
    \end{align}
    where we use the single-mode stability bound in the second line. Manipulating via \eqref{eq:barg-state}, 
    \begin{align}\label{eq:p1-multi-to-single}
        \kappa p_1(\ket{\psi(\beta,\alpha)})=\kappa \frac{\beta^2}{Z(\beta,\alpha)}=\frac{\beta^2}{Z(b',A')}=\frac{\|b'\|_2^2}{Z(b',A')}=p_1(\ket{\psi'})=p_1(\ket{\psi})
    \end{align}
    Putting \eqref{eq:p1-multi-passive-infd}, \eqref{eq:p1-multi-to-single}, and \eqref{eq:p1-multi-final-1} together, we have that $d_{\rm IF}(\ket{\psi(b,A)},\ket{\psi^*(x)})\lesssim p_1^*-p_1(\ket{\psi(b,A)})$ for the parameter $x=b/\|b\|_2$, from which the claim trivially follows.
<!-- end proof-41 -->
<!-- proof-42: body_md -->
\emph{Subgradients.}
Here we describe how to compute the subgradient of $\lambda[\|P_Wy(\theta)-z\|_2-t]_+$. We choose subgradient $0$ when $\|P_Wy(\theta)-z\|_2\leq t$, and otherwise take $\lambda$ times the gradient of $\|P_W y(\theta)-z \|_2$. The function $\theta\mapsto\|P_Wy(\theta)-z\|_2$ is $1/R_h$-Lipschitz with respect to $\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal P}$, by Theorem~\ref{thm:lf-tensor-reduction}. Hence our choice of penalty subgradient has norm at most $\lambda/R_h$.

Adding $\nabla_{\mathcal P}\ell(\theta)$, computed from \eqref{eq:lf-normalization-derivative}, gives a subgradient $g_z(\theta)$ of $J_z$ satisfying
\[
    \|g_z(\theta)\|_{\mathcal P}\leq G_h+\lambda/R_h.
\]
Clearly, all operations here are polynomial in $n$ and $\dim_\mathbb{C} W$.

\emph{Projection.}
The squared parameter distance is $\|b-b_0\|_2^2+\tfrac12\|A-A_0\|_F^2$, and the domain constraints separate between $b$ and $A$. Thus we project $b_0$ onto its Euclidean ball of radius $2\sqrt h$ and minimize $\|A-A_0\|_F^2$ over symmetric matrices satisfying $\|A\|_F\leq\sqrt h$ and $\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq r_h$.

\emph{First, the matrix projection preserves the Takagi factor.}
Write $A_0=U\operatorname{diag}(s)U^\top$, where $s_i\geq0$. For any feasible matrix $C$, put $D=U^\dagger C\overline U$ and $x_i=\max\{\operatorname{Re}D_{ii},0\}$. The aligned matrix $\widetilde C=U\operatorname{diag}(x)U^\top$ is still feasible, since
\[
    \|\widetilde C\|_F^2=\sum_i x_i^2\leq\|D\|_F^2=\|C\|_F^2,
    \qquad
    \|\widetilde C\|_{\mathrm{op}}=\max_i x_i
    \leq\max_i|D_{ii}|\leq\|D\|_{\mathrm{op}}=\|C\|_{\mathrm{op}}.
\]
Moreover, since $s_i\geq0$, replacing $D_{ii}$ by $x_i$ can only decrease its distance to $s_i$. Unitary invariance of the Frobenius norm therefore gives
\[
    \|A_0-\widetilde C\|_F^2
    =\sum_i(s_i-x_i)^2
    \leq\sum_i|s_i-D_{ii}|^2+\sum_{i\ne j}|D_{ij}|^2
    =\|A_0-C\|_F^2.
\]
Thus an optimal matrix has the same Takagi factor $U$ as $A_0$.

\emph{Second, project the singular-value vector.}
Since the Takagi factors are the same, it remains to solve the ordinary Euclidean projection problem
\[
    s'=\mathop{\rm argmin}_{x\in\mathbb{R}^n}
    \left\{\tfrac12\|x-s\|_2^2:
    \|x\|_2\leq\sqrt h,\ \|x\|_\infty\leq r_h\right\}.
\]
This projection onto the intersection of an $\ell_2$ ball and an $\ell_\infty$ ball can be computed with polynomially many real-arithmetic operations. Since $s\geq0$, its projection also satisfies $s'\geq0$. The desired matrix projection is then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lf-domain-projection}
    A'=U\operatorname{diag}(s')U^\top.
\end{equation}
<!-- end proof-42 -->
